# TCC (thérapie cognitivo-comportementale / cognitive behavioural therapy) — a sourced dossier

This document is a consolidated, fully cited reference on TCC — what it is in 2026, what it does and does not achieve, how it is delivered, and what it costs and looks like for a patient in France. It was compiled on **2026-07-28** from a nine-angle research corpus (since retired, see [Provenance](#12-provenance)) that was itself adversarially re-verified against primary sources on that date. Every substantive claim below carries its inline citation — URL, the specific finding, and the date or version of the source. Nothing is asserted from general knowledge. Where the underlying corpus contradicts itself, the disagreement is stated with both citations rather than silently resolved; where one side is clearly more recent or more authoritative, that is said and why. The single most important section for practical use is **[Superseded, abandoned or contested — do not rely on this](#9-superseded-abandoned-or-contested--do-not-rely-on-this)**: a large amount of what circulates about TCC is stale, and several of the stale items are things a reader would otherwise act on.

> **If you are an agent about to research TCC, read [`handbook.md`](./handbook.md) first**, not this file. It carries the operating rules, the source registry, the citation and fetch traps that caught this corpus, the decay schedule for claims that go stale in months, and the triaged work queue. The skill's own `SKILL.md` (one level up) is the entry point and routes between all layers.

---

## How to read this

1. **Citations are inline and complete.** Each substantive claim gives the source URL, what the source specifically says, and the date or version. Guideline claims give the issuing body's own status line ("Last reviewed: …"), because in this field a document's publication date and its evidence vintage routinely differ by a decade.
2. **`?` prefixes an unverified claim.** It means: this appears in the research corpus but was not confirmed at the primary source, or was read only through a secondary rendering. Terse is not the same as confident — `?` marks the difference.
3. **Year tags on guidelines are evidence-review dates, not text dates.** NICE states this itself: *"Recommendations marked [date 1, amended date 2] had an evidence review in date 1. The amended date means the intent of the recommendation has been changed without an evidence review."* (https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg113/chapter/Update-information, fetched 2026-07-28). A recommendation can be tagged `[2022]` and have been rewritten in October 2025 — this happened to NICE NG222 rec 1.5.3. Never date guideline text from its bracket tag.
4. **Language.** Repo convention is English. French clinical and institutional terms are kept verbatim with a gloss on first use: **TCC** (*thérapie cognitivo-comportementale* = CBT), **HAS** (*Haute Autorité de Santé*, the French national health authority), **RBP** (*recommandation de bonne pratique*, French clinical practice guideline), **ALD** (*affection de longue durée*, long-term-condition scheme), **EDC** (*épisode dépressif caractérisé*, major depressive episode), **"Mon soutien psy"** (the French statutory scheme reimbursing sessions with a contracted psychologist), **AFTCC** (Association Française de Thérapie Comportementale et Cognitive).
5. **Effect-size vocabulary.** *g* = Hedges' g; *SMD* = standardised mean difference; *RoB* = risk of bias; *NNT* = number needed to treat; *RR* = relative risk; *WL* = waitlist; *CAU* = care-as-usual; *NMA* = network meta-analysis; *IPD* = individual participant data.
6. **One reading rule dominates everything below.** A large effect size against a waitlist and a modest absolute response rate are the *same* finding stated two ways. Treat "CBT is highly effective for anxiety (g ≈ 0.9)" and "roughly a third of patients respond" as compatible, not contradictory (Cuijpers et al., *JAMA Psychiatry* 2025;82(6):563–571, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40238104/, online 2025-04-16; Cuijpers et al., *World Psychiatry* 2024;23(2):267–275, https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1002/wps.21203, 2024-05-10).

## 1. What TCC is in 2026

### 1.1 There is no single canonical definition, and the field says so

The main US professional body states it outright: *"There is no single definition of cognitive-behavior therapy. Although some common points of view are shared by most cognitive-behavior therapists, there is wide diversity among those people who call themselves cognitive therapists, behavior therapists, or cognitive-behavior therapists… The definition that follows is meant to give you a general idea… It is not, however, an absolute definition."* (ABCT, "Guidelines for Choosing a Therapist" fact sheet, page dated 2021-03-06, https://www.abct.org/fact-sheets/guidelines-for-choosing-a-therapist/).

ABCT's working definition: *"CBT is the term used for a **group** of psychological treatments"*; *"usually a short-term treatment (i.e., often between 6-20 sessions…)"*; *"What makes CBT unique is that it focuses on the ways that a person's cognitions (i.e., thoughts), emotions, and behaviors are connected and affect one another."* (https://www.abct.org/get-help/what-is-cognitive-behavioral-therapy/, undated, live 2026-07). Its expanded page (dated 2021-05-13, https://www.abct.org/get-help/what-to-expect-from-cognitive-behavioral-therapies/) adds four theoretically load-bearing features: strategies *"derived from learning theory"*; *"a present-focused approach"*; *"CBT models do not assume a single cause for a problem"*; and **collaborative empiricism** — therapist and client *"work together by forming an opinion (i.e., hypothesis) on what is maintaining a specific problem"*. Note the object of the hypothesis is the *maintaining* factor, not the cause.

**The regulatory definition is narrower, and it splits CBT from behavioural activation.** NICE NG222, *Depression in adults: treatment and management*, published 29 June 2022 (https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng222), Tables 1 and 2 describe them as *separate* first-line options: individual CBT *"Focuses on how thoughts, beliefs, attitudes, feelings and behaviour interact"*; individual BA *"Focuses on identifying the link between an individual's activities and their mood… **Does not directly target thoughts and feelings.**"* That a guideline lists a treatment which does not target cognition as an equal first-line option alongside CBT is itself a theoretical statement.

> **Operative 2026 definition.** TCC is a *family* of structured, present-focused, skills-teaching, hypothesis-testing treatments derived from learning theory and cognitive theory, in which the therapist may intervene at cognitive, affective, attentional or behavioural points in a maintaining cycle. Any definition that makes *cognitive restructuring* constitutive of CBT is narrower than what professional bodies and guidelines currently mean.

### 1.2 The "three waves" story is still the working vocabulary — and is defended by nobody

It remains how the literature scopes itself: the 2023 third-wave NMA had to run a dedicated systematic search *"to determine the scope of the term 'third-wave'"* (Heinitz/Schefft et al., *Frontiers in Psychiatry* 2023;14:1189970, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10585267/, published 2023-10-05), finding **fifteen** therapies that had been called third-wave at least once, then **excluding rumination-focused CBT from its own analysis "because of its strong overlap with CBT"**.

But it is not defended as history or taxonomy by anyone currently writing on it:

- **Linden M.**, "Twelve rather than three waves of cognitive behavior therapy allow a personalized treatment", *World Psychiatry* 21(2):316–318, DOI 10.1002/wps.20985, PMID 35524609 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9077599/, online 2022-05-07). *"The expression 'third-wave cognitive behavior therapy (CBT)' has become a trade mark"*; modern CBT encompasses *"at least a dozen 'waves'"* — classical learning theory, operant learning, coping/social learning, self-control, attribution/cognitive theory, emotion theory, therapeutic relationship, disorder-specific manuals, acceptance theory, positive psychology, life-span development, culture-sensitive psychotherapy. **Weight caveat: PMC types this as a three-page *letter*, not a review — it presents no data.**
- **Carona C.**, "The philosophical assumptions across the 'three waves' of cognitive–behavioural therapy: how compatible are they?", *BJPsych Advances* 29(3):213–217, DOI 10.1192/bja.2022.12 (print May 2023). Rejects the incompatibility inference; section heading *"Diversity calls for complementarity"*. ⚠ **Citation trap:** an automated summariser reading only the Cambridge landing metadata produced the *opposite* conclusion ("fundamentally incompatible"). Verify against the PDF.
- **Hayes S.C. & Hofmann S.G.**, "'Third-wave' cognitive and behavioral therapies and the emergence of a process-based approach to intervention in psychiatry", *World Psychiatry* 20(3):363–375, DOI 10.1002/wps.20884 (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1002/wps.20884, 2021-09-09) — the coiners of the label reframe it: these methods matter *"not as protocols for treating syndromes, but as ways of targeting an expanded range of processes of change"*, organised into six dimensions: *"cognition, affect, attention, self, motivation and overt behavior"*.

**Category instability is real:** behavioural activation is classified as "third wave" by the 2023 Frontiers NMA, as a distinct comparator node by Cochrane (Uphoff et al., CD013305, 2020-07-06) and Cuijpers' 2021 NMA, and as plain behavioural therapy by NICE NG222. Any statement of the form "third-wave therapies are/are not better than CBT" is partly a statement about which therapies the author put in the bag.

### 1.3 Beck's model: the current statement is 2014, not 1979

Beck A.T. & Haigh E.A.P., "Advances in Cognitive Theory and Therapy: The Generic Cognitive Model", *Annual Review of Clinical Psychology* 10:1–24, DOI 10.1146/annurev-clinpsy-032813-153734 (March 2014). Abstract, verbatim: *"The generic cognitive model represents a set of common principles that can be applied across the spectrum of psychological disorders… New additions to the theory include **continuity of adaptive and maladaptive function, dual information processing, energizing of schemas, and attentional focus**… **The focus on beliefs differentiates disorders and provides a target for treatment.**"*

Two consequences routinely mis-stated: the 2014 model is explicitly **transdiagnostic** (the depression-specific "cognitive triad" is the 1967/1979 model, i.e. historical), and it asserts **continuity** between adaptive and maladaptive functioning — it is not a latent-disease model.

Carona (2023) also documents that "second-wave CBT = replacing irrational thoughts with rational thoughts" is a **misreading**: *"cognitive therapy is all about genuine guided discovery and not merely about changing thoughts and beliefs"* (citing Beck 1985 principles 4 and 5), and Beck, Emery & Greenberg (1985) already described *"accepting the feelings"* strategies (pp. 232–240) and graded exposure (pp. 258–287).

**Empirical status of the cognition→symptom pathway.** The most recent and most careful test supports it — reciprocally. Smith O.R., Knapstad M. & Aarø L.E., "Dynamics of Cognitions and Symptoms during Treatment: A Prospective Test of Beck's Cognitive Theory of Depression", *Psychotherapy and Psychosomatics*, DOI 10.1159/000551172 (https://doi.org/10.1159/000551172, published 2026-03-09). N = 1,564 Norwegian primary-care adults, PHQ-9 ≥ 10, eight assessments over ~16 weeks: *"lower negative self-beliefs predicted later symptom reductions, and lower symptoms predicted later belief change… Standardized long-run effects were 0.36 (cognition→symptoms; 95% CI 0.13–0.42) and 0.21 (symptoms→cognition; 95% CI 0.12–0.26); **magnitudes did not differ (χ²(1) = 1.77, p = 0.18)**."* Read precisely: this supports a **reciprocal** model, not the primacy of cognition, and it is uncontrolled routine care, not a randomised mechanism test.

### 1.4 Is cognitive change the active ingredient? — no, not as a specific-mechanism claim

Four strands, all pointing the same way:

- **CBT changes dysfunctional cognition — but so does everything else.** Cristea I.A. et al., *Clinical Psychology Review* 42:62–71, DOI 10.1016/j.cpr.2015.08.003, PMID 26319193. 26 studies, 2,002 patients: *"a moderate effect of CBT compared to control groups on dysfunctional thinking at post-test (g = 0.50; 95% CI: 0.38–0.62)… **no significant differences between CBT and other psychotherapies (g = 0.17; p = 0.31)**… no difference between CBT and pharmacotherapy (g = 0.04)."* The authors state both readings explicitly: this *"can be interpreted as confirming the primacy of cognitive change in symptom change, **irrespective of how it is attained**, as well as supporting the idea that **dysfunctional thoughts are simply another symptom**"*. This meta-analysis is a decade old and has not been superseded by a direct update.
- **Mediation is positive in aggregate but null for the specifically Beckian mediators.** Domhardt M. et al., *Behaviour Research and Therapy* 189:104735, DOI 10.1016/j.brat.2025.104735 (2025-04-05), 25 studies / 8,110 participants: significant indirect effects for combined cognitive mediators (ab = −0.068 [−0.093, −0.047], k=14) and combined behavioural mediators (ab = −0.037 [−0.048, −0.028], k=13), *"**but not for the specific cognitive mediators interpretation bias and dysfunctional attitudes**"*.
- **Removing the cognitive component does not reliably reduce outcome; removing behavioural activation does.** Furukawa T.A. et al., "Dismantling, optimising, and personalising internet cognitive behavioural therapy for depression", *Lancet Psychiatry* 8(6):500–511, DOI 10.1016/S2215-0366(21)00077-8 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8838916/). 76 RCTs (48 with IPD, 11,704 participants; 28 aggregate, 6,474): behavioural activation **iMD −1.83 PHQ-9 points (95% CrI −2.90 to −0.80)**; cognitive restructuring **iMD 0.30 (95% CrI −0.87 to 1.41)**, standardised 0.05 (−0.15 to 0.24); relaxation **iMD +1.20 (0.17 to 2.27)**, i.e. worse. Authors: future iCBT packages *"might include behavioural activation but not relaxation… but **cognitive restructuring would probably not be chosen**"*. **Two limits must travel with this:** it is **internet-delivered CBT only** — no comparable component NMA exists for face-to-face CBT — and the authors themselves call the cognitive-restructuring evidence *"relatively imprecise"*.
- **Head-to-head as whole treatments, they tie.** Ciharova M. et al., *J Consult Clin Psychol* 89(6):563–574, DOI 10.1037/ccp0000654, PMID 34264703. 45 studies, 3,382 participants: *"**There was no evidence of a difference in effectiveness between CR, BA, and CBT.**"* Their own rider, which must travel with the result: *"More research is needed to derive conclusions about the performance of CR"* — and CR's CI against care-as-usual (0.08–1.07) is by far the widest of the three, so this is substantially imprecision, not demonstrated equivalence.

> **Defensible 2026 formulation:** cognitive change is a robust correlate and a plausible common pathway of recovery, **not a CBT-specific active ingredient**.

### 1.5 The successor proposals: process-based therapy and network models

**Process-based therapy (PBT) / the Extended Evolutionary Meta-Model (EEMM)** is the most prominent theoretical successor. Founding statements: Hofmann & Hayes, *Clinical Psychological Science* 7(1):37–50, DOI 10.1177/2167702618772296; Hayes, Hofmann & Ciarrochi, *Clinical Psychology Review* 82:101908, DOI 10.1016/j.cpr.2020.101908 — the syndrome-protocol paradigm *"failed to achieve its ultimate goal of conceptual and treatment utility"*, replaced by six psychological dimensions (affect, cognition, attention, self, motivation, overt behaviour) crossed with biophysiological / psychological / sociocultural levels under variation–selection–retention. The authors' own hedge: it offers an alternative *"in a preliminary way"*.

**Its clinical superiority claim is untested.** As of 2026-07-28 there is **no published completed RCT of PBT against CBT**. The evidence stack: an N = 5 multiple-baseline study its own authors call *"a proof of concept"* and which found treatment *"was not universally effective across problem indices"* (Ong C.W. et al., *J Contextual Behav Sci* 35, January 2025, https://contextualscience.org/article/examining_effects_processbased_therapy_multiple_baseline_study); a protocol-only first RCT (Stangier U. et al., *Trials* 25(1):838, DOI 10.1186/s13063-024-08689-3, PMID 39702504, published 2024-12-19, ClinicalTrials.gov **NCT06517589**, N = 80, PBT vs routine CBT — *"The current study will be the first RCT of PBT in a health care setting"*); a feasibility study whose primary outcomes are acceptability, not symptoms (NCT06530888, ACTIVE_NOT_RECRUITING at 2026-07-28); and a newly posted within-therapist trial (NCT07704138 / OSF b3k4v, 30 therapists / 60 patients). The protocol contains a candid limitation that undercuts the idiographic claim: *"data used in PBT to determine if an intervention should be allocated are **still based on nomothetic (i.e., group-based) research data based on diagnostic categories**"*.

The standing critique is unrebutted: **Emmelkamp P.M.G.**, *World Psychiatry* 20(3):381–382, DOI 10.1002/wps.20889 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8429340/): *"about **30–40% of patients cannot be successfully treated** with current CBT protocols, including 'third-wave' CBTs"*, and *"there is **no robust evidence for a superior effectiveness of treatment based on functional analysis compared with manualized evidence-based treatments**"*.

**Network / complex-systems models** are the main rival successor — and the machinery PBT's own trials use (the Stangier RCT selects interventions by matching them to the **central node** of an EMA-derived dynamic network). Their status in 2026 is heavily qualified:

- **Centrality is not a defensible intervention target.** Dablander & Hinne, *Scientific Reports* 9:6846, DOI 10.1038/s41598-019-43033-9; Hallquist, Wright & Molenaar, *Multivariate Behavioral Research* 56(2):199–223, DOI 10.1080/00273171.2019.1640103; Bringmann et al., *J Abnorm Psychol* 128(8):892–903, DOI 10.1037/abn0000446; Neal & Neal, *Psychological Methods* 28(1):179–188, DOI 10.1037/met0000426.
- **The signature prediction failed its first clinical test.** Gillett G. et al., *Schizophrenia* 11:94, DOI 10.1038/s41537-025-00636-8 (https://www.nature.com/articles/s41537-025-00636-8, published 2025-07-03), N = 30 psychosis patients / 15 relapses: *"substantial inter-individual variability in network structure, problematising causal inference from cross-sectional networks… **we do not find strong evidence for an association between network structure and clinical relapse**."*
- **Pairwise associations beat centrality for prediction.** Schumacher L. et al., *World Psychiatry*, DOI 10.1002/wps.21241 (published 2024-09-16), N = 254 chronic depression, 48 weeks: person-specific networks strongly improved outcome prediction, but **pairwise symptom associations outperformed centrality measures**; the authors state the study is exploratory, cross-validation was not possible, and *"the risk of overfitting remains"*.
- **Its conceptual coherence is under formal attack.** Oude Maatman & Eronen, *Journal of Medicine and Philosophy*, DOI 10.1093/jmp/jhaf037 (2025-11-07): the network approach *"appears to unravel unless changes are made to its pillars"*.
- **The field's own 2026 stocktake is cautious.** Schumacher L., Kolar D.R., Klein J.P. & Scholten S., *Cognitive Therapy and Research*, DOI 10.1007/s10608-026-10756-9 (2026-06-23): *"it needs to be continuously evaluated if and how findings from network analyses can be translated into clinical practice"*.

---

## 2. The current toolkit

Two layers define what is actually delivered: a **guideline layer** (which named protocols, at what dose) and a **process layer** (what the therapist is trying to make happen inside a session). Since roughly 2014–2022 the two have diverged.

### 2.1 Exposure — the rationale changed, the procedure did not have to

**The current canonical statement** is Craske M.G., Treanor M., Zbozinek T.D. & Vervliet B., "Optimizing exposure therapy with an inhibitory retrieval approach and the OptEx Nexus", *Behaviour Research and Therapy* 152:104069, May 2022 (online 2022-03-15), DOI 10.1016/j.brat.2022.104069. Verbatim from its Preface: *"A number of years ago, we began to question habituation-based models of exposure therapy — models that firmly placed within-exposure fear reduction as the critical determinant of long-term fear reduction along with the guiding principle of 'stay in the situation until fear subsides.'"*

Strategy tiers as published: **fundamental** — expectancy violation, attention to the feared stimulus, removal of safety signals, mental rehearsal after exposure; **advanced** — deepened extinction, occasional reinforced extinction; **generalization-promoting** — retrieval cues, multiple contexts, stimulus variability, positive affect. The **OptEx Nexus** is an associative map built with the patient identifying the aversive outcome (US), predictors (CSs), occasion setters and safety signals, used to design exposures maximising prediction error.

The operational contrast is clearest in the UCLA trial protocol that implemented both models side by side (NCT04048824, https://cdn.clinicaltrials.gov/large-docs/24/NCT04048824/Prot_SAP_ICF_000.pdf): inhibitory-retrieval condition has **no SUDS-driven stopping rule**; the habituation condition runs *"until initial SUDS has decreased by 50% or 45 minutes has passed"* with hierarchy progression gated on between-session habituation.

**The origin claim.** Craske et al. 2008, *BRAT* 46(1):5–27, DOI 10.1016/j.brat.2007.10.003: *"the evidence, reviewed herein, indicates that **neither the degree by which fear reduces nor the ending fear level predict therapeutic outcome**."*

**But the superiority test failed.** Treanor M., Zbozinek T.D., Rosenberg B.M., Sewart A., Sandman C.F., Ruiz J. & Craske M.G., *J Consult Clin Psychol* 2025;93(10):705–717, DOI 10.1037/ccp0000970, PMID 41129370 (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41129370/). Assessor-blinded parallel two-arm superiority RCT, N = 89 (77 social anxiety, 12 panic), 9 weekly sessions, inhibitory-retrieval vs habituation-focused exposure, **from Craske's own lab**: *"No significant group differences emerged on principal outcomes — interviewer-rated distress and impairment, nor significant pairwise differences on symptom scores at posttreatment or follow-up (ps > .11)."* Secondary: steeper self-reported anxiety decline and clinically significant change on self-report 43% vs 13%, but *"there were no group differences on interviewer-rated indices of clinically significant change (ps > .42)"*. Conclusion: *"Both methods of exposure therapy were effective… Habituation-focused exposure therapy was not superior on any measure."*

Corroborated by three further nulls: Nadel M.H. et al., *J Clin Psychol* 2025;81(12):1231–1243, DOI 10.1002/jclp.70032, PMID 40811725 — interrupted time-series across **1,968** OCD/anxiety patients in a programme that switched models, *"not significantly different"*; Kooistra M.J. et al., *Eur J Psychotraumatol* 16(1):2447183, DOI 10.1080/20008066.2024.2447183 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11721951/, 2025-01-07), N = 60 PTSD, *"no between-condition differences"* — with the authors' own hedge that a one-session/one-week design may miss a longer-horizon effect; and Kühne F. et al., *Frontiers in Psychology* 15:1328850, DOI 10.3389/fpsyg.2024.1328850 (2024-05-13), whose theoretically important finding is *"**IL-focused therapy was hardly distinguishable from CBT as implemented in practice**"*.

One nuance survives at process level: Thaon de Saint André S. et al., *BRAT*, DOI 10.1016/j.brat.2025.104856 (2025-09-24), N = 516 anxiety patients, ≥10 exposure exercises each: *"expectancy measures and fear reduction were only weakly correlated within exposure exercises… **both readouts independently predicted treatment success**"*; conclusion — *"addressing **both** rationales may help optimize and personalize exposure therapy."*

> **Bottom line.** The inhibitory-retrieval model won the theoretical argument; it has not won the clinical one. The honest 2026 statement is: *the habituation rationale is a bad explanation, but exposure conducted under it works about as well.* SUDS-based stopping rules and strictly ascending hierarchies are no longer the default instruction, but a clinician still running prolonged exposure to fear reduction is not delivering a disproven treatment.

### 2.2 What inside a CBT package carries the effect

**Panic — the only component NMA.** Pompoli A., Furukawa T.A., Efthimiou O., Imai H., Tajika A. & Salanti G., *Psychological Medicine* 2018;48(12):1945–1953, DOI 10.1017/S0033291717003919 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6137372/). 72 RCTs, 4,064 participants, 12 pre-defined components. **Interoceptive exposure** and **face-to-face setting** associated with better efficacy and acceptability; **muscle relaxation** and **virtual-reality exposure** with *"significantly lower efficacy"*. Most vs least efficacious constructed package: OR for remission **7.69 (95% CrI 1.75–33.33)**.

Three caveats from the paper's own Results/Discussion, without which this is over-read: **only muscle relaxation reached significance** on remission (all other component estimates carry very wide credible intervals); the 7.69 OR contrasts two *constructed* extremes, not two real trial arms; and against a *plausibly typical* package the advantage falls to **OR 2.33 (CrI 1.01–5.26)** — barely excluding 1.

**Insomnia — the largest component NMA in the field.** Furukawa Y., Sakata M., Yamamoto R. et al., *JAMA Psychiatry* 2024;81(4):357–365, DOI 10.1001/jamapsychiatry.2023.5060, PMID 38231522 (online 2024-01-17). **241 trials, 31,452 participants**, searched to 2023-07-21. Incremental odds ratios for remission: cognitive restructuring **1.68 (1.28–2.20)**; third-wave components **1.49 (1.10–2.03)**; sleep restriction **1.49 (1.04–2.13)**; stimulus control **1.43 (1.00–2.05)**; sleep hygiene education **1.01 (0.77–1.32)** — not essential; relaxation **0.81 (0.64–1.02)** — *"potentially counterproductive"*; in-person therapist-led delivery **1.83 (1.19–2.81)**. Best combination vs in-person psychoeducation: risk difference 0.33 (0.23–0.43), **NNT 3.0**. Caveats: remission is imputed from continuous scores; 75% of trials are comorbid insomnia; 21% of participants were on hypnotics, which plausibly confounds the relaxation finding.

**Depression component studies are underpowered, not negative.** Cuijpers P., Cristea I.A., Karyotaki E., Reijnders M. & Hollon S.D., *Psychotherapy Research* 2019;29(1):15–29, DOI 10.1080/10503307.2017.1395922. 16 studies, 22 comparisons, 15 components; pooled full-vs-minus-one-component **g = 0.21 (95% CI 0.03–0.39)**. Verbatim: *"The currently available component studies do not have the statistical power nor the quality to draw any meaningful conclusion about key ingredients of psychotherapies for adult depression."* `?` The full text returned HTTP 403; the figures and quotation are consistent across secondary reports but were not read at source.

### 2.3 Cognitive restructuring, behavioural experiments and ordering

- **Behavioural experiments vs plain exposure.** McMillan D. & Lee R., *Clinical Psychology Review* 2010;30(5):467–478 (CRD/DARE record https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK79820/). 14 studies, N = 644: *"some evidence that behavioural experiments were more effective than exposure alone, but methodological limitations of the included studies prevented definitive conclusions."* Practice implication the authors offer: exposure is maximally effective **when set up to challenge a specific cognition**.
- **Behavioural experiments vs thought records.** McManus F., Van Doorn K. & Yiend J., *J Behav Ther Exp Psychiatry* 2012;43(1):540–547. N = 91, single-session, non-clinical: both beat control; **small advantage for behavioural experiments** — target belief changed earlier and change generalised.
- **Order does not matter, contra inhibitory-learning theory.** Buchholz J.L., Blakey S.M., Hellberg S.N. et al., *J Behav Cogn Ther* 2022;32(1):13–24, DOI 10.1016/j.jbct.2021.12.004 (published on behalf of the **AFTCC**). N = 45 spider phobia randomised to CR-before-exposure, exposure-before-CR, or stress-management control: both active arms beat control, **no significant difference between CR-EXP and EXP-CR**; *"therapists may not need to be concerned with the order of treatment components when delivering CBT for specific phobia."*
- **Where cognitive therapy clearly beats a behaviour-only package.** Clark D.M., Ehlers A., Hackmann A., McManus F., Fennell M., Grey N., Waddington L. & Wild J., *J Consult Clin Psychol* 2006;74(3):568–578. N = 62 social phobia: no longer meeting criteria post-treatment — **cognitive therapy 84%, exposure + applied relaxation 42%, waitlist 0%**; difference persisted at 1 year. This trial is why UK guidance names the Clark & Wells protocol specifically rather than "CBT" generically.

### 2.4 Behavioural activation

- **Non-inferior to full CBT, and deliverable by less-trained staff.** Richards D.A. et al., **COBRA**, *The Lancet* 2016;388(10047):871–880, ISRCTN27473954 (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27461440/). N = 440 adults with MDD; BA by **junior mental health workers with no specific psychotherapy training** vs CBT from qualified therapists; PHQ-9 at 12 months, non-inferiority margin 1.9 points. Result: CBT 8.4 vs BA 8.4, mean difference 0.1 (−1.3 to 1.5), p = 0.89; per-protocol 0.0 (−1.5 to 1.6) → **BA non-inferior**, and cheaper. Full NIHR HTA report: *Health Technology Assessment* 2017;21(46), https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK448297/.
- **Cochrane's tightest equivalence estimate anywhere in this dossier.** Uphoff E. et al., CD013305, published 2020-07-06, search 2020-01-17 (https://www.cochrane.org/evidence/CD013305_behavioural-activation-therapy-depression-adults). 53 studies, 5,495 participants: **BA vs CBT RR 0.99 (0.92–1.07), 5 RCTs, 601 patients, moderate certainty**. BA vs TAU RR 1.40 (1.10–1.78), moderate certainty — but the difference disappeared in worst-case and ITT sensitivity analyses. `?` The BA-vs-medication comparison was previously reported as moderate certainty; Cochrane's own plain-language summary says *"We were uncertain whether behavioural activation worked better than medication"* — treat as low certainty from a single trial. The review also concludes BA *"is probably less acceptable to people than usual care"*.

### 2.5 Doses, session counts and stepped vs matched care

**England has the most explicit dose specification anywhere.**

- NICE NG222 (depression) replaces stepped care with **matched care**, rec 1.4.2: *"Match the choice of treatment to meet the needs and preferences of the person with depression. Use the least intrusive and most resource efficient treatment that is appropriate for their clinical needs, or one that has worked for them in the past."* Review point at 2–4 weeks (rec 1.4.3). Doses from the NICE-authored BMJ guideline summary (Kendrick T., Pilling S., Mavranezouli I. et al., *BMJ* 2022, https://discovery.ucl.ac.uk/id/eprint/10152602/3/Mavranezouli_BMJ%20NICE%20Depression%20summary%20accepted.pdf): less severe — guided self-help usually 6–8 sessions, group BA 2 practitioners / ~8 participants / usually 8 sessions, group CBT 8, individual CBT 8, individual BA 8; more severe — individual CBT usually 16, individual BA 12–16, individual problem-solving 6–12, counselling 12–16, STPP 16, behavioural couples therapy 15–20 over 5–6 months. All with the qualifier *"although additional sessions may be needed for people with comorbid mental or physical health problems or complex social needs, or to address residual symptoms."*
- NICE CG159 (social anxiety, published 1 May 2013, https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg159): *"Individual CBT (the Clark and Wells model)… should consist of up to 14 sessions of 90 minutes' duration over approximately 4 months"*; Heimberg model *"15 sessions of 60 minutes' duration, and one session of 90 minutes for exposure, over approximately 4 months"*. And rec 1.3.3: *"**Do not routinely offer group CBT in preference to individual CBT.**"*
- NICE NG116 (PTSD, published 5 December 2018): individual trauma-focused CBT *"typically be provided over **8 to 12 sessions**, but more if clinically indicated"* (rec 1.6.17); EMDR the same 8–12 (rec 1.6.20); supported trauma-focused computerised CBT 8–10 sessions (rec 1.6.22); children/young people individual TF-CBT 6–12 sessions.
- NICE CG31 (OCD, November 2005): dose framed in **therapist hours** — low-intensity ERP up to **10 therapist hours per patient**; more intensive CBT including ERP at **more than 10 therapist hours**. Overview carries *"Last reviewed: 11 July 2024"* **and "Update in progress"** (https://www.nice.org.uk/Guidance/CG31); the replacement is **GID-NG10435, expected 16 February 2027**, final scope 14 November 2025, batch 1 (covering ERP and CBT-with-ERP) to be consulted on autumn 2026 (https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/indevelopment/gid-ng10435).
- NICE CG178 (psychosis, February 2014): *"CBT should be delivered on a **one-to-one basis over at least 16 planned sessions**"* (rec 1.3.7.1 [2009]).
- **⚠ NICE CG113's panic dose figures are all tagged `[2004]`.** 1.3.15: *"**CBT in the optimal range of duration (7 to 14 hours in total) should be offered.**"* **[2004]**; 1.3.16 weekly 1–2 hour sessions completed within 4 months **[2004]**; 1.3.18 briefer CBT *"around 7 hours"* **[2004]** (https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg113/chapter/Recommendations, tag-by-tag verified 2026-07-28). NICE says so itself in plain English in its patient version: *"The advice on the treatment of GAD has been updated. **The advice on the treatment of panic disorder has not been updated.**"* (https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg113/resources/treating-generalised-anxiety-disorder-and-panic-disorder-in-adults-pdf-176693878213). **Never cite "7 to 14 hours" as NICE's 2024-reviewed position.** No anxiety-guideline update is scoped — verified three ways on 2026-07-28 (the in-development register returns "No results found" for anxiety; the 25-item clinical-guidelines list has no anxiety topic; the *Mental health guidelines* hub GID-HUB10005 shows CG113's "Updates planned and in progress" column **empty**). Full working: the deleted `raw/gap-4.md` — `git show 15e599a:reference/health/tcc/raw/gap-4.md` in the `me` repository (see [§12](#12-provenance)).
- **GAD, by contrast, really is `[2011]`:** CBT *"usually consist of 12 to 15 weekly sessions… each lasting 1 hour"* (1.2.19), and applied relaxation given **word-for-word the same dose** (1.2.20).

**Empirical dose–response inside the English system.** Robinson L., **Kellett S., Delgadillo J.**, "Dose-response patterns in low and high intensity cognitive behavioral therapy for common mental health problems", *Depression and Anxiety* 2020;37(3):285–294, DOI 10.1002/da.22999. **N = 102,206 patients across 16 services.** Low-intensity CBT: **95% of those attaining reliable and clinically significant improvement did so within 7 sessions**; high-intensity CBT: **95% within 14 sessions**. Social anxiety, PTSD and OCD needed longer high-intensity courses (6–16 sessions).

> **The denominator caveat — this is the single most-misread statistic in the field.** Those figures are conditional on *being a responder at all*. In this dataset only **25.3%–26.8% of low-intensity cases** and **38.6%–39.3% of high-intensity cases** met the improvement criterion, and response rates for social anxiety, OCD, somatoform disorder and PTSD under low-intensity CBT were **below 25%**. "95% of responders improve within 7 sessions" is **not** "7 sessions is enough for most patients", and cannot on its own carry an argument that longer guideline doses are excessive.

**Frequency and spacing.**
- **Twice weekly beats once weekly in depression.** Bruijniks S.J.E. et al., *Br J Psychiatry* 2020;216(4):222–230, DOI 10.1192/bjp.2019.265. N = 200, 2×2 factorial (CBT or IPT × once/twice weekly): BDI-II difference at month 6 = 3.85 points, **d = 0.55**; attrition 16 vs 32; hazard ratio for response 1.48 (1.00–2.18); no difference between CBT and IPT. Note the authors' point that Beck's original manual prescribed twice-weekly starts — weekly delivery was drift, not doctrine.
- **Massed prolonged exposure is non-inferior to spaced (PTSD).** Foa E.B. et al., STRONG STAR, *JAMA* 2018;319(4):354–364, DOI 10.1001/jama.2017.21242. N = 370 active-duty military: 10 sessions over 2 weeks non-inferior to 10 over 8 weeks at 2 weeks (difference 0.79 PSS-I, 1-sided 95% CI −∞ to 2.29, p = .049) and at 12 weeks (0.55, −∞ to 2.05, p = .03). ⚠ *JAMA* published a correction on 2018-08-21 for an error in the spaced-PE dropout number — treat any dropout figure from the original print version with suspicion.
- **Intensified exposure is equivalent but faster.** Pittig A., Heinig I., Goerigk S. et al., *Depression and Anxiety* 2021;38(11):1169–1181, DOI 10.1002/da.23204. N = 726, prediction-error-based exposure, 3 sessions/week vs weekly: formally **equivalent** symptom reduction, but **time to response 32% shorter** (median 68 vs 108 days), lower dropout, better quality of life at follow-up without increased relapse.
- **Single-session exposure for specific phobia.** Odgers K., Kershaw K.A., Li S.H. & Graham B.M., *BRAT* 2022;159:104203, DOI 10.1016/j.brat.2022.104203. k = 55 single-session / 30 multi-session, n = 1,758 clinical participants: **no significant difference** at post-treatment or follow-up, with **total treatment time 45% lower** for single-session (One-Session Treatment, typically up to 3 h).

### 2.6 Relapse prevention

- **Effect size.** Breedvelt J.J.F. et al., *Nature Mental Health* 2024;2(2):154–163, DOI 10.1038/s44220-023-00178-x. IPD meta-analysis, 14 RCTs, N = 1,720: **HR 0.60 (95% CI 0.48–0.74)** for relapse over 12 months. **Number of previous episodes moderated the effect** — ≥3 prior episodes HR 0.55 (0.37–0.79). No difference between preventive cognitive therapy, MBCT, CBT and continuation cognitive therapy.
- **MBCT specifically.** Kuyken W. et al., *JAMA Psychiatry* 2016;73(6):565–574, DOI 10.1001/jamapsychiatry.2016.0076. IPD, 9 trials, 1,258 patients: **HR 0.69 (0.58–0.82)** over 60 weeks vs no MBCT; **HR 0.79 (0.64–0.97)** vs active treatments including antidepressants. On severity as a moderator the authors are deliberately weak — *"there was **some evidence to suggest** that a greater severity of depressive symptoms prior to treatment was associated with a larger effect"* — an exploratory signal, not an established moderator.
- **A psychological module is a viable substitute for indefinite medication.** Breedvelt J.J.F., Warren F.C., Segal Z., Kuyken W. & Bockting C.L., *JAMA Psychiatry* 2021;78(8):868–875. IPD, 4 RCTs, 714 participants: **HR 0.86 (0.60–1.23) — no difference** over 15 months between continuing antidepressants and a sequential psychological intervention.
- **Partial remitters benefit too.** Gülpen J. et al., *Psychological Medicine* 2025;55:e50, DOI 10.1017/S0033291725000157 (2025-02-17). 16 RCTs, n = 705: **HR 0.60 (0.43–0.84)** over 12 months; symptom reduction g = 0.29 (0.04–0.54); **no effect on quality of life**; trim-and-fill attenuated the relapse effect to HR 0.72 (0.50–1.04). Equivalent doses named: *"8 weekly sessions MBCT or PCT; 6–10 sessions CBT; 4 weeks MemFlex; or 10 sessions C-CT over 8 months."*
- **NICE's own recommendation.** NG222 rec 1.8.5 [2022]: for people remitted on antidepressants but at higher relapse risk, consider continuing medication, **or "a course of psychological therapy (group CBT or mindfulness-based cognitive therapy [MBCT]) for people who do not wish to continue on antidepressants"**, or both; rec 1.8.6: *"usually consists of 8 sessions over 2 to 3 months with the option of additional sessions in the next 12 months."*

### 2.7 Transdiagnostic and unified protocols

The adult **Unified Protocol** is at its **second edition** (Barlow D.H. et al., Oxford University Press, ISBN 9780190685973) — **still current as of 2026-07-28, no third edition exists**. `?` The publication date is inconsistent across channels (OUP's page underpins "25 January 2018"; retail metadata for the same ISBN gives 15 December 2017) — cite as "2nd ed., 2017/2018". Eight modules (five core), expanded guidance for depression, eating disorders, non-suicidal self-injury, substance use and anger, new chapters on functional assessment and group delivery.

- **Anchor trial.** Barlow D.H., Farchione T.J., Bullis J.R. et al., *JAMA Psychiatry* 2017;74(9):875–884, DOI 10.1001/jamapsychiatry.2017.2164. Randomised **equivalence** trial, N = 223, up to 16 sessions over 16–21 weeks in both active arms, pre-specified margin ±0.75 ADIS clinical severity units. UP vs single-disorder protocols: post-treatment **d = 0.15**, 6-month **d = 0.10** — both CIs entirely inside the equivalence zone. Completion **87.5% (77/88) UP vs 69.2% (63/91) SDP; OR 3.11 (1.44–6.74)** favouring UP.
- **Transdiagnostic CBT is not better than disorder-specific CBT.** Schaeuffele C. et al., *Nature Human Behaviour*, DOI 10.1038/s41562-023-01787-3 (2024-01-16), PROSPERO CRD42019141512. 53 studies, 6,705 participants: TD-CBT vs controls depression **g = 0.74 (0.57–0.92)**, anxiety **g = 0.77 (0.56–0.97)**; **vs disorder-specific CBT depression g = 0.09 (−0.07 to 0.25, p = 0.269), anxiety g = 0.09 (−0.01 to 0.20, p = 0.091) — no difference**; superior to controls at 3, 6 and 12 months **but not at 24 months**; individual, group and internet formats comparable.
- **And it has a dropout problem.** *"Transdiagnostic psychological interventions for emotional disorders: A comprehensive meta-analysis"*, *Journal of Affective Disorders* 2025;388:119537, DOI 10.1016/j.jad.2025.119537. Metapsy database to 2024-01-01; **94 RCTs, 108 comparisons, 12,443 patients**. Pooled **g = 0.59 (0.50–0.68)**, I² = 78.9%, prediction interval **−0.18 to 1.37**; anxiety in anxiety-only samples g = 0.87. **"A significantly higher risk of study dropout was found in the intervention conditions compared to the control groups"** — RR 1.18 (1.04–1.34), p = .010. ⚠ **Authorship conflict:** the corpus attributes this same paper (same journal, volume, page, DOI, 94 trials / 108 comparisons / 12,443 patients / g = 0.59 / I² 78.9) to **Jiménez-Orenga N. et al.** in one file and **Díaz-García A. et al.** in another. Unresolved — see [§10](#10-where-this-dossiers-own-sources-disagree).

### 2.8 Modular vs manualised

**Guidelines mandate manuals.** NICE NG222 rec 1.4.5: *"Use psychological and psychosocial treatment manuals to guide the form, duration and ending of interventions."*; CG159: *"Psychological interventions should be based on the relevant treatment manual(s)"*; CG113 rec 1.2.19: CBT for GAD *"should be based on the treatment manuals used in the clinical trials of CBT for GAD."*

**Modularity is the recognised alternative in youth services** — MATCH-ADTC, whose 2-year evidence is Chorpita B.F., Weisz J.R., Daleiden E.L. et al., *J Consult Clin Psychol* 2013;81(6):999–1009, DOI 10.1037/a0034200. **But it did not replicate outside the US:** the Norwegian MATCH RCT (*BMC Psychology* 2024;12:569, DOI 10.1186/s40359-024-02082-0, published 2024-10-18, N = 121 across seven CAMHS clinics) states *"Results did not provide evidence that MATCH reduces symptoms of these problems compared to TAU… The findings were inconclusive."*

---

## 3. What works for what, and how well

### 3.0 The two cross-disorder anchors — read these first

Everything downstream hangs off two syntheses from the same group (Cuijpers / Metapsy / Furukawa), because they are the only sources applying **identical** search, extraction, risk-of-bias and pooling methods across disorders.

**Cuijpers et al., *JAMA Psychiatry* 2025;82(6):563–571**, DOI 10.1001/jamapsychiatry.2025.0482, PMID 40238104, online 2025-04-16 (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40238104/). 375 trials, 423 CBT-vs-control comparisons, 32,968 patients, searches to 2024-01-01. Note the authors' own hedging: *"CBT was **probably** effective… and **possibly** effective in psychotic and bipolar disorders. However, the effect sizes depended on the type of control condition."*

| Disorder | g (95% CI) | k | Patients |
|---|---|---|---|
| Bipolar — depression | 0.31 (0.15–0.47) | 15 | 1,268 |
| Bipolar — mania | 0.32 (0.08–0.56) | 15 | 1,268 |
| Psychotic disorder | 0.43 (0.22–0.63) | 16 | 1,715 |
| Major depression | 0.84 (0.71–0.97) | 123 | 14,081 |
| Panic disorder | 0.86 (0.70–1.02) | 39 | 2,441 |
| Social anxiety disorder | 0.87 (0.71–1.03) | 45 | 3,329 |
| Binge eating disorder | 0.89 (0.67–1.10) | 22 | 1,764 |
| Generalised anxiety disorder | 0.93 (0.71–1.15) | 39 | 3,311 |
| Bulimia nervosa | 0.97 (0.61–1.33) | 11 | 614 |
| Obsessive-compulsive disorder | 0.98 (0.81–1.16) | 19 | 1,118 |
| Specific phobia | 1.17 (0.58–1.76) | 8 | 454 |
| PTSD | 1.27 (1.03–1.52) | 38 | 2,873 |

Four facts from that paper constrain every claim below:
1. **The effect size is largely a property of the control group.** *"Large effect sizes (g > 0.94) were observed in waitlist-controlled trials… Trials using care as usual showed more modest effect sizes (0.22–1.13)."*
2. **Waitlist dominates the anxiety/eating/PTSD/OCD literatures** — >60% of trials in each (panic 71.8%, social anxiety 84.4%, GAD 76.9%, phobia 62.5%, PTSD 73.7%, OCD 73.7%, bulimia 90.9%, BED 81.8%), against 38.2% in MDD, 12.5% in psychosis, 6.7% in bipolar. Pill placebo was used in **18 of 375 trials (≈5%)**.
3. **Trial quality is poor and not disorder-uniform.** *"Only 10% of studies had an overall low RoB (range: 0% for BIP and OCD to 21% for PTSD)."*
4. **Acceptability differs sharply.** *"Study dropout rates within the CBT conditions ranged from 8% for specific phobia to 24% for PTSD."*

Anorexia nervosa was **excluded for having fewer than 5 trials**. Exposure-only treatments, mindfulness-based CBT, supportive-counselling controls, inpatients and self-report-only samples were excluded by design — so the "specific phobia" estimate is *not* the exposure-therapy literature.

**Cuijpers et al., *World Psychiatry* 2024;23(2):267–275**, DOI 10.1002/wps.21203, 2024-05-10 (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1002/wps.21203). 441 trials, 33,881 patients, searches to 2023-01-01. Response = ≥50% symptom reduction, intention-to-treat. Pooled **absolute** response rate *within the psychotherapy arm*: MDD **0.42** (0.39–0.45); PTSD **0.38** (0.33–0.43); OCD **0.38** (0.30–0.47); panic **0.38** (0.33–0.43); GAD **0.36** (0.30–0.42); social anxiety **0.32** (0.29–0.37); specific phobia **0.32** (0.23–0.42); borderline personality disorder **0.24** (0.15–0.36). Conclusion: *"most psychotherapies… are effective compared with control conditions, **but absolute response rates are modest**."* The relative risks were significant for all disorders **except BPD**.

### 3.1 Major depression

**Guideline status.** NICE **NG222**, published 2022-06-29, **last reviewed 30 January 2026** — *"We reviewed this guideline and will not update the recommendations"* (https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng222). Severity is binary: **less severe = PHQ-9 < 16**, **more severe = PHQ-9 ≥ 16**. Table 1 (less severe), in the committee's own order of clinical and cost effectiveness: **guided self-help → group CBT → group behavioural activation → individual CBT → individual BA → group exercise → group mindfulness and meditation → IPT → SSRIs → counselling → STPP**. Table 2 (more severe): **combined individual CBT + antidepressant → individual CBT → individual BA → antidepressant → individual problem-solving → counselling → STPP → IPT → guided self-help → group exercise**. Rec 1.6.1 [2022]: *"take into account that all treatments in table 2 can be used as first-line treatments"* — NICE does **not** mandate CBT first; it ranks it first on cost-effectiveness and leaves the choice to shared decision-making. Note also that the ordering encodes "implementation factors": guided self-help is first for being *least intrusive*, not most effective.

**HAS (France) does not give TCC privileged status.** RBP *Épisode dépressif caractérisé de l'adulte : prise en charge en premier recours*, validated 4 October 2017, online 8 November 2017, **still current at 2026-07-28** (https://www.has-sante.fr/jcms/c_1739917/fr/episode-depressif-caracterise-de-l-adulte-prise-en-charge-en-premier-recours). Verbatim: the proven psychotherapies are *"la psychothérapie de soutien ; les thérapies cognitivo-comportementales (TCC) ; les psychothérapies psychodynamiques ou d'inspiration analytique ; les thérapies systémiques"*, and **"En l'absence de données comparatives suffisantes, ces psychothérapies peuvent toutes être mises en œuvre. Les thérapies cognitivo-comportementales (TCC) rassemblent les données les plus nombreuses (études randomisées contrôlées)."** For *EDC léger* the first-line is **psychothérapie de soutien**, and HAS recommends **not** prescribing an antidepressant for mild EDC or subsyndromal symptoms. Response assessed at **4–8 weeks**. This is the single most important France/UK divergence in the dossier.

**Best current meta-analysis.** Cuijpers P. et al., *World Psychiatry* 2023;22(1):105–115, DOI 10.1002/wps.21069, PMID 36640411 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9840507/) — 409 trials, 518 comparisons, **52,702 patients**, *"the largest meta-analysis ever of a specific type of psychotherapy for a mental disorder"*:

| Comparison | g (95% CI) | I² | NNT | k |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CBT vs control, post-test | **0.79 (0.70–0.89)** | 85% | 3.8 | 271 |
| …low-RoB trials only | **0.60 (0.49–0.71)** | 77% | 5.2 | 90 |
| …corrected for publication bias | **0.47 (0.35–0.59)** | 90% | 7.0 | — |
| 6–9 month follow-up | 0.74 (0.36–1.11) | 91% | 4.1 | 78 |
| 10–12 month follow-up | 0.49 (0.01–0.98) | 91% | 6.5 | 22 |
| **13–24 month follow-up** | **0.22 (−0.12 to 0.56)** — n.s. | 86% | 16.2 | 8 |

Response CBT 0.42 vs control 0.19; RR 2.13 (1.96–2.32), NNT 4.7. Remission 0.36 vs 0.15; RR 2.45 (2.06–2.92), NNT 3.6. **vs other psychotherapies g = 0.06 (0.00–0.12)**, and **0.02 (−0.05 to 0.09), I² = 0%** in low-RoB trials. **vs pharmacotherapy** short-term g = 0.08 (−0.07 to 0.24) — no difference — but at 6–12 months CBT pulls ahead, **g = 0.34 (0.09–0.58)**. Combined CBT+pharmacotherapy vs pharmacotherapy alone 0.51 (0.19–0.84), NNT 6.3; combined vs CBT alone 0.19 (−0.11 to 0.50), n.s. **Unguided self-help CBT** 0.45 (0.31–0.60) → publication-bias corrected **0.25 (0.07–0.43)**. Trial quality: only 131/409 (32.0%) low RoB; **assessor masking in only 24.7%**.

**NICE's own NMA.** Mavranezouli I. et al., *eClinicalMedicine* 2024;75:102780, DOI 10.1016/j.eclinm.2024.102780, PMID 39246718 (2024-08-16), 676 RCTs, 105,477 participants, 63 treatment classes. Less severe vs TAU: **group cognitive/CBT SMD −1.01 (95% CrI −1.76 to −0.06)**, and *"in the 2023 update, **group yoga and self-help without support emerged as efficacious for less severe depression**"*; interpretation — *"Group CT/CBT (and possibly group yoga and self-help) appears efficacious in less severe depression, whereas antidepressants do not show evidence of effect."* More severe vs **pill placebo**: combined individual CT/CBT + antidepressant −1.18 (−2.07 to −0.44); individual behavioural therapies −0.86 (−1.65 to −0.16); combined light therapy + antidepressants −0.86; combined acupuncture + antidepressants −0.78; individual CT/CBT −0.78 (−1.42 to −0.33); mirtazapine −0.35; SNRIs −0.32; TCAs −0.29; SSRIs −0.24. **Individual CBT is therefore not uniquely placed among the efficacious classes.** Quality "low and low-to-moderate"; the new severity categorisation *"has not been validated"*.

### 3.2 Generalised anxiety disorder

**Guideline:** NICE CG113, published 2011-01-26, *"Last reviewed: 7 May 2024"* (presentational only), minor change April 2026 (NG215 link). Step 3: *"If a person with GAD chooses a high-intensity psychological intervention, offer **either CBT or applied relaxation**"* (rec 1.2.18 [2011]), 12–15 weekly 1-hour sessions for both. **No HAS RBP on GAD exists.**

**Best NMA.** Papola D., Miguel C., Mazzaglia M. et al., *JAMA Psychiatry* 2024;81(3):250–259, DOI 10.1001/jamapsychiatry.2023.3971, online 2023-10-18 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10585589/). ⚠ **This article was corrected on 24 January 2024** *"to fix errors in the Abstract, Key Points, text, References, Table 2, Figures 1-3, and eAppendixes D-N"* — use post-correction figures only (**65 studies, 5,048 participants**).

| vs treatment-as-usual | SMD (95% CI) | CINeMA certainty |
|---|---|---|
| Third-wave CBT | −0.76 (−1.15 to −0.36) | Moderate |
| **CBT** | **−0.74 (−1.09 to −0.38)** | Moderate |
| Relaxation therapy | −0.59 (−1.07 to −0.11) | Low |
| Waiting list (as a "treatment") | +0.28 (−0.12 to 0.68) | Low |

**No significant differences between psychotherapies head-to-head.** At 3–12 month follow-up **only CBT retained superiority over TAU: SMD −0.60 (−0.99 to −0.21)**. Excluding the 23 high-RoB studies, **relaxation lost significance (−0.47, −1.18 to 0.23)** while CBT and third-wave CBT survived. Actual verbatim conclusion — *"**CBT may represent the first-line therapy of GAD**. Third-wave CBTs and relaxation therapy were associated with short-term effectiveness and **may also be offered**."* (An earlier corpus draft quoted a stronger, non-existent sentence; corrected.) Stated limitations: one-third at high RoB; **two-thirds used per-protocol rather than ITT analysis**; excluding per-protocol studies removed two-thirds of the base and *"the network analysis became underpowered"*; **no comparison reached high certainty**.

**There is essentially no CBT-vs-drug evidence base for GAD** — only 2 pill-placebo-controlled CBT trials in the whole Metapsy dataset. Treat any claim of CBT superiority over SSRIs in GAD as unsupported.

### 3.3 Panic disorder

**Best NMA.** Papola D. et al., *Br J Psychiatry* 2022;221(3):507–519, DOI 10.1192/bjp.2021.148 (online 2021-10-06, open access), **136 RCTs**, 7,352 participants, searched to 2021-01-01: **CBT vs TAU SMD −0.67 (95% CI −0.95 to −0.39), CINeMA moderate**; short-term psychodynamic therapy −0.61 (−1.15 to −0.07), low. *"**After removing RCTs at high risk of bias only CBT remained more efficacious than TAU**"*, and *"Studies with high risk of bias tend to inflate the overall efficacy of treatments."* The NMA **sets no limits on duration or number of sessions** and treats session count only as a transitivity/meta-regression covariate — so it validates the *type* recommendation and is silent on the 7–14 hour dose. Cross-check: Cuijpers 2025 panic g = 0.86, 71.8% waitlist controls, **only 6.2% of panic trials low-RoB — the worst of the eight disorders**.

### 3.4 Social anxiety disorder

**The most explicitly CBT-privileging guideline in the set.** NICE CG159, rec 1.3.2: *"**Offer adults with social anxiety disorder individual cognitive behavioural therapy (CBT) that has been specifically developed to treat social anxiety disorder (based on the Clark and Wells model or the Heimberg model)**."* Patient-facing version, verbatim: *"**Antidepressants are usually not as helpful as CBT for social anxiety disorder.**"* (https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg159/ifp/chapter/treatments-for-adults — note *helpful*, not *effective*; the bracketed substitution in an earlier corpus draft turned a patient-facing hedge into an efficacy claim). STPP sits fourth, with the explicit caveat *"Be aware of the more limited clinical effectiveness and lower cost effectiveness of this intervention compared with CBT, self-help and pharmacological interventions."*

**Best NMA.** Sun L., Dai X., Zhu S., Liu Z. & Zhang Z., *J Affect Disord* 2025;378:301–319, DOI 10.1016/j.jad.2025.02.092 (online 2025-02-27), 92 studies / 90 RCTs, searched to June 2024: **Clark & Wells cognitive therapy SMD 1.42 (95% CrI 1.14–1.70) vs waitlist — highest-ranked**; internet-delivered CBT (Andersson & Carlbring) 1.15 (0.87–1.42); psychodynamic 0.98 (0.61–1.36); guided > unguided; internet ≈ face-to-face. This **confirms rather than supersedes** the 2014 NMA NICE CG159 was built on.

**The caveat that matters clinically:** the near-total absence of active-control trials (84.4% waitlist) means SMD 1.42 is a **waitlist-referenced** number and cannot be read as "1.4 SD more benefit than an alternative credible treatment". Absolute response 0.32 — tied with specific phobia for second-lowest of the eight disorders, above only BPD.

### 3.5 Specific phobia

Cuijpers 2025 gives **g = 1.17 (0.58–1.76)** — but on **8 trials / 454 patients**, with the widest CI in the set, 62.5% waitlist controls, mean **5.3 sessions**, and **0% of participants recruited from clinical settings**. Dropout was the *lowest* of any disorder (**8%**). Absolute response 0.32. **Cuijpers 2025 excluded exposure-only treatments**, which is the dominant modality here.

VR exposure vs in vivo at matched dose: Wechsler T.F., Kümpers F.M.L.M. & Mühlberger A., *Frontiers in Psychology* 2019;10:1758 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6746888/), 9 RCTs, n = 371 — overall **g = −0.20, non-significant**; specific phobia −0.15 (n.s.); agoraphobia −0.01 (n.s.); **social phobia −0.50, significant, favouring in vivo**. Replicated: Kuleli D., Tyson P.J., Davies N. & Zeng B., *J Behav Cogn Ther*, DOI 10.1016/j.jbct.2025.100524 (2025-03-10) — VRET ≈ in-vivo for both social anxiety and specific phobia, with the authors noting the literature is too thin to declare either optimal.

### 3.6 OCD

**Weakest guideline currency in the dossier.** NICE CG31 dates from November 2005, carries *"Last reviewed: 11 July 2024"* plus an explicit **"Update in progress"** banner citing the **February 2019** surveillance decision; replacement GID-NG10435 expected **16 February 2027**. **No HAS RBP on OCD exists.** Current CG31 content: mild impairment — low-intensity ERP up to 10 therapist hours; moderate — *"either a course of an SSRI or more intensive CBT (including ERP; more than 10 therapist hours per patient), **because these treatments appear to be comparably efficacious**"*; severe — **combined SSRI + CBT**; children/young people moderate-to-severe — **family-involved CBT including ERP as treatment of choice**.

**Reference NMA.** Skapinakis P., Caldwell D.M., Hollingworth W. et al., *Lancet Psychiatry* 2016, PMID 27318812 (https://uhra.herts.ac.uk/id/eprint/7820/1/Lancet.pdf), 54 trials / 6,652 participants, searched to 2016-02-16. Mean YBOCS difference vs **drug placebo**:

| Intervention | Full network | Trials / patients | Excluding waitlist-controlled trials |
|---|---|---|---|
| Behavioural therapy | **−14.48 (−18.61 to −10.23)** | 11 / **287** | −10.41 (−14.04 to −6.77) |
| Cognitive therapy | −13.36 (−18.40 to −8.21) | 6 / **172** | −9.45 (−13.76 to −5.19) |
| BT + clomipramine | −12.97 (−19.18 to −6.74) | **1 / 31** | −11.68 (−16.73 to −6.65) |
| **CBT** | **−5.37 (−9.10 to −1.63)** | 9 / **231** | **−7.98 (−11.02 to −4.93)** |
| Clomipramine | −4.72 (−6.85 to −2.60) | 13 / 831 | −4.66 (−6.26 to −3.05) |
| SSRIs (class) | −3.49 (−5.12 to −1.81) | 37 / **3,158** | −3.62 (−4.89 to −2.34) |
| **Waiting list** | **+5.62 (0.91 to 10.26)** — *worse than drug placebo* | — | n/a |

**Read the sample sizes before the point estimates.** The three largest effects rest on 287, 172 and **31** patients, against 3,158 for the SSRI class. The authors' caveats: *"12 [80%] of the 15 psychotherapy trials explicitly allowed antidepressants"*, and *"trials using control groups with no or minimal contact with therapists usually lead to grossly overestimated effect sizes"*. **Their actual bottom line is pro-combination**, and it is routinely dropped: *"the combination of psychotherapeutic and psychopharmacological interventions is likely to be more effective than are psychotherapeutic interventions alone, at least in severe obsessive-compulsive disorder."*

**Delivery format.** Wang Y., Miguel C., Ciharova M. et al., *Br J Psychiatry* 2026;228(3):252–262, DOI 10.1192/bjp.2024.197, PMID 41189304 (online 2025-11-05, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12912871/). 61 RCTs, 3,710 patients. All formats beat controls (SMD −0.39 to −1.66); **no significant differences among individual, remote-delivery, guided self-help, time-intensive and family-involved formats**; individual, remote and family-involved **superior to group** (SMD −0.38 to −0.60); most formats superior to **unguided self-help** (−0.58 to −0.80), which was also least acceptable (RR 1.11–1.18 against it). The authors' own conclusion is notably less dismissive than a "downgraded" framing: *"Most CBT delivery formats serve as potential alternatives to conventional individual CBT. **Unguided self-help has lower but still moderate effects… and it holds important potential for assisting a larger number of individuals with OCD who face barriers to accessing treatments.**"* ⚠ **Integrity flag:** a **corrigendum** exists — *Br J Psychiatry* 2026;228(3):292, DOI 10.1192/bjp.2025.10527, epub 2025-12-23, PMID 41431266 — with no abstract and unretrievable content, so **which figures it corrects is unknown**. Read it before relying on the SMDs.

**The waitlist problem, quantified in OCD.** Reid J.E., Laws K.R., Pellegrini L., Drummond L.M. & Fineberg N.A., *Frontiers in Psychiatry* 2022;13:877089, DOI 10.3389/fpsyt.2022.877089 (2022-06-22). ⚠ **This is an OPINION article, not a primary synthesis** — its numbers are secondary re-workings. ERP vs waitlist **−1.82 (−2.11 to −1.52, k = 8)** vs ERP vs all other controls **−0.32 (−0.52 to −0.13, k = 15)**, Q = 11.67, p < 0.001 — *"effect sizes for ERP being almost 6 times larger when compared to waiting-list"*; control type explained ~33–41% of variance. Their own earlier primary meta-analysis: ERP vs waitlist g = 1.27 (0.79–1.75) but **ERP vs active psychological therapy g = −0.05 (−0.27 to 0.16)**.

Cuijpers 2025 OCD **g = 0.98 (0.81–1.16)**, 73.7% waitlist, **0% of OCD trials at low risk of bias**. Absolute response 0.38.

### 3.7 PTSD

**Strongest first-line endorsement of any disorder here.**
- **VA/DoD CPG for Management of PTSD and Acute Stress Disorder, Version 4.0, June 2023**, evidence reviewed through **2022-05-04** (https://www.healthquality.va.gov/guidelines/mh/ptsd/, page last updated 2025-08-20 — still the current edition at 2026-07-28). **Rec 7 (Strong for):** *"We recommend individual psychotherapies… **over pharmacologic interventions**"*. **Rec 8 (Strong for):** *"the individual, manualized **trauma-focused psychotherapies**: **Cognitive Processing Therapy, Eye Movement Desensitization and Reprocessing, or Prolonged Exposure**"*. **Rec 9 (Weak for):** Ehlers' Cognitive Therapy, Present-Centered Therapy, Written Exposure Therapy.
- **NICE NG116**, published 2018-12-05, **last reviewed 8 April 2025**: *"We found no new evidence that affects the recommendations in this guideline"*; surveillance decision *"We will not update the guideline on post-traumatic stress disorder at this time."* Rec 1.6.16 [2018]: *"**Offer an individual trauma-focused CBT intervention**… cognitive processing therapy; cognitive therapy for PTSD; narrative exposure therapy; prolonged exposure therapy."*
- **EMDR carries a narrower offer in NICE, and this is widely misread.** 1.6.18: **consider** EMDR 1–3 months after a **non-combat-related** trauma if the person prefers it; 1.6.19: **offer** EMDR >3 months after non-combat trauma; 1.6.20 gives EMDR the **same 8–12 session dose**. NICE's own rationale says the opposite of inferiority: *"studies that compared EMDR directly with trauma-focused CBT did not show significant differences, there was a trend towards EMDR. This trend in favour of EMDR was also present in the cost effectiveness results."* The narrower wording reflects a smaller evidence base, non-effectiveness in combat-related trauma, and a single study in the 1–3 month window.
- **APA 2025** (approved by APA Council of Representatives February 2025, https://www.apa.org/ptsd-guideline/ptsd.pdf) — **recommended (top tier): cognitive processing therapy, prolonged exposure, trauma-focused CBT**; **suggested (second tier): cognitive therapy, EMDR, narrative exposure therapy**, plus SSRIs/SNRIs. APA is the **outlier** on EMDR.

**Effect sizes.** Cuijpers 2025: **PTSD g = 1.27 (1.03–1.52) — the largest of all disorders**, with the **highest proportion of low-RoB trials (21%)** and **the highest CBT-arm dropout (24%)**. Absolute response 0.38.

**Long-term maintenance.** Weber M., Schumacher S., Hannig W. et al., *Psychological Medicine* 2021;51(9):1420–1430, PMID 34176532. 22 RCTs, N = 2,638, mean follow-up 18 months: within-group pre-test to follow-up **g = 1.36 (1.14–1.57)**; **controlled** effect vs non-directive controls at follow-up **g = 0.42 (0.15–0.68)** — small; **trauma-focused vs non-trauma-focused at follow-up g = 0.35 (−0.03 to 0.74), p = 0.07 — not significant**; dropout active 24% vs controls 18%. Smaller follow-up effects in military personnel, low-%-female samples and self-rated measures. **The authors' own conclusion is positive** and must travel with the numbers: *"Active treatments for PTSD yielded large effect sizes from pretest to follow-up and **a small controlled effect size**… **The findings suggest that CBT for PTSD is efficacious in the long term.**"* `?` The individual g values come from the full text and were not re-verified.

**Trauma-focused vs non-trauma-focused, meta-analytically.** Hoppen T.H., Jehn M., Holling H., Mutz J., Kip A. & Morina N., *J Consult Clin Psychol* 2023;91(8):445–461, DOI 10.1037/ccp0000809. 157 RCTs, 11,565 patients: TF-CBT over non-trauma-focused interventions **g = 0.17 (0.03–0.31)** short-term, **0.23 (0.06–0.40)** mid-term, **0.20 (0.04–0.35)** long-term — but dropout **RR 1.36 (1.08–1.70)** *higher* for TF-CBT. Verbatim: *"Interventions with and without trauma focus are effective and acceptable in the treatment of PTSD."*

**Cochrane is the historical baseline, not current evidence.** Bisson J.I., Roberts N.P., Andrew M., Cooper R. & Lewis C., CD003388.pub4, published **2013-12-13**, PMID 24338345, search to 2013-04-12. 70 studies, 4,761 participants: individual TF-CBT vs waitlist/usual care **SMD −1.62 (−2.03 to −1.21; 28 studies, n = 1,256)**; EMDR **−1.17 (−2.04 to −0.30; 6 studies, n = 183)**. **GRADE certainty: "very low" across all comparisons.** Re-checked 2026-07-28: `.pub4` is still the current version (chain 2005 → 2007 → 2013, stops there). One wording correction: Cochrane says there was no significant difference between individual TF-CBT, EMDR and **Stress Management** post-treatment — not "non-TF-CBT" generally.

**EMDR vs TF-CBT, at participant level — both nulls.** EMDR IPD meta-analysis, *Psychological Medicine*, online 2024-01-04: 15 eligible RCTs, 8 with usable IPD, 346 patients — **no significant difference** on severity (β = −0.24), response (β = 0.86), remission (β = 1.05) or dropout (β = −0.25); restricted to CBT-TF comparators β = −0.18 (−0.75 to 0.38), p = .525. *"The current study did not provide support for the notion that psychotherapies with eye movements are more effective in treating PTSD than those without eye movements."* And the mirror on the CBT side: Wright, Karyotaki, Sijbrandij, Cuijpers, Bisson et al., *J Consult Clin Psychol* 2025;93(6):401–426 — CBT-TF **more effective than inactive conditions (β = −0.78; OR = 2.34)** but **not significantly different from active comparators (β = 0.02)**. The waitlist-versus-active gap, not the brand, is doing the work. On mechanism: Cuijpers, van Veen, Sijbrandij, Yoder & Cristea, *Cogn Behav Ther* 2020;49(3):165–180 — dismantling studies found **no benefit of the eye movements**.

### 3.8 Insomnia — CBT-I has the cleanest first-line status here

- **AASM CPG.** Edinger J.D., Arnedt J.T., Bertisch S.M. et al., *J Clin Sleep Med* 2021;17(2):255–262, DOI 10.5664/jcsm.8986 (2021-02-01, https://jcsm.aasm.org/doi/10.5664/jcsm.8986). *"We recommend that clinicians use multicomponent cognitive behavioral therapy for insomnia for the treatment of chronic insomnia disorder in adults. (**STRONG**)"* — the only strong recommendation. **CONDITIONAL for**: multicomponent brief therapies; stimulus control alone; sleep restriction alone; **and relaxation therapy as a single-component therapy**. **CONDITIONAL against**: sleep hygiene as a single-component therapy.
- **European Insomnia Guideline update 2023.** Riemann D., Espie C.A., Altena E. et al., *J Sleep Res* 2023;32(6):e14035, DOI 10.1111/jsr.14035, PMID 38016484. Verbatim: *"Cognitive-behavioural therapy for insomnia is recommended as the first-line treatment for chronic insomnia in adults of any age (including patients with comorbidities), either applied in-person or digitally (**A**)."* Drug treatment *"can be used for the short-term treatment of insomnia (≤ 4 weeks)"*. No newer European guideline exists at 2026-07-28 — this is the cleanest "first-line" wording in the whole dossier.
- **CBT-I vs medication, the head-to-head.** Furukawa Y., Sakata M., Furukawa T.A., Efthimiou O. & Perlis M., *Psychiatry and Clinical Neurosciences* 2024;78(11):646–653, DOI 10.1111/pcn.13730, PMID 39188094 (2024-08-26), searched to 2023-12-27: **CBT-I vs pharmacotherapy OR 1.82 (1.15–2.87) for long-term remission — ≈41% (31–53%) vs 28% — certainty HIGH**; CBT-I + medication vs pharmacotherapy 1.71 (0.88–3.30), moderate; CBT-I vs CBT-I + medication 1.07 (0.63–1.80), moderate. ⚠ **The scale caveat that must travel with this table:** the entire NMA rests on **13 trials and 823 randomised participants**, median follow-up 24 weeks. "High certainty" is a CINeMA rating of a very small, consistent network — not a large body of evidence. Only one trial used a psychological placebo; all CBT-I arms were therapist-guided.
- **AASM's newest CPG reverses the "add a hypnotic" default.** Buysse D.J., Arnedt J.T., Buenaver L. et al., *J Clin Sleep Med* 2026 Apr 13;22(1):56, DOI 10.1007/s44470-025-00038-8, PMID 41975142 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13076838/). Two conditional recommendations on **low** certainty from 6 RCTs: **CONDITIONAL FOR** combination CBT-I + medication over medication alone; **CONDITIONAL AGAINST** combination over **CBT-I alone**. Remark: patients prioritising sleep *duration* early may reasonably choose combination.

### 3.9 Eating disorders

**NICE NG69**, published 2017-05-23, content last updated 2020-12-16, **last reviewed 15 August 2024** (presentational). CBT-ED is **not uniformly first-line**: anorexia nervosa in adults — *"**consider one of**: individual CBT-ED; MANTRA; specialist supportive clinical management"* (three co-equal options; CBT-ED typically up to 40 sessions over 40 weeks); binge eating disorder — *"**Offer** a binge-eating-disorder-focused guided self-help programme"* first, then group CBT-ED (16 weekly 90-minute sessions over 4 months), individual CBT-ED third; bulimia nervosa — guided self-help first, then individual CBT-ED (up to 20 sessions over 20 weeks); children/young people — family therapy first.

**Most recent meta-analysis.** Bruns J., Meier M. & Jansen K., *Int J Eat Disord*, DOI 10.1002/eat.24519, PMID 40810212 (2025-08-14), **42 RCTs**: *"CBT was more efficacious with regard to all relevant outcomes than waitlist conditions. **For anorexia nervosa, there were no studies using a waitlist condition. CBT was not superior to active treatments for any disorder or any outcome.**"* And: *"**the majority of studies had a poor risk of bias assessment**. There is a paucity of research, particularly of treatment studies for anorexia nervosa."*

### 3.10 Psychosis — the clearest guideline-vs-evidence gap

**Guideline.** NICE CG178, published February 2014, **last reviewed 29 July 2025** — the surveillance examined **metacognitive therapy only** and concluded *"We reviewed this guideline and will not be including meta-cognitive therapy (MCT) as a treatment option for psychosis."* The recommendation stands: *"**Offer CBT to all people with psychosis or schizophrenia.**"* [2009], one-to-one over **at least 16 planned sessions**.

**What the evidence says.** Jauhar S., McKenna P.J., Radua J., Fung E., Salvador R. & Laws K.R., *Br J Psychiatry* 2014;204(1):20–29, DOI 10.1192/bjp.bp.112.116285:

| Outcome | Pooled ES (95% CI) | Non-masked | Masked | p |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Overall symptoms (k=34) | −0.33 (−0.47 to −0.19) | −0.62 (−0.88 to −0.35) | **−0.15 (−0.27 to −0.03)** | 0.001 |
| Positive symptoms (k=33) | −0.25 (−0.37 to −0.13) | −0.57 (−0.76 to −0.39) | **−0.08 (−0.18 to 0.03)** — n.s. | <0.001 |
| Negative symptoms (k=34) | −0.13 (−0.25 to −0.01) | −0.22 (−0.51 to 0.08) | −0.04 (−0.14 to 0.06) | 0.26 |

*"Cognitive-behavioural therapy has a therapeutic effect on schizophrenic symptoms in the 'small' range. **This reduces further when sources of bias, particularly masking, are controlled for.**"* Use of an active control did *not* moderate; no consistent publication bias. The paper drew **four** published *BJP* comments with authors' replies (Birchwood/Shiers/Smith 2014; Byrne 2014; Peters 2014; Gold 2015) — a live dispute, not a settled one. `?` The claim that "no CBTp trial has ever used double-blinding" is unsourceable and has been withdrawn; what Jauhar et al. establish is narrower and sufficient — **assessor masking** halved-to-nullified the effect.

**Umbrella review.** Berendsen S., Berendse S., van der Torren J., Vermeulen J. & de Haan L., *eClinicalMedicine* 2024;67:102392, PMID 38274116 (2024-01-05), 26 meta-analyses (16 with usable data), searched to 2023-08-18: **only 42.9% of comparisons significant in favour of CBT; 57.1% non-significant; no comparison reached "convincing" or "highly suggestive" evidence.** Suggestive only for general psychopathology (−0.33, I² 67.9%), delusions (0.36, I² 50.5%), hallucinations (0.32, I² 45.1%); negative symptoms **weak (−0.13, −0.24 to −0.02) or non-significant (0.12, −0.03 to 0.27)**; *"At longer follow-up, evidence became weak or non-significant."* Recommendation to guideline bodies, verbatim: *"CBT has no convincing impact on other relevant outcomes. Guidelines may use these results to specify their recommendations."*

⚠ **A frequently mis-deployed "counterweight".** Tan G.X.D., Hartanto A., Eun Z.K.Y., Hu M., Hsu K.J. & Majeed N.M., *J Clin Med* 2026;15(1):187, DOI 10.3390/jcm15010187 (published 2025-12-26) is **not** a counterweight to Berendsen. Its headline medians (g = −0.38 / −0.30 / −0.39) are across the whole basket of *cognitive and behavioural* interventions — horticultural therapy, exercise, music therapy, cognitive remediation, metacognitive training and others. Its **CBT-specific** medians are total symptoms −0.31 (k=6), positive −0.31 (k=7), **negative −0.17 (k=7)**, which the authors themselves call *"little to no effect"*. It does not grade evidence strength and does not stratify by masking.

**Largest IPD moderator analysis.** Varese F., Sudell M., Morrison A.P., Longden E. & Tudur Smith C., *Health Technology Assessment* 2025;29(53), DOI 10.3310/NCFR5074 (2025-11-06). 27 trials (n = 2,870) CBT vs TAU; 11 trials (n = 961) vs active psychosocial controls. Verbatim: *"There was no reliable evidence indicating that any of the covariates considered… significantly impacted the efficacy of cognitive–behavioural therapy… **Cognitive–behavioural therapy should continue to be offered equally to service users irrespective of their demographic or clinical characteristics.**"* **Currency caveat:** its IPD requests came from database searches run **February 2018, updated January 2019** — a paper published in late 2025 resting on a six-to-seven-year-old literature search.

### 3.11 Substance use disorders

**Most recent meta-analysis.** Magill M., Nichols L.M., Kiluk B.D., Alton E., Tartak O. & Ray L.A., *Behaviour Research and Therapy*, DOI 10.1016/j.brat.2026.105101, published **2026-06-24**, NIAAA-funded. **52 RCTs (54 sites; 887 effect sizes; N = 9,442)**, search through June 2025: *"CBT was efficacious based on overall between-group pooled estimates, but… **CBT did not outperform other evidence-based treatments** but did outperform usual care or minimal treatment for consumption outcomes (**g = 0.14; 95% CI 0.01, 0.26**, I² = 61.8%; k = 28). When CBT was tested as an addition to usual care, it showed significant effects for consumption (**g = 0.44; 0.01, 0.88**, I² = 87.6%) and psychosocial functioning (**g = 0.56; 0.08, 1.03**; I² = 93.8%)."* Conclusion: benefit *"primarily for consumption outcomes, **and when tested against non-evidence-based comparators**."*

**Prior benchmark showing the decay.** Magill M. et al., *J Consult Clin Psychol* 2019;87(12):1093–1105: vs minimal treatment, early follow-up frequency g = 0.58 (0.15–1.01); vs nonspecific therapy/TAU 0.18 (0.02–0.35) early but **0.05 (−0.09 to 0.19) — n.s.** at 8+ months; **vs another specific therapy −0.02 (−0.12 to 0.08), n.s.** early and −0.04 (−0.15 to 0.08), n.s. late.

**Formal grading.** Boness C.L., Votaw V.R., Schwebel F.J. et al., DOI 10.1037/cps0000131 (2023-02-27): a **"strong recommendation"** for CBT as an empirically supported treatment for SUD on **moderate**-quality evidence; overall effect at late follow-up **g = 0.17 (0.08–0.25)**.

### 3.12 Chronic pain

**NICE NG193**, published 2021-04-07, and — checked 2026-07-28 — carrying **no "last reviewed" banner and no update notice**, i.e. never re-surveilled. Rec 1.2.3: *"**Consider** acceptance and commitment therapy (ACT) or cognitive behavioural therapy (CBT) for pain for people aged 16 years and over with chronic primary pain."* Rec 1.2.4: *"Do not offer biofeedback."* NICE's rationale is unusually explicit about why "consider" and not "offer": *"**A consistent benefit was not demonstrated in other outcomes**… the committee agreed that **the evidence was not of high quality**"*; and *"There was not enough evidence to support a preference for ACT over CBT or CBT over ACT."*

**Cochrane.** Williams A.C. de C., Fisher E., Hearn L. & Eccleston C., CD007407.**pub4**, Cochrane Library Issue 8, 2020, PMID 32794606, search to 2020-04-16. **75 studies, 9,401 participants**; CBT is the largest evidence base (59 studies). Re-checked 2026-07-28: still `.pub4`, no `.pub5`.

| Comparison | Outcome | SMD (95% CI) | n / studies | GRADE |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CBT vs **active control** | Pain | **−0.09 (−0.17 to −0.01)** | 3,235 / 23 | Moderate |
| | Disability | −0.12 (−0.20 to −0.04) | 2,543 / 19 | Moderate |
| | Distress | −0.09 (−0.18 to −0.00) | 3,297 / 24 | Moderate |
| CBT vs **TAU** | Pain | **−0.22 (−0.33 to −0.10)** | 2,572 / 29 | Moderate |
| | Disability | −0.32 (−0.45 to −0.19) | 2,524 / 28 | Low |
| | Distress | −0.34 (−0.44 to −0.24) | 2,559 / 27 | Moderate |

*"Effects were largely maintained at follow-up for CBT versus TAU, but **not** for CBT versus active control."* Adverse events *"were inadequately recorded and/or reported across studies"*. This is the clearest case in the dossier where a moderate-certainty, statistically significant result is clinically negligible: SMD −0.09 against an active control is roughly a 2-point shift on a 100-point scale, and Cochrane's own plain-language wording is *"**very slightly** less pain"*.

### 3.13 Health anxiety

**No NICE guideline and no HAS RBP** dedicated to health anxiety was found.
