**Most recent NMA.** Lai L., Liu Y., Axelsson E., Tyrer P., Li Y., Yi L., Shi C. & Ren Z., *Br J Psychiatry*, DOI 10.1192/bjp.2026.10647, published **2026-05-22**. **35 trials, 3,263 participants**, searched to 2025-01-16: significant effects vs waitlist for **CBT, exposure therapy, ACT, metacognitive therapy, MBCT and behavioural stress management**; *"**however, cognitive bias modification, imagery therapy and short-term psychodynamic psychotherapy did not show significant effects**"*. Component analysis: **exposure and response prevention, cognitive restructuring and mindfulness** linked to improved outcomes. Conclusion: *"**Both CBT and third-wave CBT are reasonable first-line choices for managing health anxiety.**"*

**The maintenance signal that matters.** Olatunji B.O. et al., *Behav Res Ther* 2014;58:65–74: 13 RCTs, N = 1,081 — post-treatment **g = 0.95**, at follow-up **g = 0.34**, a large decay; smaller effects vs TAU than vs waitlist; effect size did **not** vary by blind assessment (unusual — contrast with CBTp). Earlier benchmark: Cooper K., Gregory J.D., Walker I., Lambe S. & Salkovskis P.M., *Behav Cogn Psychother* 2017;45(2):110–123 — 14 studies, **d = 1.01 (0.77–1.25)** post-therapy, maintained at 6 and 12 months.

### 3.14 Adult ADHD — where the evidence has most clearly weakened

**NICE NG87** is current and very recently re-affirmed: *"Last reviewed: 23 July 2026"* with the surveillance decision *"We reviewed this guideline and will not be updating the recommendations on adapted trauma-focused treatment"* (https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng87). Medication is first-line in every major guideline; CBT is adjunctive.

**The decisive synthesis.** Ostinelli E.G., Schulze M., Zangani C. et al. (Cortese group), *The Lancet Psychiatry* 2025;12(1):32–43, DOI 10.1016/S2215-0366(24)00360-2, PMID 39701638, NIHR-funded. **113 unique RCTs, 14,887 participants** (28 psychological-therapy RCTs contributing only **1,116** participants), databases to 2023-09-06; primary outcomes on **both self-rated and clinician-rated** scales. Verbatim: atomoxetine and stimulants beat placebo on **both** rater sources; *"**Cognitive behavioural therapy (−0.76, −1.26 to −0.26)**, cognitive remediation, mindfulness, psychoeducation, and transcranial direct current stimulation were **better than placebo only on clinician-reported measures**"*, and *"**The effects of non-pharmacological strategies were inconsistent across different raters.**"* The self-report/clinician-report divergence is exactly what expectancy effects in unblinded psychological trials would produce.

⚠ **A competing, far more favourable synthesis should not be cited as an effect estimate.** *"Short-term and long-term effect of non-pharmacotherapy for adults with ADHD"*, *Frontiers in Psychiatry*, 2025-01-31 (37 RCTs, 2,289 participants) reports CBT vs control **SMD −4.43 short-term / −3.61 long-term** — implausibly large (>4 SD), with the paper's own quality assessment reporting **risk of bias low in only 24.3%, high in 48.6%**, and CINeMA confidence *"very low or low for most"*. Treat as a red flag about pooling or scale handling.

**Adding CBT to medication.** Li Y. & Zhang L., *Journal of Attention Disorders*, DOI 10.1177/10870547231214969, 6 RCTs: CBT+medication superior to medication alone, but *"**At the 3-month, CBT + M was superior to M, but at the 6 and 9 months, no statistically significant differences were observed.**"*

### 3.15 Bipolar disorder — adjunctive only, and contested

**Best NMA.** Miklowitz D.J., Efthimiou O., Furukawa T.A., Scott J., McLaren R., Geddes J.R. & Cipriani A., *JAMA Psychiatry* 2021;78(2):141–150, DOI 10.1001/jamapsychiatry.2020.2993 (online 2020-10-14). 39 RCTs, 3,863 participants. Manualised treatments vs controls, **recurrence OR 0.56 (0.43–0.74)**; vs TAU: family/conjoint therapy **OR 0.30 (0.17–0.53)**; **CBT OR 0.52 (0.34–0.79)**; standard psychoeducation 0.52; brief psychoeducation 0.34. Highest SUCRA: family/conjoint therapy (95%). Component finding: psychoeducation with guided skills practice and self-monitoring in a **family or group** format beats the same components delivered individually (**OR 0.12; 95% CrI 0.02–0.94**). And explicitly: *"**there is no evidence that cognitive restructuring is beneficial in acute mania**."* `?` The league-table figures come from the full text and were not independently re-verified.

**A dissenting NMA of largely the same trials.** Chatterton M.L. et al., *Br J Psychiatry* 2017;210(5):333–341: **only carer-focused interventions significantly reduced relapse (RR 0.61, 0.44–0.86)**; family-focused therapy, psychoeducation and CBT *"were **not significant**"*; *"**No intervention was associated with a significant reduction in depression symptom scale scores.**"* Miklowitz et al. explicitly reject Chatterton's conclusions — an unresolved live disagreement.

**A large recent RCT complicates it further.** *"Adjuvant Psychotherapies to Prevent Relapse in Bipolar Disorder: A Randomized Clinical Trial"*, *JAMA Psychiatry* 2024;81(9):855–862, DOI 10.1001/jamapsychiatry.2024.1310, PMID 38837133, NCT02506322 (online 2024-06-05). n = 305 euthymic BD-I/BD-II, 9 German outpatient units, observer-blind, 18 months, 24 hours of group psychotherapy over four days. Skill-oriented CBT (SEKT) vs supportive emotion-focused therapy (FEST): **relapse 49% vs 46% — equally effective**; BD-II patients did **worse** on the CBT-style therapy (70% relapse). ⚠ A previously circulated "verbatim" conclusion was fabricated; the actual text reads *"**It seems possible that** the provision of **psychoeducation**, empathy, and support are active and sufficient ingredients in relapse prevention for BD."*

Cuijpers 2025: bipolar is the **lowest** effect size of all disorders (0.31 / 0.32), **0% of bipolar trials at low risk of bias**, 66.7% care-as-usual controls, mean **18.1 sessions** — the most intensive intervention in the dataset for the smallest effect.

### 3.16 Children and adolescents

**Anxiety — Cochrane, current.** James A.C., Reardon T., Soler A., James G. & Creswell C., CD013162.pub2, published **2020-11-16**, search to October 2019. **87 studies, 5,964 participants**:

| Comparison | Remission of **primary** anxiety diagnosis | Quality |
|---|---|---|
| vs waitlist / no treatment | **CBT 49.4% vs 17.8%; OR 5.45 (3.90–7.60)**, n = 2,697, 39 studies, **NNTB 3** | Moderate |
| vs **treatment as usual** | **OR 3.19 (0.90–11.29) — not significant**, n = 487, 8 studies | Low |
| vs **attention control** | OR 2.28 (1.33–3.89), n = 822, 10 studies | Low |
| vs **alternative treatment** | **OR 0.89 (0.35–2.23) — no difference** (all anxiety diagnoses), n = 401, 4 studies | Low |

Verbatim: *"**CBT is probably more effective in the short-term than waiting lists/no treatment, and may be more effective than attention control. We found little to no evidence across outcomes that CBT is superior to usual care or alternative treatments.**"* No consistent differences by format or by presence of ASD. *"No adverse effects were reported across all studies; however… it was not clear that these were systematically monitored."*

**Depression — much weaker, and the field says so.** Zhou X., Teng T., Zhang Y. et al., *Lancet Psychiatry* 2020 (71 trials, 9,510 participants): *"**Only fluoxetine plus CBT and fluoxetine were significantly more effective than pill placebo or psychological controls**"* — i.e. CBT alone did not separate. Zhou X., Hetrick S.E., Cuijpers P. et al., *World Psychiatry* 2015;14(2):207–222 (52 RCTs, 3,805 patients): at post-treatment only IPT and CBT beat most controls, but *"**only IPT retained this superiority at both short-term and long-term follow-up**"* and *"**in the analysis of efficacy at long-term follow-up… CBT was not superior to any control condition**"*; IPT and problem-solving had significantly fewer all-cause discontinuations than CBT. That paper also states the nocebo finding directly: *"**Waitlist was significantly inferior to no-treatment (SMD = −0.46)**… **Waitlist may inflate the effect of psychotherapies.**"*

Weersing V.R. et al., *J Clin Child Adolesc Psychol*, DOI 10.1080/15374416.2024.2384022 (2024): for adolescents, CBT, IPT-A, CBT+medication and collaborative care are **well-established**; *"The evidence was considerably weaker for children, with **no treatments achieving well-established or probably efficacious status**."* And on the trajectory: *"**this early promise was not borne out fully in the trials and reviews that followed**… **Effect sizes for depression treatments now rank as the lowest among interventions for common youth mental health problems**"* — 54% clinically significant improvement, **39% robust recovery**.

Direct number: Cuijpers 2023 *World Psychiatry*, children/adolescents subgroup **g = 0.41 (0.25–0.57)**, NNT 8.1, 39 comparisons — **but restricted to low-RoB studies, g = 0.17 (−0.10 to 0.45), not significant.**

> **Scope this correctly.** The corpus in one place lists "children and adolescents" flatly as a population where CBT works less well. The supporting figures are all from **depression** trials. Read together with the Cochrane paediatric-anxiety result above (OR 5.45 vs waitlist, NNTB 3, moderate quality), the defensible claim is **"CBT underperforms in youth *depression*"**, not a population-level statement.

---

## 4. Third-wave and adjacent therapies

### 4.1 The cross-cutting answer: none has beaten classic TCC

**Dedicated third-wave NMA, depression.** Heinitz C., Schefft C., Guhn A., Brakemeier E.-L., Sterzer P. & Köhler S., *Frontiers in Psychiatry* 2023;14:1189970, DOI 10.3389/fpsyt.2023.1189970, PROSPERO CRD42020147535 (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10585267/, 2023-10-05). 55 RCTs, 5,827 patients, searched to 2022-07-31; ACT, BA, CBASP, DBT, MBCT, MCT, positive psychotherapy and schema therapy against each other, CBT, TAU, waitlist, medication management and active controls.

- *"**None of the third-wave psychotherapies differed from CT/CBT in terms of efficacy.**"*
- All except **DBT and schema therapy** were significantly superior to waitlist and TAU (0.78–1.99).
- ACT, BA, MBCT, MCT and positive psychotherapy beat *active* controls; only MCT beat medication management.
- Head-to-head: MCT > MBCT (SMD 0.92, 0.23–1.65), MCT > DBT (1.27, 0.30–2.33), MCT > CBASP (0.88, 0.14–1.67). MCT ranked first on SUCRA, BA second.
- **All third-wave therapies tolerated as well as CBT; none *more* acceptable than waitlist or CBT.**
- Quality: only **27% of trials at low risk of bias**; GRADE confidence *"largely low"*; I² = 77%.
- **The authors' verdict is symmetrical and should be quoted as such:** *"Third-wave therapies are largely efficacious and acceptable **alternatives** to CBT when compared to TAU… **The evidence so far does not point toward superiority or inferiority over CBT.**"* The harsher line sometimes quoted (*"no evidence suggesting that they overcome the limitations in efficacy or acceptability of CBT"*) is discussion text, not the abstract.
- **The MCT exception is self-undercut in the body:** its advantage was *"partly driven by two trials in which patients in the control conditions did not improve"*, and *"In a more recent high quality trial (n = 155), MCT did not show superiority over CBT."*

**All psychotherapies for depression.** Cuijpers P. et al., *World Psychiatry* 2021;20:283–293, DOI 10.1002/wps.20860. 331 RCTs, 34,285 patients: pairwise CBT vs "third wave" **OR 0.96 (0.67–1.36), 8 trials, I² = 0**; NMA response third wave vs CBT **OR 1.00 (0.76–1.31)**. **At 12-month follow-up, CBT, BA, problem-solving, IPT and psychodynamic therapy had significant effects vs care-as-usual — "third wave" therapies did not.** `?` SUCRA ranks other than the third-wave value (66.5) were not re-verified; third wave ranking nominally above CBT is a ranking artefact, not a difference.

**Older adults, anxiety.** Hendriks et al., Cochrane CD007674.pub3, published 2024-07-08, evidence to 2024-02-12. CT, BT, CBT **and third-wave CBT** pooled as one class in ≥55-year-olds; 21 RCTs, 1,234 participants: anxiety SMD −0.51 (−0.66 to −0.36) post-treatment, low certainty; **at 3–6 months SMD −0.29 (−0.59 to 0.01), i.e. little or no difference from minimal management**; dropout RR 1.19 (0.80–1.78). Only 2 studies compared with another psychological therapy → *"Nor is there enough evidence to determine whether CBT works better than other psychological treatments for older adults with anxiety disorders."*

### 4.2 Therapy by therapy

**ACT.** Pro: Gloster A., Walder N., Levin M., Twohig M. & Karekla M., *J Contextual Behav Sci* 2020;18:181–192, DOI 10.1016/j.jcbs.2020.09.009 — 20 meta-analyses, 100 controlled effect sizes, n = 12,477: ACT *"generally superior to inactive controls…, treatment as usual, and most active intervention conditions **(excluding CBT)**"* — the parenthesis is load-bearing; the same paper concedes *"the quality of studies within the ACT literature varies greatly"*. Depression-specific, most recent: *BMC Psychiatry* 2025;25:627 (2025-07-01, https://link.springer.com/article/10.1186/s12888-025-07067-w), 13 RCTs / 1,362 patients — depression SMD −0.66 (−0.80 to −0.52), certainty **low**; **no significant effect on automatic thoughts** −0.28 (−0.69 to 0.12).
Sceptical: Öst L.-G., *BRAT* 2014;61:105–121, PMID 25193001 — 60 RCTs, 4,234 participants; overall ES **0.42**; **ACT vs cognitive/behavioural treatments ES 0.16, n.s.**; effect size **fell from 0.68 (2008) to 0.42 (2014)**; *"ACT is not yet well-established for any disorder"*. Full comparator breakdown, which is the cleanest single demonstration of the control-condition problem: post-treatment vs waitlist 0.63, placebo 0.59, TAU 0.55, active treatments 0.22, **CBT/BT 0.16 (n.s.)**; at follow-up overall 0.30, active 0.17, **CBT/BT 0.06 (n.s.)**. **Declared dispute:** Atkins et al. (2017) published a critique arguing it should *"be set aside"*; Öst rebutted point by point; Öst was **sole author and sole rater**. His estimates track the independent A-Tjak et al. (2015) figures closely.
Fang S. & Ding D., *J Contextual Behav Sci* 2023;28:149–168 — 38 studies, 2,536 participants: **traditional CBT outperformed ACT on anxiety**, g = 0.16 (0.03–0.29) long term; ACT outperformed CBT on **mindfulness** g = 0.48 (0.11–0.85), short term only. ⚠ The published abstract prints the short-term anxiety interval as *"g = 0.22, 95%CI (0.01, 0.23)"*, which is internally impossible — the error is in the source. Treat the short-term interval as unusable.
Williams et al., *Behavior Therapy* (issue Nov 2023): *"ACT is credibly better than weak control groups… Some evidence comparing ACT with CBT was **ambiguous**… **Other evidence comparing ACT with CBT credibly indicated CBT's superiority**"*; overall *"the evidence against stronger controls was equivocal, if not suggestive of ACT's inferiority."*
Long-term head-to-head: A-Tjak et al., *BMC Psychiatry* 2021;21:41 — 12-month follow-up of an ACT vs CBT RCT in MDD, pre-to-12-month d = −1.26 (ACT) / −1.60 (CBT), **no significant difference**; the pre-registered hypothesis that CBT would beat ACT was not supported. Mediation: **experiential avoidance mediated in the ACT arm only**, while dysfunctional attitudes and decentring mediated in *both*.
Guideline status: ACT's **only** NICE recommendation is NG193 rec 1.2.3 (chronic primary pain), with no preference between ACT and CBT. **ACT appears nowhere in NG222.**

**DBT.** Storebø et al., Cochrane CD012955.pub2, 2020-05-04, search March 2019: **75 RCTs, 4,507 participants**. Psychotherapy vs TAU for BPD symptom severity **SMD −0.52 (−0.70 to −0.33), moderate quality** — the **only** primary outcome reaching the pre-defined minimal clinically relevant difference; self-harm −0.32, suicide-related −0.34, psychosocial functioning −0.45, all low quality and **none reaching the MIREDIF threshold**. Critically: *"**A subgroup analysis comparing the different types of psychotherapy compared to TAU showed no clear evidence of a difference**"* — brand does not matter much here. DBT vs TAU specifically: severity −0.60 (3 trials, 149 patients), all low quality.
Head-to-head vs schema therapy: Assmann N., Schaich A., Arntz A. … Fassbinder E., *Psychother Psychosom* 2024;93(4):249–263 (online 2024-07-10), 164 outpatients, rater-blind, BPDSI at 1-year naturalistic follow-up: **mean difference 3.32 (95% CI −0.58 to 7.22), p = 0.094, d = −0.24 — not significant**, numerically favouring DBT. An erratum has been published on this article.
Guidelines: **NICE CG78** (BPD), published **2009-01-28**, last reviewed 2024-07-30 with presentational changes only, rec 1.3.4.5 — *"**For women** with borderline personality disorder for whom reducing recurrent self-harm is a priority, consider a comprehensive dialectical behaviour therapy programme"*. This is **17-year-old, sex-restricted guidance**, and the strongest candidate in the dossier for "current guidance that has not kept up" — though the page does carry a "Future updates" notice about contextualising to ICD-11. **NICE NG225** (self-harm, 2022-09-07, last reviewed 2024-08-16) recommends **DBT-A** for children/young people with frequent self-harm, with a candid rationale: *"**there was no evidence of effect of DBT-A on repeat self-harm by 12-month follow-up**"*.

**MBCT.** See §2.6 for Kuyken 2016. In acute depression (Schefft 2023) MBCT beat waitlist/TAU and active controls but **not CBT**, and was inferior to MCT head-to-head. NICE NG222 offers **group CBT or MBCT** as alternatives for relapse prevention (rec 1.8.5–1.8.6).

**Mindfulness-based interventions — efficacy and harms.**
*Efficacy vs a first-line drug:* Hoge E.A. et al., *JAMA Psychiatry* 2023;80(1):13–21 (online 2022-11-09), TAME trial NCT03522844: 276 randomised, 8-week MBSR vs escitalopram 10–20 mg; CGI-S improvement 1.35 vs 1.43; **difference −0.07 (95% CI −0.38 to 0.23), p = 0.65, inside the −0.495 non-inferiority margin → MBSR non-inferior**. Safety: ≥1 study-related adverse event in **78.6% of escitalopram patients vs 15.4% of MBSR patients**. The PCORI final report adds that **online MBSR was non-inferior to in-person MBSR**, while online MBSR vs online escitalopram was **inconclusive**.
*Harms — the systematically under-reported side:*
- Farias M., Maraldi E., Wallenkampf K. & Lucchetti G., *Acta Psychiatr Scand* 2020;142(5):374–393 — 83 studies, 6,703 practitioners; **pooled adverse-event prevalence 8.3% (5–12%)**, 3.7% in experimental vs **33.2% in observational** studies; commonest anxiety, depression, cognitive anomalies; events *"may occur in individuals with no previous history of mental health problems"*.
- Goldberg S.B., Lam S.U., Britton W.B. & Davidson R.J., *Psychother Res* 2022;32(3):291–305 — first population-based US survey, n = 434: **32.3%** endorsed the general adverse-effect item, **50.0%** ≥1 specific item, **10.4%** effects lasting ≥1 month, **10.6%** some functional impairment.
- In standardised 8-week programmes (n = 96, Meditation Experiences Interview, https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8845498/): **83%** ≥1 meditation-related side effect, **58%** ≥1 negatively-valenced, **37%** ≥1 with negative impact on functioning, **6–14%** "lasting bad effects" — a rate the authors note is *similar to psychotherapy generally (3–14%)*. Open-ended questions under-detected side effects by ~70%.
- Matko K. & Van Dam N., "Beyond serenity: Adverse effects of meditation and mindfulness in clinical practice", *Current Opinion in Psychology* 2026;67:102197, DOI 10.1016/j.copsyc.2025.102197 (issue February 2026): *"**25–87 % of those who engage in meditation report adverse effects (AEs), with 3–37 % experiencing functional impairment**"*; *"**It remains unclear to what extent AEs reflect transitory discomfort vs. lasting harm.**"*
- **>80% of RCTs of mindfulness-based interventions provide no adverse-effect statement** (cited in Goldberg 2022 from Wong et al. 2018) — historical safety claims rest on non-measurement.

**Schema therapy.** Arntz A. et al., *JAMA Psychiatry* 2022;79(4):287–299 (online 2022-03-02): 495 outpatients, 15 sites, 5 countries, **both formats delivered over 2 years**, blinded assessors. Both ST formats vs optimal TAU **d = 0.73 (0.29–1.18)**; **combined individual + group ST vs TAU d = 1.14 (0.57–1.71)**; **predominantly group ST vs TAU d = 0.30 (−0.29 to 0.89), p = .32 — not significant**. Format matters more than brand. Authors' stated limitation: *"this study did not compare ST with another specialized psychotherapy."* ⚠ This **contradicts** the smaller meta-analysis (Zhang et al., *Nord J Psychiatry* 2023;77(7), 8 RCTs / 587 participants) which found **group ST g = 0.859 vs individual ST g = 0.163** — a live inconsistency. No NICE recommendation for schema therapy exists.

**Metacognitive therapy.** Andersson E. et al. (Karolinska), *Cognitive Behaviour Therapy*, DOI 10.1080/16506073.2024.2434920, PROSPERO CRD42023479923, search to 2024-05-27: 21 MCT + 28 metacognitive *training* studies, **N = 3,239**. **MCT vs waitlist g = 1.84; MCT vs other forms of CBT g = 0.43.** Critical caveat, verbatim: *"Many of the included studies lacked data on blinding procedures, reporting of inter-rater reliability, treatment adherence, competence, treatment expectancy and pre-registration procedures."* Conclusion hedged to **"probably efficacious"**. **The effect size is shrinking as trials improve:** Normann 2014 g = 0.97 vs CBT → Normann & Morina 2018 g = 0.37–0.69 → Andersson 2024 **g = 0.43**. NICE rejected MCT for psychosis on 2025-07-29; CANMAT 2023 places it **third line** for MDD; APA 2025 places it under **insufficient evidence** for PTSD.

**Compassion-focused therapy.** Millard L., Wan M., Smith D. & Wittkowski A., *J Affect Disord* 2023;326:168–192 (2023-04-01): CFT improved clinical symptomatology and significantly favoured CFT for **self-compassion** and **self-reassurance**. Vidal J. & Soldevilla J.M., *Br J Clin Psychol* 2023;62(1):70–82, DOI 10.1111/bjc.12394 — but note the base: **only 7 controlled trials (N = 640) plus 7 observational studies (N = 207)**, and the authors' own conclusion: *"given that the number of studies is small and that **only half of them are controlled trials**, the results must be interpreted cautiously."* (This is a statement about *design*, not quality ratings — an earlier corpus draft mis-rendered it.) A 2025 systematic review of 21 studies (https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12382812/) finds consistent gains in self-compassion, **limited and inconsistent evidence for internal shame**, and *"variable methodological quality, including frequent selection bias and small sample sizes"*. **No guideline recommendation found.**

**Functional analytic psychotherapy.** López-Pinar C., Galán-Doña A.M., Tsai M. & Macías J., *Clinical Psychology: Science and Practice*, DOI 10.1037/cps0000221 (2024-06-27): 70 studies — 45 single-case designs, 16 RCTs, 4 non-randomised, 5 uncontrolled. Controlled group studies show *"robust results for depression and anxiety symptoms"*; **no significant changes for anger, experiential avoidance or mindfulness**. Ferro-García R. et al., *Papeles del Psicólogo* 2024;45(2):73–82 (official journal of the Spanish Consejo General de la Psicología) state plainly: *"**FAP is not recognized as an officially empirically supported treatment for any specific diagnostic disorder**."*

**Rumination-focused CBT.** Founding trial: Watkins E. et al., *Br J Psychiatry* 2011;199(4):317–322, n = 42 medication-refractory residual depression: response 81% vs 26%, remission 62% vs 21%, relapse 9.5% vs 53% — with the authors' own caveat that *"the trial lacked an attentional control group so cannot test whether the effects were as a result of the specific content"*. Later: Mak W. et al., *Appl Psychol Health Well Being* 2024;16:696–722, three-arm internet RCT n = 256 — *"**similar levels of improvement in all outcomes across the three conditions**… implying that the two active interventions were not superior to EDU control"*; a Swedish primary-care RCT (n = 73, *Front Psychol* 2023) found **no significant effect on repetitive negative thinking, worry, anxiety or quality of life**; and a 2024 systematic review (12 studies, *Front Psychol*, 2024-12-03) found *"**when compared to the active controls… RFCBT did not show substantial superiority**"*. The strongest generic support is for *targeting rumination* rather than for the brand: a transdiagnostic meta-analysis of CBT on repetitive negative thinking (55 studies / 4,970 participants, 2025-02-07, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39916353/) found overall g = −0.67, with **RNT-specific interventions g = −0.99 vs less specific approaches g = −0.56, a significant difference**.

### 4.3 EMDR — the live guideline divergence

EMDR is not third-wave (its own lineage, Shapiro 1989) but is the sharpest current example of guideline disagreement over a non-classic protocol. NICE NG116 is the most restrictive (combat exclusion, preference-gating in the 1–3-month window, second-choice in children); VA/DoD 2023 places it **inside the Strong-for trauma-focused recommendation**; APA 2025 places it **second tier**, making APA the outlier against NICE 2018, ISTSS 2019/2020, Phoenix Australia 2022, VA/DoD 2023 and APS 2024.

A 2025 critique in the *Journal of EMDR Practice and Research* (https://spj.science.org/doi/10.34133/jemdr.0034) argues APA 2025 *"relied on a systematic review published in 2018 that analyzed research published up to 2017"* and that *"across all 4 reviews… the most recent EMDR therapy study is from 2017"*. **Conflict of interest: published in an EMDR-specialty journal by EMDR proponents** — an interested party, not a neutral appraisal, though its comparison table is the clearest published statement that APA is out of step. Similarly, Simpson et al., *Br J Psychology* 2025 (17 studies / 16 RCTs, n = 1,031, plus 13 carried over from NICE 2018) is **funded by EMDR Association UK**; it finds EMDR significantly better than waitlist/usual care (self-report SMD 1.86, 95% CrI 1.18–2.58) but **EMDR vs TF-CBT only a small, non-significant potential benefit** — self-report SMD 0.46 (−0.40 to 1.41), clinician-rated 0.15 (−0.17 to 0.54), with *"Most studies had small sample sizes, and all but one was at high/moderate risk of bias."*

An AMSTAR-2-appraised overview of **55 meta-analyses** of psychological interventions for adult PTSD (search March 2024, PROSPERO CRD42020151234, https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0887618525000532) reports: *"**Beneficial long-term effects exceeding one-month post-treatment were limited to trauma-focused cognitive behavior interventions (TF-CBT) and eye movement desensitization and reprocessing (EMDR).**"* It also documents *"substantial overlap of primary RCTs, indicating redundancy between meta-analyses"* — which is why different meta-analyses reach opposite conclusions on TF-CBT vs EMDR.

### 4.4 Distinct or repackaged?

**Where "distinct" holds up is mediation, not outcome:** experiential avoidance mediated in the ACT arm only (A-Tjak 2021); Ruiz's 2012 meta-analysis found ACT affected its own putative processes while neither therapy differentially affected CBT's; RNT-specific interventions reduce rumination more than non-specific ones; ACT and CBT differ in *which* secondary outcome moves.

> **Net reading (2026-07-28).** For most third-wave therapies the correct statement is **"no evidence of a difference from TCC", which is not the same as "evidence of equivalence"**. Schefft 2023 is explicit that its evidence *"does not point toward superiority or inferiority over CBT"*, with GRADE confidence largely low, 27% of trials at low risk of bias and I² = 77%. **Only two results in the whole dossier are equivalence estimates precise enough to exclude a meaningful difference: Cochrane's BA vs CBT RR 0.99 (0.92–1.07), and the COBRA non-inferiority trial.** Everything else is an underpowered null. Third-wave therapies are **credible alternatives** with additional value in delivery economics (BA), specific populations (DBT in BPD/self-harm, ACT in chronic pain, MBCT in relapse prevention) and process targeting — **not** improvements on TCC's outcome ceiling.

---

## 5. The honest efficacy picture, and its limits

### 5.0 The framing fact

The largest and most self-critical evidence base in the field is the **Metapsy / VU Amsterdam living systematic review** run by Pim Cuijpers' group (https://www.metapsy.org, database searched from 1966, updated at least yearly). Almost every number in this section traces back to it — **which is itself a limitation**: the sceptical literature on CBT and the pro-CBT literature are largely produced by the same small network of authors, several of whom appear on both sides of the decline-effect dispute. Independent replication of the *meta-analytic* layer is scarce. The genuinely independent sceptical sources here are few: Jauhar/Laws (psychosis), Reid/Laws (OCD waitlists), Leichsenring (NMA method), de Felice (common factors), Johnsen & Friborg (the decline claim), Klatte and Honkalampi (harms), Krieger/Munder (iCBT contact), and Ormel (the treatment-prevalence paradox, though Cuijpers and Hollon are co-authors).

Cuijpers' own summary, unusually blunt for a proponent — *American Psychologist* 79(9):1407–1417, DOI 10.1037/amp0001387 (December 2024, https://www.doctortic.net/CBCN2025/Cuijpers2024.pdf):

> *"The effect sizes of psychological treatments have also not improved in the past 5 decades… and less than half of patients receiving psychological treatment respond… Furthermore, the uptake of services has increased considerably in high-income countries over the past decades, but this has not resulted in a reduction in the prevalence of depression in the community: the treatment-prevalence paradox."*

And verbatim: *"**none of the new therapies that have been introduced over the past 50 years are more effective than previous treatments**"* — with the recommendation *"not to embrace new therapies too easily but to focus on other innovations… such as increased frequency of sessions, feedback to patients, and better matching the needs of patients to the expertise of therapists."*

### 5.1 The decline effect: settled as "no linear decline, but no improvement either"

- **The original claim.** Johnsen T.J. & Friborg O., *Psychological Bulletin* 141(4):747–768, DOI 10.1037/bul0000015 (July 2015; **correction published 2016**, PMID 26890388). 70 trials 1977–2014, huge *within-group* effect sizes (g ≈ 1.58 BDI, 1.69 HRSD) showing a linear decline with publication year.
- **Rebuttal 1 — the decline stopped in 1995.** Ljótsson B., Hedman-Lagerlöf E., Mattsson S. & Andersson E., *Psychol Bull* 143(3):321–325, DOI 10.1037/bul0000055: a piecewise meta-regression on the same data shows the decline **halts from 1995 onward**.
- **Rebuttal 2 — it is spurious.** Cristea I.A., Ștefan S., Karyotaki E., David D., Hollon S.D. & Cuijpers P., *Psychol Bull* 143(3):326–340, DOI 10.1037/bul0000062. Restricting to RCTs they found **30 additional eligible trials** the original missed; within-group effect sizes showed **I² ≈ 90%**, *"effectively precluding the realization of a meta-analysis"*; publication year was **not** significant in low-RoB studies, BDI-II-only studies, most HRSD analyses, or **any analysis using between-group effect sizes**. The predictors that survived were **number of participants in the CBT arm** and **recruitment from clinical samples**, both negatively related to outcome — i.e. the "decline" is largely *trials got bigger and recruited real patients rather than volunteers*.
- **The original authors did not concede.** Friborg O. & Johnsen T.J., *Psychol Bull*, DOI 10.1037/bul0000090: they replicate the flat-after-1995 result for the 34 HRSD studies but **not** for the 67 BDI studies (best non-linear model quadratic, not flat), and close with *"when looking at all of the studies together, there is a clear decline in the effects of CBT, which should concern therapy researchers within the field rather than being explained away."*
- **Where it stands now.** Cuijpers 2023 *World Psychiatry*: *"**There was no reduction of the effect size of CBT according to the publication year**"*. Cuijpers, Harrer, Miguel, Ciharova & Karyotaki, *American Psychologist* 80(3):297–310, DOI 10.1037/amp0001250 (online 2023-11-16): 562 RCTs, 669 comparisons, 66,361 patients; pooled **g = 0.67 (0.62–0.72)**, I² = 83, **prediction interval [−0.41, 1.75]** (in a non-trivial fraction of populations the true effect is *negative*), NNT 4.57; *"We found no indication that year of publication was significantly associated with the effect size."*
- **The strongest sceptical datum in this whole dossier is in that same paper.** **Low-RoB studies have significantly smaller effect sizes (b = −0.21, SE = 0.05, p < .001)**; restricting to low-RoB comparisons drops the pooled effect from **g = 0.67 (669 comparisons) to g = 0.49 (0.42–0.55, 218 comparisons)**, NNT 4.57 → 6.67, prediction interval still spanning zero **[−0.27, 1.25]**. And: *"**the effect sizes were also considerably smaller after adjustment for publication bias (g ranged from 0.26 to 0.57)**"*, plus *"The quality of trials has increased over time, but still, a majority do not meet basic quality criteria, not even in recent years."*

> **Synthesis.** The *linear decline* claim did not survive. What replaced it is arguably worse for CBT's story: **effects have not fallen, but they have not improved in fifty years either**, and the headline effect size is substantially an artefact of low-quality trials. The debate also silently established that **within-group (pre-post) effect sizes are not a legitimate meta-analytic quantity** — the same group published a methods note to that effect (Cuijpers, Weitz, Cristea & Twisk, *Epidemiol Psychiatr Sci* 26:364–368, 2017).

### 5.2 Publication bias

**The only *direct* test in the field** (comparing published with genuinely unpublished trials rather than inferring from funnel-plot asymmetry): Driessen E., Hollon S.D., Bockting C.L.H., Cuijpers P. & Turner E.H., *PLoS ONE* 10(9):e0137864, DOI 10.1371/journal.pone.0137864 (2015-09-30). **13 of 55 (23.6%)** NIH-funded grants that began trials never produced a publication. Adding the unpublished studies (**g = 0.20, −0.11 to 0.51, k = 6** — not significantly different from zero) to the published ones (**g = 0.52, 0.37–0.68, k = 20**) gives **g = 0.39 (0.08–0.70)** — a **25% reduction**. Authors' caveat: *"these findings may overestimate the 'true' effect… as **outcome reporting bias could not be examined quantitatively**"*. Conclusion: *"Clinicians, guidelines developers, and decision makers should be aware that the published literature overestimates the effects of the predominant treatments for depression."* Calibration points in the same paper: Turner et al.'s FDA-based antidepressant analysis reduced the pooled effect **0.41 → 0.31 (24%)**; Cuijpers et al. 2010 *Br J Psychiatry* trim-and-fill on 117 published trials imputed **51 "missing" studies** and reduced the effect **0.67 → 0.42 (37%)**.

**What correction does to the clinically meaningful numbers.** Cuijpers P., Karyotaki E., Ciharova M., Miguel C., Noma H. & Furukawa T.A., *Acta Psychiatrica Scandinavica* 144(3):288–299, DOI 10.1111/acps.13335 (2021-06-09), 228 RCTs (75 low RoB):

| Comparator | NNT (main) | NNT after publication-bias adjustment | NNT low-RoB only |
|---|---|---|---|
| vs care-as-usual | 5.3 (3.9–7.4) | **9.7** | 6.0 |
| vs waitlist | 3.9 (3.2–4.8) | **5.7** | 5.1 |
| vs pill placebo | 6.7 (3.5–23.0) | **14.3 — no longer significant** | 24.5 |

Significant publication bias for the CAU and waitlist comparisons (**p < 0.001** for both). **Against a pill placebo, the effect of psychotherapy for depression does not survive correction for publication bias.** That is the single most damaging quantitative finding in this dossier, and it comes from the field's leading proponent.

**Bias is real but not uniform.** Papola 2023's GAD NMA found *"both the visual inspection of the funnel plot and the Egger test were negative for small study effects"*; Jauhar 2014 found *"no consistent evidence of publication bias"* in CBT for schizophrenia.

**The meta-analytic layer itself is now under audit.** Fober I., Baran L., Samara M., Siafis S., Grimes D.R. & Helfer B., *PLoS One* 20(6):e0325384, DOI 10.1371/journal.pone.0325384 (2025-06-06) — protocol only, no results. Their prior: reviews of prior work report *"critically low quality of 53%–99% and 68%–88%"* of systematic reviews in medicine and mental health respectively. Their planned toolkit — AMSTAR 2, ROBIS, spin measure, **Fragility Index, Ellipse of Insignificance, Region of Attainable Redaction, GRIM test, Leave-N-Out, prediction intervals** — is the current state of the art for stress-testing psychotherapy meta-analyses.

### 5.3 The comparator problem

**The quantification.** Cuijpers P., Miguel C., Harrer M., Ciharova M. & Karyotaki E., *Epidemiology and Psychiatric Sciences* 33:e56, DOI 10.1017/S2045796024000611 (2024, open access). 333 RCTs, 472 comparisons, 41,480 participants: overall g = 0.77 (0.71–0.84), I² = 84; **waitlist-controlled g = 0.95 (0.85–1.04) vs care-as-usual-controlled g = 0.63 (0.55–0.71), p < 0.001** — surviving every sensitivity analysis. Mechanism identified: **the pre-post change *within* the waitlist arm (g = 0.37, 0.28–0.46) is significantly smaller than within the CAU arm (g = 0.64, 0.50–0.78)**; change within *therapy* arms did not differ. So the inflation comes from the control arm being artificially frozen, not from therapy working better. Verbatim conclusion: *"**WL control conditions should be avoided in randomized trials examining psychological treatments of adult depression.**"* Their own calibration: the waitlist-vs-CAU SMD is **0.29 (0.14–0.45)** — *"comparable to the difference between antidepressants and placebo (SMD = 0.30; Cipriani et al., 2018)"*. **The choice of control group is worth as much effect size as an antidepressant.**

**Outside depression it is worse, because waitlists dominate.** From Cuijpers 2024 *World Psychiatry*, Table 1, proportion of trials using a waitlist control: specific phobia **95.8%**, social anxiety **84.6%**, PTSD **79.3%**, panic **75.0%**, OCD **72.7%**, GAD **70.8%**, MDD 42.0%, **BPD 0%** (100% care-as-usual). Overall 270/441 = **61.2%**. The anxiety-disorder evidence base is built on the most inflating comparator available — and BPD, the disorder with the *lowest* measured response, is the only one built entirely on the stricter comparator. That confound is rarely stated when disorders are ranked by "treatability".

**The origin claim.** Furukawa T.A., Noma H., Caldwell D.M. et al., "Waiting list may be a nocebo condition in psychotherapy trials", *Acta Psychiatr Scand* 130(3), 2014, DOI 10.1111/acps.12275. `?` The commonly quoted figures (49 RCTs, 2,730 participants, OR 2.9, 95% CI 1.3–5.7 favouring no-treatment over waitlist) come from secondary summaries; the primary paper is paywalled and was not opened. The *existence* and *direction* of the finding are confirmed by Cuijpers 2024 EPS, which cites it as one of four establishing studies. `?` Cuijpers has since asked in print whether control groups should be used at all — *"Has the time come to stop using control groups in trials of psychosocial interventions?"*, *World Psychiatry*, cited as in press; publication status unverified.

### 5.4 Risk of bias in the trial base

**Depression / CBT, from Cuijpers 2023 *World Psychiatry* Table 1 (409 trials):** adequate sequence generation 224 (54.8%); adequate allocation concealment 201 (49.1%); **adequate masking of assessors 101 (24.7%)**; intention-to-treat analysis 262 (64.1%); **low RoB on all four: 131 (32.0%)**; no or only one domain met: 105 (25.7%). Three quarters of the CBT-for-depression trial base has **unmasked outcome assessment** — in a treatment that cannot be blinded to patient or therapist, that leaves essentially no blinding anywhere in three quarters of trials.

**Across eight disorders (Cuijpers 2024 *World Psychiatry*, Table 1):** overall low-RoB **93/441 = 21.1%**; MDD 39.5%; GAD 16.7%; OCD 13.6%; specific phobia 12.5%; BPD 9.5%; PTSD 8.7%; social anxiety 7.7%; **panic disorder 6.2%**.

**The effect of rigour on the estimate, across four independent syntheses:** Cuijpers 2023 *Am Psychol* b = −0.21, pooled g 0.67 → 0.49; Papola 2023, excluding high-RoB studies caused **relaxation therapy to lose superiority over TAU**; Jauhar 2014, masking cut the CBTp effect from −0.62 to −0.15; Cuijpers 2023 *World Psychiatry* 0.79 → 0.60 → 0.47. **The consistent pattern is that methodological rigour costs roughly 0.2–0.5 SD of apparent effect.**

**Outcome-measure heterogeneity is itself a bias channel — new in 2025/2026.** Sprenger A.A., Harrer M., Miguel C. et al., *Journal of Affective Disorders* 397:120873, DOI 10.1016/j.jad.2025.120873, PMID 41371355 (online 2025-12-09). 492 trials, 1,260 comparisons, ~51,000 patients, 1977–2024, **17 different depression outcome instruments**. PHQ-9 use rose (b = 1.04, p < 0.001) and HDRS use rose (b = 0.29, p = 0.021), **but overall heterogeneity in instrument choice also rose significantly (b = 0.12, p < 0.001)**, and **which instrument a trial used predicts its effect estimate** — CES-D inflates, GDS deflates. The underlying measurement problem: **>280 depression instruments exist**; average symptom overlap among seven common scales is a Jaccard index of only **41%** (Fried 2017); common scales cover at most **59%** of domains that matter to patients (Veal et al., *Lancet Psychiatry* 11(4):285–294, DOI 10.1016/S2215-0366(23)00438-8). *Pooling CBT effects across these scales assumes an equivalence that does not hold.*

### 5.5 Spin, preregistration, replication

- **Spin.** Stoll M., Mancini A., Hubenschmid L. et al., *Journal of Clinical Epidemiology*, DOI 10.1016/j.jclinepi.2020.06.008 (2020), depression psychotherapy RCTs 2015–2018. `?` **Figures from the indexed abstract; full text not opened:** discrepancies between registration and publication in **102 of 134 (76%)** registered trials; of **72** trials with a non-significant primary outcome, **68 (94%) showed spin**. Prospective registration reduced protocol discrepancies but had **no relation to spin**.
- **Preregistration does not demonstrably fix it.** van den Akker O.R., van Assen M.A.L.M., Bakker M. & Wicherts J.M., *Behavior Research Methods*, DOI 10.3758/s13428-023-02277-0 (2023-11-10). 193 preregistered vs 193 matched non-preregistered psychology studies: **no significant difference in proportion of positive results (β = 0.01, p = .96), no significant difference in effect sizes (0.29 vs 0.36; p = .175), no fewer statistical errors.** What preregistration bought: power analyses (0.55 vs 0.23) and larger samples (mean 959 vs 537). *"Our study does not provide convincing evidence that preregistration prevents p-hacking and HARKing."* Note this **contradicts** earlier work (Schäfer & Schwarz 2019: median effect sizes 0.16 preregistered vs 0.36; Scheel et al. 2021: 0.44 vs 0.96 positive-result rates) — the field is not settled.
- **Preregistration of the meta-analyses themselves is poor.** *Advances in Methods and Practices in Psychological Science*, DOI 10.1177/25152459241300113: of **1,403** psychology meta-analyses published in 2021, **382 (27%)** were preregistered; in a random sample of 100 of those, publication bias was preregistered in only 37%, hypotheses in only 7%, and **every single one contained at least one deviation from protocol (median 9), most undisclosed (median 8)**.
- **Replication.** No registered direct replication of any landmark CBT trial was found.

### 5.6 Dodo bird, common factors, and where it cracks

- **Equivalence inside depression is robust.** Cuijpers P., Quero S., Noma H. et al., *World Psychiatry* 20(2):283–293, DOI 10.1002/wps.20860, 331 RCTs / 34,285 patients: *"**Individual psychotherapies did not differ significantly from each other, with the only exception of non-directive supportive counseling**, which was less efficacious than all other therapies."* SMDs vs CAU ranged −0.81 (life-review) to −0.32 (NDST); results held in low-RoB-only analyses; **NDST and psychodynamic therapy did not beat pill placebo**.
- **CBT's specific advantage is ~0.06 SD and evaporates.** Cuijpers 2023: *"CBT was significantly more effective than other psychotherapies, but the difference was **small (g = 0.06; 95% CI: 0–0.12)** and became non-significant in most sensitivity analyses."* The lower bound is exactly zero.
- **The 2025 transdiagnostic replication — and its one crack.** Boxell O. et al., *Journal of Contemporary Psychotherapy*, DOI 10.1007/s10879-025-09712-7 (2025-12-22). Controlled trials 2014–2024, **k = 90, n = 9,637**, five modalities, 18 disorders. Overall **g = −0.32**; *"The effect was not moderated by either the modalities or the 18 mental disorders"*, and a factor analysis found all five modalities loading on a **common latent variable**. **But** modelling *rate of change*, *"psychotherapy modality had a moderating effect **contra the equivalence hypothesis**"*, driven by mindfulness acting faster.
- **The "common factors explain 30–70%" claim is statistically unsound as stated.** de Felice G., Giuliani A., Halfon S., Andreassi S., Paoloni G. & Orsucci F.F., *New Ideas in Psychology* 54:50–55, DOI 10.1016/j.newideapsych.2019.01.006: *"This formulation is based on the assumption that common and specific factors are independent of each other. The present study… empirically demonstrate[s] that common and specific factors of change are actually correlated… the prevalent practice… is both **statistically unsound and conceptually distorted**."* **The familiar "alliance ≈ 30%, technique ≈ 5–15%" figures should not be quoted as established.**
- **The NMA method itself is contested.** Leichsenring F., Steinert C., Luyten P. & Heim N., "Network meta-analysis: science or alchemy? — What works best in generalized anxiety disorder?", UCL Discovery record dated 2024-06-01 (https://discovery.ucl.ac.uk/id/eprint/10181383). `?` **Source-grade caveat: the journal of record, volume and DOI could not be confirmed** — treat as an author manuscript of a published comment. Their points: *"As the treatments being compared in indirect comparisons have not been randomized directly within the individual trials **NMA provides evidence of only an observational nature**"*; Papola's own NMA found no significant differences between active treatments yet concluded CBT should be first-line, which is the difference-of-significance-is-not-significance-of-difference error; controlling for RoB, the CBT-vs-psychodynamic difference shrinks **−0.46 → −0.23**, below Papola's own prespecified clinical-meaningfulness threshold of −0.40; and the CI for CBT vs TAU has a **lower bound below that same threshold**.

> **A caution about the equivalence claim's flatness.** "Non-directive supportive counselling is the only therapy CBT reliably beats — everything else is a tie" is the disorder-level, brand-level finding. It sits against clear **within-CBT** differentiation documented elsewhere in this dossier: Clark & Wells cognitive therapy 84% vs exposure + applied relaxation 42% recovery in social anxiety; Pompoli's component NMA finding muscle relaxation and VR exposure significantly *reduce* efficacy in panic; individual/remote/family formats significantly beating group for OCD. Both can be true — brands converge while components diverge — but the equivalence claim should not be stated at a flatness the rest of the evidence does not support.

### 5.7 Therapist effects

Johns R.G., Barkham M., Kellett S. & Saxon D., *Clinical Psychology Review* 67:78–93, DOI 10.1016/j.cpr.2018.08.004 (February 2019). 20 studies (3 RCTs, 17 practice-based), therapist effects found in 19 of 20; **range 0.2%–29% of outcome variance; weighted average ≈ 5%**; RCTs 8.2% (12.9% by patients, 17.4% by therapists); naturalistic ≈ 5%; university counselling centres 2.4%. Consistent with Baldwin & Imel 2013 (3–7%). **Patient intake severity predicts larger therapist effects** — the therapist matters most for the sickest patients. The clinically decisive line: *"studies from the review reported **some therapists being consistently more than twice as effective as others after controlling for case-mix**"*; Saxon & Barkham (2012), 119 therapists / 10,786 patients — *"The least effective therapists… had almost half the recovery rate of the above average therapists."* The authors explicitly **declined to meta-analyse** because heterogeneity was too high.

**The measurement-invalidating point.** Owen J., Drinane J.M., Idigo K.C. & Valentine J.C., *Psychotherapy* 52(3):321–328, DOI 10.1037/pst0000014 — re-examining 17 meta-analyses with significant positive effects, **80% remained significant under a conservative therapist-effect assumption but only 20% under a large one (ICC = 0.20)**. `?` These figures are quoted from Johns' doctoral thesis, not from the Owen paper itself. Since almost no psychotherapy meta-analysis models therapist nesting, the standard errors in this entire literature are too small.

**Counter-evidence worth recording, and unresolved.** *"Understanding the Therapist Contribution to Psychotherapy Outcome: A Meta-Analytic Approach"*, *Adm Policy Ment Health* 44:664–680, DOI 10.1007/s10488-016-0783-9 — 15 studies (N = 910 commencing) randomising participants to the *same* treatment as self-help versus with a therapist: *"no difference in treatment completion rate and broad equivalence of treatment outcomes"*, and contrary to expectation **outcome variability was also broadly equivalent** — *"the findings did not suggest that individual differences between therapists play a major role in psychotherapy outcome."* This sits awkwardly with the main body and should be presented as unresolved tension.

### 5.8 Dropout, non-response and the honest denominator

- **Dropout.** Fernandez E., Salem D., Swift J.K. & Ramtahal N., *J Consult Clin Psychol* 83(6):1108–1122, DOI 10.1037/ccp0000044. 115 studies, 20,995 participants. `?` **Figures from the indexed abstract:** weighted dropout **15.9% pre-treatment and 26.2% during treatment**; highest attrition in **depression**; highest by format in **e-therapy**; dropout **not** associated with client age group, therapist licensure, RCT vs non-RCT design, or publication recency.
- **Acceptability relative to controls: no advantage.** Papola 2023: *"Relative risks for all-cause discontinuation signaled **no differences compared with treatment as usual for all psychotherapies**"* (CBT vs TAU RR 1.04, 0.64–1.67). Cuijpers 2021 NMA: *"No consistent differences in acceptability were found."*
- **Absolute response — the number a patient should actually be told.** Cuijpers 2021 *Acta Psychiatr Scand* (228 RCTs), response = ≥50% symptom reduction at 2 (±1) months: any psychotherapy **0.41 (0.38–0.43)**; care-as-usual 0.17 (0.15–0.20); waitlist 0.16 (0.14–0.18). Remission ~one third vs 7–13% of controls. Verbatim: *"**more than half of patients receiving therapy do not respond and only one third remitted.**"*
- **And the decomposition.** Cuijpers 2024 *Am Psychol*: with a 41% response rate, *"a considerable proportion of patients do not respond (**59%**) or would also have responded without treatment (**16%–17%**)."* **The number of patients for whom CBT is the reason they got better is on the order of one in four.**
- **Real-world usual care has not improved either.** Cuijpers P., Miguel C., Harrer M., Ciharova M. & Karyotaki E., *Journal of Affective Disorders*, DOI 10.1016/j.jad.2024.05.019 (May 2024): pooling the CAU arms of the RCT database, response rates are low (primary care **0.27**, 0.23–0.31), and *"We found hardly any indications that the outcomes have improved over the years."*

### 5.9 Harms — the fastest-moving and worst-measured part

- **The one clean quantitative estimate.** Cuijpers 2021 *Acta Psychiatr Scand*, using a Reliable Deterioration Index computed uniformly rather than relying on authors' reporting: **reliable deterioration <5% in psychotherapy arms vs 7–13% in control arms** (RRs 0.27–0.37 favouring therapy). The same paper reports that in an earlier meta-analysis **only 6% of trials reported deterioration rates at all**.
- **The reporting failure, quantified.** Klatte R., Strauss B., Flückiger C., Färber F. & Rosendahl J., *Psychotherapy Research*, DOI 10.1080/10503307.2023.2286992 (December 2023). From 115 protocols, **85 RCTs**, 14,420 participants: *"Harmful events… were explicitly reported in **60% of the studies**"*; *"**For most reported AEs, the association to study treatment remained unclear**"*; *"**AEs can be expected to affect more than one in ten participants. Serious AEs occurred more than in one in 21 participants.**"*
- **The review-of-reviews angle.** Honkalampi K., Urhonen H.-R. & Virtanen M., *Psychotherapy Research* 35(1):110–111, DOI 10.1080/10503307.2024.2301972 (January 2024). Of **1,430** reviews screened, **only 30% mentioned negative outcomes at all**; only **57 original RCTs** monitored negative effects; of those, **12 (21%) reported more negative effects in the therapy arm, statistically significant in 3**. **Balance the corpus initially omitted:** the authors write that in all three cases *"the number of cases with negative effects was small, and the observed differences… could therefore also be due to chance"*, and their overall finding is that *"**psychotherapy appeared not to increase the risk of negative effects when compared to the control group**"*. Their thesis is a *measurement* failure, not a demonstrated harm signal. Citing this as evidence that CBT harms people misreads it.
- **The 2025 field statement.** O'Neil A., Rossell S.L. & Berk M., *World Psychiatry* 24(2):194–195, DOI 10.1002/wps.21304 (2025-05-15). Real-world survey of **224** psychotherapy recipients: **unpleasant memories 57.8%, unpleasant feelings 30.3%, perceived lack of understanding by the therapist 18.4%**. Clinical-trial literature by contrast: *"**5% of participants experienced an (unspecified) adverse event, 2.5% a serious adverse event… and 4% attempted suicide**"*; deterioration *"estimated to occur in 5%"*. And: *"**only 11% of authors of psychotherapy trials used harm assessment instruments**"* despite nine validated instruments existing. The structural cause, verbatim: *"contrary to trials of pharmacotherapies — for which adverse events are required to be monitored… **psychotherapy trials are not subject to these regulations, which encourages under-reporting**."*

> **Note the ~10x discrepancy** between the survey figure (57.8% report unpleasant memories) and the trial figure (5% adverse events). Either the constructs differ entirely or trial-based harm estimates are floor estimates. This is unresolved, and it is the honest answer to "is CBT safe?" — *we do not know, because the field has not measured it to pharmacological standards.*

### 5.10 Durability

**Current best answer.** Cuijpers P., Miguel C., Kaya Aglio E., van Straten A., Karyotaki E. & Harrer M., "Long-term effects of psychotherapies for depression: an advanced meta-analysis", *World Psychiatry* 25(2):287–294, DOI 10.1002/wps.70065 (published **2026-05-15**). 191 RCTs, 209 comparisons, **33,691 participants**, 534 follow-up measurements, restricted-cubic-spline modelling: SMD rises to **0.55 at 12 weeks**, declines to **0.39 at ~1 year**, then **remains largely stable**; statistically significant out to **8.3 years**. RR for response peaks at **1.68 at 3 months**, decays to **1.14 at 5 years**; significant to **4.5 years**. **Only 52 of 534 (9.7%) follow-up measurements occurred more than 12 months after randomisation** — the 8-year claim rests on very thin data, which the authors flag. **Behavioural activation had poorer long-term outcomes than CBT**; no other between-therapy difference; the authors note this is correlational. `?` A third-party clinical summary reports 40.3% high RoB / 18.3% low RoB and long-term response ~35% vs ~24% — not in the abstract, unverified.

Corroborating: Cuijpers 2023 CBT > pharmacotherapy at 6–12 months **g = 0.34 (0.09–0.58)**, *"although the number of trials was small"*; Cuijpers 2021 NMA — only **nine** studies reported outcomes beyond 18 months; Papola 2023 GAD — at 3–12 months **only CBT** remained superior to TAU; Berendsen 2024 psychosis — *"At longer follow-up, evidence became weak or non-significant."* And *Frontiers in Psychiatry* 15:1415905, DOI 10.3389/fpsyt.2024.1415905 (2024-11-27) — only **19 RCTs, 1,154 participants** met the criterion of ≥12 months after *termination*; psychotherapy beat pharmacotherapy alone on relapse (**RR 0.58, 0.38–0.89, p = .023**).

`?` **Relapse rates after CBT for depression are widely quoted at 25–31% vs 50–78% in controls. No primary source for those specific figures could be located.**

### 5.11 The effectiveness gap and the treatment-prevalence paradox

**Routine care, England, most recent full year.** NHS England Digital, *NHS Talking Therapies for anxiety and depression, Annual reports, 2024-25*, published 2025-09-25 (page last edited 2026-06-01), https://digital.nhs.uk/data-and-information/publications/statistical/nhs-talking-therapies-for-anxiety-and-depression-annual-reports/2024-25: **1.81 million referrals** (−0.9%); **1.21 million accessed services** (−3.8%); **91.6% accessed within 6 weeks**; **670,419 referrals completed a course**; **50.5% moved to recovery** (+0.4pp); **mean 8.4 sessions**. All re-verified verbatim 2026-07-28.

⚠ **A funnel illustration, not an NHS statistic.** 670,419 completers against 1.81m referrals is roughly 37%, and the 50.5% recovery rate is computed on completers, so recovery as a fraction of people referred is on the order of **19%**. NHS England states on the same page that *"these numbers reflect activity in the year and are not all based on the same group of referrals"* — referrals made and courses completed within one financial year are overlapping but different populations. **Do not quote 37% or 19% as NHS statistics.**

**The paradox.** Ormel J., Hollon S.D., Kessler R.C., Cuijpers P. & Monroe S.M., "More treatment but no less depression: The treatment-prevalence paradox", *Clinical Psychology Review* 91:102111, DOI 10.1016/j.cpr.2021.102111 (online 2021-12-11). Verbatim conclusion: *"there is little evidence that incidence or prevalence have increased as a result of error or fact… and **strong evidence that (a) the published literature overestimates short- and long-term treatment efficacy, (b) treatments are considerably less effective as deployed in 'real world' settings, and (c) treatment impact differs substantially for chronic-recurrent cases relative to non-recurrent cases**. Collectively, these a-c explanations likely account for most of the TPP. Lastly, little research exists on iatrogenic effects of current treatments, but further exploration is critical."* Point prevalence of depression in Western countries since 1980 has not fallen and may have risen slightly. **This is the most important external-validity check on the entire CBT literature: forty years of scaling an "effective" treatment produced no population-level signal.**

For scale: Cuijpers 2024 *Am Psychol* reports that modelling studies show psychological treatment *and* antidepressants together *"can only reduce the disease burden of depression at the population level with approximately **one third**, even in optimal conditions of 100% uptake"* (Andrews et al. 2004).

### 5.12 For whom does CBT *not* work — what is actually established

**Established:**
1. **Youth depression** (see §3.16). Cuijpers 2023 *Am Psychol*: significantly smaller effects than in unselected adults, **b = −0.27 (SE 0.10, p = .007)**.
2. **Borderline personality disorder.** Absolute response 0.24 (0.15–0.36) and **the RR versus control was not significant** — the only one of eight disorders where it wasn't. Confound: BPD trials are 100% care-as-usual-controlled.
3. **Negative symptoms of schizophrenia.** Weak (−0.13) or non-significant (0.12) at end of treatment; nothing at follow-up.
4. **Country / region is the largest unexplained moderator.** Cuijpers 2023 *Am Psychol*: East Asia b = 0.28 (p = .005) and "other countries" b = 0.61 (p < .001) vs North America. **A 0.61 SD swing by geography is larger than the whole treatment effect in low-RoB trials**, and is dismissed in one line as "almost certainly methodological" — see [gaps](#11-known-gaps-and-unresolved-questions).
5. **Recruitment source is a trial-design artefact masquerading as a clinical fact.** Cristea 2017 found "recruitment from clinical samples" a consistent *negative* predictor; Cuijpers 2023 *Am Psychol* found trials diagnosing by clinical interview had **larger** effects (b = 0.17, p = .002). The two point in opposite directions.
6. `?` **Comorbid substance use problems** — smaller effects, cited by Cuijpers 2023 *Am Psychol*; the primary source was not read.

**Established as NOT a contraindication — both were previously believed to be:**
7. **Childhood trauma / maltreatment.** Kuzminskaite E. et al. (Childhood Trauma Meta-Analysis Study Group), *Lancet Psychiatry* 9(11):860–873, DOI 10.1016/S2215-0366(22)00227-9 (November 2022, correction August 2023). 29 trials, up to 6,830 participants, **62% reporting childhood trauma**: patients with childhood trauma were **more severe at baseline (g = 0.202, 0.145–0.258)** but **benefited equally** — treatment effect difference between groups **g = 0.016 (−0.094 to 0.125)**; dropout RR 1.063 (0.945–1.195). Verbatim: *"Evidence-based psychotherapy and pharmacotherapy should be offered to patients with major depressive disorder **regardless of childhood trauma status**."* Caveat: **21 of 29 studies (72%) had moderate-to-high RoB**.
8. **Comorbid personality disorder.** *Cognitive Therapy and Research*, DOI 10.1007/s10608-021-10203-x (2021-01-20), 11 studies (N = 769): PD patients ended CBT with marginally higher depression **g = 0.26 (0.10–0.43), p = .002** — **but not significant in controlled trials (p = .075), not in low-RoB studies (p = .107), and not in studies adjusting for intake severity (p = .827)**. Verbatim: *"**Excluding these patients from evidence-based care for depression is unjustified**, and adequately lengthy CBT should be routinely offered."* PD cases improved particularly with **16–20 sessions**.

**Not established:** baseline severity as a moderator (findings go both directions), and virtually all "personalisation" claims. Cuijpers 2024 *Am Psychol*, verbatim: *"**we have very little evidence supporting such personalization**"* and *"Because all therapies have comparable effects and we do not know who benefits from which treatment, **there is currently no reason to choose one therapy over the other**."*

### 5.13 Researcher allegiance

Meta-meta-analytic estimate: mean researcher-allegiance-to-outcome association **r ≈ 0.262**, individual meta-analyses up to 0.85 (Munder T. et al., *Clinical Psychology Review*, DOI 10.1016/j.cpr.2013.02.002, 2013). `?` Figures from the indexed abstract only. Recent work is more equivocal: Zimmerman & Marcus, *Clin Psychol Psychother*, DOI 10.1002/cpp.2974 (2024) — 19 pairs of studies with **opposing** allegiances found *"evidence of a likely allegiance bias in a substantial number of treatment comparison studies"*; while a 2024 meta-analysis of cognitive processing therapy for PTSD (DOI 10.1080/13284207.2024.2347643) found allegiance *"typically small to negligible"* with **no** association across 17 studies with active comparators. `?` Both 2024 papers read at abstract level only. Practical implication carried by all of them: **review studies from multiple independent teams before concluding one therapy is superior.**

---

## 6. Delivery: digital, remote, low-intensity

### 6.1 The guided/unguided binary has been retired by the people who built it

**The current strongest source.** Tong L., Panagiotopoulou O.M., Radio M., Miguel C., Harrer M., Andersson G., Riper H., Cuijpers P. & Karyotaki E., *"Impact of support levels on effectiveness and drop-out of internet-based interventions for depression: network meta-analysis"*, **British Journal of Psychiatry, First View pp. 1–9, published online 22 May 2026**, DOI **10.1192/bjp.2026.10653**, PMID 42169099, Open Access CC BY (https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/the-british-journal-of-psychiatry/article/impact-of-support-levels-on-effectiveness-and-dropout-of-internetbased-interventions-for-depression-network-metaanalysis/42D9FF81DB5EB215A112BD827A1693FB). **141 RCTs, 169 pairwise comparisons, n = 32,197**; searches to **1 January 2025**; preregistered at https://osf.io/amw4r; PRISMA 2020; Metapsy/Cochrane RoB 2; CINeMA + ROB-MEN. Inclusion is *stand-alone asynchronous* iCBT only.

Its Discussion retires the framing the field used for twenty years, verbatim: *"For two decades, research on internet-based interventions has primarily used a binary classification: guided versus self-guided formats. However, this binary classification **did not have a unified definition and no longer reflects the complexity of modern digital mental health treatments**."* Two charges, not one: the label was never operationalised consistently across studies, *and* it no longer maps onto what products do.

**The seven levels** (coded at the most intensive component offered): **(a) no support**; **(b) technical support only** (troubleshooting); **(c) program-generated automated support** (*"automated encouragement or personalised feedback generated by artificial intelligence algorithms"*); **(d) support on demand**; **(e) minimal coaching** (*"human support on encouragement and adherence only"*); **(f) full coaching** (*"brief and (semi)standardised feedback without in-depth discussion on therapeutic strategies"*); **(g) therapeutic support** (*"more structured and individualised clinical guidance, primarily delivered by licensed therapists"*).

**Effectiveness vs care-as-usual** (Hedges' g): therapeutic support **0.42 (0.30–0.55)** > full coaching **0.31 (0.20–0.43)** ≈ automated support **0.30 (0.12–0.48)** … > technical support only **0.15 (−0.02 to 0.33)**, non-significant. *(The league-table paragraph prints the technical-support contrast as 0.16 with the same CI; both figures appear in the published text — do not present one as *the* figure.)* All seven beat waiting list (g = 0.54–0.81) and other controls (0.22–0.48).

**The result that reverses the old reading:** *"**Most support formats showed comparable effectiveness, except that therapeutic support outperformed minimal coaching (g = 0.19 [0.03–0.35]) and technical support (g = 0.27 [0.08–0.45])**"* — and the abstract states the corollary explicitly: therapeutic support *"had similar effects to those with **full coaching…, automated support, on-demand support or no support**"*. So licensed-therapist guidance is statistically indistinguishable from *fully automated* and from *no* support, while **technical-support-only is the least effective format and is not superior to care-as-usual**.

**Acceptability inverts at the top and confirms at the bottom.** Drop-out RR vs CAU: **minimal coaching 1.13 (0.88–1.46)** — lowest, and the only format not significantly worse than controls; therapeutic support **1.20 (0.98–1.48)**; **technical support highest at 1.61 (1.21–2.15)**. Full coaching has *worse* retention than minimal coaching (RR 1.32 [1.02–1.72]) for no significant symptom gain over it.

**Pre-intervention human contact is the operative moderator.** With it (**104 studies, 121 comparisons**): *"**no significant differences in treatment effectiveness or acceptability were observed across support levels**"*. Without it (**38 studies, 48 comparisons**): *"**therapeutic support was significantly more effective than other formats (g = 0.32 [0.05–0.59] to g = 0.68 [0.35–1.02])**"*, and drop-out rises across the board (RR 1.13–3.10 without contact vs 1.04–1.82 with). "Contact" means *"pre-intervention symptom screening, clinical diagnostic interviews, assistance in study onboarding, or referrals from general practitioners, nurses or other health workers"*. Conclusions, verbatim: *"**Low-intensity supported iCBT can be as effective as therapist-guided iCBT when initial human contact is present. Evidence regarding the potential harms of no-human support is needed before implementation.**"*

**Three limits that must travel with every citation of this paper.** (i) **In the low-risk-of-bias subset (45 studies, 56 comparisons) the hierarchy disappears entirely** — *"all support levels showed comparable effectiveness… **automated support emerged as the most effective format (g = 0.66 [0.40–0.92])**"* — a *stronger* statement against a guidance premium than the headline. (ii) **Substantial heterogeneity (τ² = 0.05; I² = 68.1%)** and **significant global inconsistency (design-by-treatment Q = 110.41, d.f. = 32, p < 0.001)**, with **57 of 141 trials (40.4%) at high risk of bias**; the authors' first limitation is that findings *"should be interpreted cautiously owing to global inconsistency and a high risk of bias"*. (iii) **No interrater κ was computed for the support-level coding** — conceded in the paper — which is the single most consequential hole given that the entire contribution *is* the re-classification. Long-term results also scramble the ranking (58 trials: therapeutic support still first at g = 0.33, but **technical support rises to second at 0.23**, and no support has the *lowest* drop-out at RR 1.02).

**"No support works" is a statistical statement, not a deployment recommendation** — the authors' own brake: *"application of purely unguided interventions in clinical practice requires caution. This is particularly relevant for individuals with severe depression, comorbid conditions or elevated suicide risk…"*

`?` **Protocol-vs-paper deviations** (own comparison against https://api.osf.io/v2/registrations/amw4r/, no third party has published this): registration date 26 March 2025 per OSF vs "16 March 2025" per the paper; search dates disagree in both directions on both databases; heterogeneity estimator changed REML → DerSimonian–Laird undeclared; the primary analysis's g ≥ 2.0 outlier exclusion is **not** in the protocol, while two preregistered population-specific analyses and the preregistered prediction intervals are **not reported**. None overturns the finding; all belong in a careful citation. Full working: the deleted `raw/gap-1.md` — `git show 15e599a:reference/health/tcc/raw/gap-1.md` in the `me` repository (see [§12](#12-provenance)).

**The superseded baseline, and why it read the way it did.** Karyotaki E. et al., *JAMA Psychiatry* 2021;78(4):361–371, DOI 10.1001/jamapsychiatry.2020.4364, PMID 33471111 (https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jamapsychiatry/fullarticle/2774861), searched only to **1 January 2019**: **guided > unguided post-treatment, MD −0.8 PHQ-9 (95% CI −1.4 to −0.2)**; **no evidence of a difference at 6 or 12 months**; *"baseline depression was found to be the most important modifier"*, with a small guided–unguided difference at **PHQ-9 5–9** and guided better overall at **PHQ-9 > 9**. Its Methods pre-collapsed the distinctions that matter: *"**Unguided iCBT was defined as CBT delivered via the internet where automated and technical support was permitted, but not support related to the therapeutic content.**"* — so "unguided" pooled the 2026 taxonomy's *no support*, *technical support only* and *automated support*, whose CAU-referenced effects run from a non-significant 0.15 to a significant 0.30. **−0.8 PHQ-9 is a weighted average across a boundary that does not carve the field at its joints.** Its guided arms were also mostly **not** licensed clinicians. `?` The provider counts (6 paraprofessionals / 14 students / 5 licensed psychologists) come from a third-party rendering; the three *categories* are verified.
**Two corrections exist on that paper and neither is substantive:** the header reads *"Last corrected on December 27, 2023"*; both are bibliographic (an abstract typo, 2021-02-17; an author-surname display error, 2023-12-27 — *Error in Display of Author Name*, *JAMA Psychiatry* 2024;81(3):320, DOI 10.1001/jamapsychiatry.2023.5122). **Trap:** the free PMC copy (PMC8027916) shows only the 2021 correction.
**What is *not* superseded is the severity moderator** — Karyotaki 2021 is IPD; Tong 2026 is aggregate data and cannot estimate patient-level effect modification. **No IPD NMA across the seven support levels exists.** Treat the PHQ-9 5–9 vs >9 split as legacy patient-level evidence *pending replication*.

**Independent corroboration of the contact finding, from a group with no Amsterdam overlap.** Krieger T., Bur O.T., Weber L., Wolf M., Berger T., Watzke B. & Munder T., *Internet Interventions* 2023;32:100617, DOI 10.1016/j.invent.2023.100617, preregistered https://osf.io/4mzyd. 56 trials, 13,335 patients: *"**Degree of contact was a robust predictor of outcome and adherence in all pre-registered and exploratory analyses**"*; but in pairwise contrasts **only** interventions with contact *both before and during* (SMD 0.573 [0.437–0.709]) significantly beat those with no contact (SMD 0.224 [0.090–0.340]).

> **Operative statement to use now:** *support is not binary; a brief human contact at intake matters more than continuous guidance during treatment; the one configuration to avoid is technical-support-only; therapist-delivered support ranks first but its margin is significant against only two of six alternatives and vanishes in the least-biased trials; and encouragement-only coaching is the retention optimum.* NICE NG222 already encodes this shape, on other grounds: its **guided self-help is defined as materials plus "Support from a trained practitioner", usually 6–8 structured sessions** — the guideline never recommends *unguided* digital CBT.

### 6.2 Guided iCBT vs face-to-face CBT: equivalence, replicated outside trials

- **Meta-analytic.** Hedman-Lagerlöf E., Carlbring P., Svärdman F., Riper H., Cuijpers P. & Andersson G., *World Psychiatry* 2023 (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37159350/), PubMed searched to 2022-09-13; 31 trials, 16 conditions: **pooled g = 0.02 (95% CI −0.09 to 0.14)**, quality "acceptable" on Cochrane RoB v1. Restricted to *therapist-guided* iCBT against a *full-length* face-to-face programme, adults, English-language only.
- **Routine-care replication.** Rosenström T. et al., *Lancet Psychiatry*, PubMed record 2025 (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39954684/). Helsinki University Hospital registries linked to Finnish social/health registers; from 32,343 registered therapies, **392 face-to-face CBT + 5,467 iCBT**, analysis sample **5,834**; targeted maximum-likelihood estimation. **ATE: PHQ-9 fell 0.745 points more (95% CI 0.156–1.334) in guided iCBT than in face-to-face CBT** — i.e. iCBT was, if anything, slightly *better*, with dropout penalised by design. Non-randomised; the iCBT cohort excluded psychotic/neurological/personality disorders, chronic or bipolar depression and under-16s; the face-to-face arm is small and geographically restricted.
- **Acceptability.** Linardon J. et al., *Cognitive Behaviour Therapy* 2025, DOI 10.1080/16506073.2025.2542364. **Thirty** head-to-head trials: **non-initiation 8.7% (5.0–14.5) iCBT vs 11.9% (8.3–16.8) face-to-face, RR 0.58, significant**; **dropout 16.3% vs 12.0% — RR non-significant**; post-treatment and follow-up attrition non-significant. iCBT is *easier to start* and no harder to finish, in trial conditions.
- **Children and young people — an order of magnitude thinner.** Bevilacqua L. et al., *Clinical Child Psychology and Psychiatry* 2024, DOI 10.1177/13591045241259070: 1,849 citations screened → **only 10 eligible studies**; non-inferiority with low heterogeneity, **d = 0.06 (anxiety), d = 0.12 (depression)** — but **each pooled estimate rests on only 7 of the 10 studies**, and the authors' conclusion is hedged: technology-assisted CBT *"**may be** a valid alternative"*.
- **Videoconference-specific RCT evidence is much thinner than the "teletherapy = in person" slogan implies.** The largest disorder-specific non-inferiority trial is Bouchard S. et al., *J Clin Med* 2022;11(19):5924, DOI 10.3390/jcm11195924, ISRCTN 12662027 — 148 adults with GAD, 15 weekly manualised sessions, 10% non-inferiority margin. **Published correction: *J Clin Med* 2025;14(1):226, DOI 10.3390/jcm14010226 (2025-01-03)** — corrects Tables 1, 2, 5, 6 and the Section 3.4 hierarchical regression (originally F(7,112) = 10.05, R² = 0.62; corrected F(7,113) = 7.33, R² = 0.32). **The non-inferiority result is not among the corrections** and the authors state the scientific conclusions are unaffected. Generalisability limit the trial's own methods impose: 148 **White**, French-fluent adults aged 18–75, five Quebec metropolitan areas, recruited 2014–2016. **For most disorders other than GAD, the videoconference "equivalence" claim is being borrowed from the iCBT literature, which is a different intervention.**

### 6.3 Apps: small effects that shrink further once publication bias is corrected

- **The headline meta-analysis.** Linardon J., Torous J., Firth J., Cuijpers P., Messer M. & Fuller-Tyszkiewicz M., *World Psychiatry* 2024;23(1):139–149 (2024-01-12). **176 RCTs.** Depression: N = 33,567, **g = 0.28, NNT = 11.5**; generalised anxiety: N = 22,394, **g = 0.26, NNT = 12.4**. Moderators: **effects for depression were significantly larger when apps incorporated CBT features or chatbot technology**. Other conditions (all flagged high-RoB, small-n): social anxiety g = 0.52, OCD g = 0.51, PTSD g = 0.12, acrophobia g = 0.90, **panic g = −0.12 (non-significant, negative)**.
- **The correction that matters.** *"Efficacy of standalone smartphone apps for mental health: an updated systematic review and meta-analysis"*, **The Lancet Digital Health, November 2025**, DOI 10.1016/S2589-7500(25)00105-0, PROSPERO CRD42022310762. **72 RCTs, 21,702 participants**, searched to 2023-11-10, standalone apps vs **inactive** controls. Post-treatment: depression g = 0.45 (0.30–0.60), anxiety 0.35 (0.22–0.48), sleep 0.71 (0.51–0.92), PTSD 0.15 (0.02–0.28), eating disorders 0.50, BDD 0.86. **Null pooled effects for smoking, self-injury, suicidal ideation and alcohol misuse.** Critically: *"Publication bias was found for RCTs targeting depression and anxiety… adjustments reduced the effect sizes for **depression from 0·45 to 0·18** (0·02 to 0·34) and **anxiety from 0·35 to 0·18** (0·03 to 0·32), with no change for sleep problems."* I² 74–81% **for the depression, anxiety and sleep pools specifically** (PTSD is 28.7%). Three author statements, quoted accurately: standalone apps *"might be offered for symptoms of depression, anxiety, and sleep problems, **if no evidence-based first-line intervention is available**"*; the negative recommendation covers **five** conditions — *"smoking, alcohol misuse, self-injury, suicidal ideation, and schizophrenia… should not be recommended for use until further evidence is developed"*; and the line that most constrains this whole section — *"**first-line treatment via face-to-face psychotherapy, psychiatric treatment, or internet-based interventions should be prioritised**"*. Separately and more weakly, on harm: *"It must be determined whether the use of apps for alcohol misuse and suicidal behaviours… could create harm"* — an open question, not a finding of harm.
