**Exposure augmentation: d-cycloserine has effectively failed.** Smits J.A.J., Kredlow M.A., Monfils M.-H. & Otto M.W., *Annual Review of Clinical Psychology* 2026;22:263–287, DOI 10.1146/annurev-clinpsy-061324-071811 (Review in Advance 2025-12-05), open access. Verbatim: *"concentrating mostly on d-cycloserine, the agent with the most extensive evidence base across levels of analysis. **Despite early promise, results across preclinical, human laboratory, and clinical trials have been mixed.**"* Their programme is a mechanistic restart, not a dosing question. The quantitative baseline, all 2017 and all negative-to-marginal: McGuire J.F. et al., *J Clin Psychiatry* 2017;78(2):196–206 — 20 RCTs, 957 participants, **overall efficacy g = 0.15, non-significant**; *"d-Cycloserine does not universally enhance treatment outcomes"*; Rodrigues H. et al., *PLoS ONE* 2017;12(3):e0173660 — *"the overall effect of DCS is very small and almost indistinguishable"*. **No 2024–2026 guideline or authority recommends DCS as routine exposure augmentation.**

**VR-delivered exposure: solid meta-analytic effects, thin regulatory footprint.** Chang Y.C., Arifin H., Hung T.M. et al., *"Innovative virtual reality exposure therapy for anxiety and post-traumatic stress disorder: a meta-analysis of randomised controlled trials"*, ***J Glob Health* 2026;16:04090**, DOI 10.7189/jogh.16.04090, published **2026-03-27** (https://jogh.org/2026/jogh-16-04090/). **26 RCTs, 1,649 participants**, seven databases, OSF-registered, **restricted to clinically diagnosed samples** (its premise being that earlier VRET meta-analyses were inflated by including non-diagnosed participants): **phobia g = −0.98 (−1.37 to −0.60)**; approach behaviour **g = 0.62 (0.11–1.12)**; anxiety **g = −0.61 (−0.90 to −0.33)**; PTSD **g = −0.51 (−0.72 to −0.30)**. Moderator: **shorter sessions (<60 minutes) produced larger effects**. **This is the resolution to the corpus's own open question about VR** — see [§10](#10-where-this-dossiers-own-sources-disagree).
**gameChange** (automated VR cognitive therapy for agoraphobic avoidance in psychosis) is the largest VR mental-health RCT: Freeman D. et al., *Lancet Psychiatry* 2022;9:375–388, N = 346, nine NHS trusts — agoraphobic avoidance adjusted mean difference **−0.47 (−0.88 to −0.06), Cohen's d = −0.18, p = 0.026**; distress **−4.33, d = −0.26** — **small effects overall**, larger in the severely avoidant subgroup. A **2025–2027 real-world waitlist RCT to inform NICE** is running (ISRCTN **79060696**, six NHS trusts, primary endpoint at 26 weeks). Regulatory status: **UKCA-marked** for NHS use while evidence is generated. `?` No US FDA clearance number could be confirmed.
⚠ **The only guideline-level VR positioning in the corpus is expired.** Germany's **S3-Leitlinie *Behandlung von Angststörungen*** (AWMF 051-028), Version 2.0, *Stand* **06.04.2021**, *gültig bis* **05.04.2026**: VR exposure **kann** be offered as an adjunct in social phobia and **soll nicht** be the sole measure; for specific phobia VR exposure **soll** be offered when in-vivo is unavailable or impossible. The AWMF detail page now states *"Stand: 06.04.2021 (**in Überarbeitung**), gültig bis 05.04.2026… **Seit > 5 Jahren nicht aktualisiert, Leitlinie wird aktuell überarbeitet, Anmeldeverfahren noch nicht abgeschlossen**"*. **As of 2026-07-28 the German anxiety guideline has formally expired and its revision is not yet registered.** Its still-quoted core recommendations: KVT **soll** be offered for panic/agoraphobia, GAD and social phobia (Ia*, A+); CBT-based internet interventions **kann** be offered as bridging or adjunct but **soll nicht** be the sole treatment.

**Wearables / just-in-time adaptive interventions: real but small, and the field admits immaturity.** von Lützow U., Neuendorf N.L. & Scherr S., *BMJ Mental Health* 2025;28(1):e301641, DOI 10.1136/bmjment-2025-301641 (2025-09-01) — covering **JITAIs *and* ecological momentary interventions**, studies 2018 → May 2025, **K = 23, N = 2,563**: **between-group g = 0.15 (0.05–0.26, p = 0.003)**; interventions **<6 weeks** had the strongest pooled follow-up effect **g = 0.71 (0.18–1.24)**; the authors note JITAIs/EMIs are *"probably more effective in symptom reduction than in increasing positive PWB"* and that *"control group selection… significantly influences effect sizes"*. `?` **Four sub-effect figures and their GRADE certainty ratings previously attached to this paper are not in the abstract, which reports PRISMA + Cochrane Risk of Bias and no GRADE at all — do not cite GRADE certainty for this paper.** Structurally: van Genugten C. et al., *Front Digit Health* 2025;7:1460167 (2025-01-28) — of **1,419 records screened, only 9 papers describing 5 distinct JITAIs** met criteria; **only 1 of 5 used passive EMA with machine learning; none used micro-randomised trials**; JITAIs for mental health *"are still in their early stages"*. The framework's own authors (Nahum-Shani I. & Murphy S.A., *Annual Review of Psychology* 2026, 2026-01-20) name three unsolved problems and conclude *"much research effort is needed to build more sophisticated and effective JITAIs."*

**Guideline updates in progress — and the structural finding.**
- **APA**: the 2019 depression CPG stated on its own face that it *"is set to expire in approximately 2024"*; **in 2025 the APA Board appointed a multidisciplinary panel to update it**, with **no expected publication date**. ⚠ **Citation trap:** the landing page (https://www.apa.org/depression-guideline) is *"Last updated: June 2025; Date created: 2019"* and prints its suggested citation as **"American Psychological Association. (2025)"** for what is substantively the February-2019 document. A downstream reference reading "APA (2025), depression guideline" would be **wrong about the evidence vintage by six years** — the same class of trap the corpus already documents for the PTSD guideline, where `apa.org/ptsd-guideline/ptsd.pdf` now serves the **2025** text.
- **Germany**: S3 anxiety guideline expired 2026-04-05, revision unregistered (above); NVL *Unipolare Depression* is at **Version 3.2 (July 2023)**, adopted 29 September 2022 and *"gültig… spätestens bis 29. September 2027"*, with v3.1 (January 2023) and v3.2 (July 2023) both **editorial/regulatory patches, not evidence updates**, and the whole thing an explicit **Teilpublikation** with older patients, culture/migration, gender, peripartum and comorbidity chapters still outstanding.
- **France**: 2017/2019, with no revision announced.
- **VA/DoD**: MDD CPG remains **Version 4.0 (2022)**, *"based on evidence reviewed through January 2021"*; PTSD/ASD CPG remains **2023** (evidence to 2022-05-04). The CPG index page (last updated 2026-01-13) flags some guidelines as **"Update of CPG in Progress"**. `?` **Which guidelines that marker applies to was not established.**
- **NICE NG222 is the exception — it is *reviewed-and-current*, not merely old.** NICE ran a **January 2026 exceptional surveillance review** (report published **2026-01-30**) and decided **not to update**. Trigger: an application from the Royal College of Psychiatrists concerning **intravenous racemic ketamine**; NICE concluded it *"is not currently possible to state that intravenous ketamine is equivalent to or superior than ECT"*, with only ~6 RCTs comparing the two, reused repeatedly across meta-analyses, and none of the 8 registered RCTs UK-based.

> **The structural finding of the frontier section:** **the 2026 evidence and the in-force guidance are separated by three to seven years everywhere checked.** No national guideline published in 2024–2026 has yet incorporated the d-cycloserine null, the single-session-intervention decay finding, the SSRI-combination subgroup null, or generative-AI CBT.

---

## 9. Superseded, abandoned or contested — do not rely on this

**This is the section to read before quoting anything about TCC.** It consolidates every superseded, abandoned or contested item found across the corpus, with what replaced it and when. Items are grouped by how likely you are to be shown the stale thing.

### A. Theory and mechanism

| Claim / practice | Status | Superseded or contested by | Date |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Habituation as the mechanism of exposure** — "stay in the situation until fear declines"; within-session fear reduction as the index of learning (Foa & Kozak 1986 emotional processing theory) | **SUPERSEDED as theory.** Not defended in the current literature | Craske et al. 2008 *BRAT* 46:5–27 (*"neither the degree by which fear reduces nor the ending fear level predict therapeutic outcome"*); Craske et al. 2014 *BRAT* 58:10–23; **Craske et al. 2022 *BRAT* 152:104069** (current canonical statement: inhibitory *retrieval*, OptEx Nexus) | 2008 → 2022 |
| **…but the superseding claim's own clinical corollary** — that inhibitory-learning-optimised exposure produces *better outcomes* | **NOT ESTABLISHED / CONTESTED.** The theory won; the trial did not | Treanor et al. 2025 *JCCP* 93(10):705–717 (no differences on principal outcomes, ps > .11, **from Craske's own lab**); Kooistra et al. 2025 *EJPT* (null, N = 60); Nadel et al. 2025 *J Clin Psychol* (null, N = 1,968); Kühne et al. 2024 *Front Psychol* (*"IL-focused therapy was hardly distinguishable from CBT as implemented in practice"*) | 2024–2025 |
| **Craske et al. 2014's prediction that cognitive restructuring *before* exposure is deleterious** | **NOT CONFIRMED** | Buchholz et al. 2022 *J Behav Cogn Ther* 32(1):13–24: *"no significant differences between CR-EXP and EXP-CR"* | 2022 |
| **Cognitive restructuring as the necessary / uniquely-active ingredient of CBT** | **ABANDONED as a specific-mechanism claim** — with load-bearing caveats | Furukawa et al. 2021 *Lancet Psychiatry* 8(6):500–511 (CR iMD 0.30, 95% CrI −0.87 to 1.41; *"cognitive restructuring would probably not be chosen"* — **but internet CBT only, and the authors call this estimate "relatively imprecise"**); Ciharova et al. 2021 *JCCP* 89(6):563–574 (with their rider *"More research is needed to derive conclusions about the performance of CR"*); Domhardt et al. 2025 *BRAT* 189:104735 (no indirect effect for dysfunctional attitudes). **Partially rebutted** by Smith et al. 2026 *Psychother Psychosom* (within-person cognition→symptom path, standardised long-run effect 0.36 — but the reverse path 0.21 is statistically indistinguishable) | 2021 → 2026 |
| **Angelakis et al. 2022 (*BJP* 221(2):459–467) as support for "cognitive restructuring is dispensable"** | **DEMOTED.** It varies how many components are *added* to a BA/CR core rather than removing cognition, and **beyond 6 months only the complex (−0.73) and ultra-complex (−1.09) tiers retained significant benefit** — the opposite direction. ⚠ A widely circulated "verbatim" headline — *"Core, complex and ultra-complex CBT protocols were equally effective… at post-treatment"* — **does not exist in the paper** | Paper's own abstract, verified 2026-07-28 | corrected 2026-07-28 |
| **CBT's cognitive effects are specific to CBT** | **SUPERSEDED** | Cristea et al. 2015 *CPR* 42:62–71: no difference vs other psychotherapies (g = 0.17, p = .31) or pharmacotherapy (g = 0.04) | 2015 |
| **Beck's depression-specific cognitive triad (1967/1979) as the current statement of cognitive theory** | **SUPERSEDED by the author himself** | Beck & Haigh 2014, *Annu Rev Clin Psychol* 10:1–24 — the **generic cognitive model**: transdiagnostic, adds dual information processing, schema energization, attentional focus, theory of modes, continuity of adaptive/maladaptive function | 2014 |
| **"Second-wave CBT = replacing irrational thoughts with rational thoughts"** | **A MISREADING**, not a superseded position | Carona 2023 *BJPsych Advances* 29(3):213–217: *"cognitive therapy is all about genuine guided discovery and not merely about changing thoughts and beliefs"* | 2023 |
| **"Three waves" as an accurate historiography** | **CONTESTED / NOT DEFENDED by anyone currently writing on it** — still used to scope literature searches | Linden 2022 *World Psychiatry* 21(2):316–318 (twelve waves; *"has become a trade mark"*); Carona 2023; Hayes & Hofmann 2021 *World Psychiatry* 20(3):363–375 | 2021–2023 |
| **Wolpe's reciprocal-inhibition account of systematic desensitization; the psychoanalytic symptom-substitution prediction** | **HISTORICAL / FALSIFIED respectively** | McNally 2020 *Annu Rev Clin Psychol*: symptom substitution *"failed to materialize"*. Systematic desensitization survives as a technique; its 1958 mechanism does not | — |
| **Symptom-network *centrality* as a treatment target** | **NOT DEFENSIBLE on current methods** | Dablander & Hinne 2019 *Sci Rep* 9:6846; Hallquist et al. *MBR* 56(2):199–223; Bringmann et al. *J Abnorm Psychol* 128(8):892–903; Neal & Neal *Psychol Methods* 28(1):179–188; empirically Gillett et al. 2025 *Schizophrenia* 11:94 (no hysteresis–relapse association); Schumacher et al. 2024 *World Psychiatry* (**pairwise associations outperformed centrality**) | 2019 → 2024 |
| **Individualised functional-analysis-based treatment outperforms manualised protocols** | **NOT ESTABLISHED (2021 → still 2026)** | Emmelkamp 2021 *World Psychiatry* 20(3):381–382: *"there is no robust evidence for a superior effectiveness of treatment based on functional analysis compared with manualized evidence-based treatments."* Unchanged: the first PBT RCT (NCT06517589) has not reported | 2021 → 2026 |
| **Öst's 2008/2014 methodological appraisal of third-wave RCTs as a *current* appraisal** | **HISTORICAL** (pre-2015) | Superseded in scope by Heinitz/Schefft et al. 2023 (27% of trials low RoB; GRADE *"largely low"*) and Williams et al. 2023 | 2023 |
| **"An inhibitory learning-based turn in exposure therapy", *Nat Rev Psychol* 3:726 (2024), cited as a Craske review** | **MISATTRIBUTION** | It is a one-page **Research Highlight by Nora Skjerdingstad**, DOI 10.1038/s44159-024-00370-5, 2024-10-02. Cite Craske et al. 2022 *BRAT* 152:104069 for the model | 2024 |

### B. Techniques

| Claim / practice | Status | Superseded or contested by | Date |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Thought stopping** | **ABANDONED.** Not named in NG222, CG113, CG159, NG116, CG31 or the ISTSS lists | Abramowitz, Tolin & Street 2001 *CPR* 21(5):683–703 (*"a small to moderate rebound effect of thought suppression"*); Wang, Hagger & Chatzisarantis 2020 *Perspect Psychol Sci*, k = 31 (*"Rebound effects were observed regardless of cognitive load"*). Replaced by exposure + response prevention, defusion/acceptance, or behavioural experiments testing the suppression rule. `?` **No guideline or professional body was found that names "thought stopping" and formally deprecates it** — the abandonment is documented by mechanism studies and by absence, not by a "do not offer" recommendation | 2001 → 2020 |
| **Single-session psychological debriefing / CISD / catharsis-abreaction** | **ABANDONED, with evidence of harm** | Cochrane CD000560 (Rose, Bisson, Churchill & Wessely, 15 trials): no prevention of PTSD; **at one year one trial reported significantly increased PTSD risk, OR 2.51 (1.24–5.09)**; *"Compulsory debriefing of victims of trauma should cease."* **NICE NG116 rec 1.6.5 [2018]: "Do not offer psychologically-focused debriefing for the prevention or treatment of PTSD"** — **re-surveilled and left standing on 8 April 2025**. **Phoenix Australia / NHMRC is stronger still — a recommendation *against*:** *"Individual psychological debriefing — **Weak Recommendation Against**"*, plus parallel recommendations against group debriefing and against individual debriefing in children | 2002 → 2025 |
| **…and the "give more sessions instead" escape route** | **ALSO CLOSED** | Roberts, Kitchiner, Kenardy, Robertson, Lewis & Bisson, Cochrane CD006869.pub3, published 2019-08-08, 27 studies / 3,963 participants: *"**multiple session interventions aimed at everyone exposed to traumatic events cannot be recommended**"*; PTSD severity at 3–6 months SMD −0.10 (−0.22 to 0.02); dropout vs active control RR 1.61 (1.11–2.34) | 2019 |
| **…contested at the margins, occupational settings only** | **HEDGE, not a reversal** | *"Revisiting the debriefing debate…"*, *Front Psychol* 2023;14:1248924 (2023-12-21): *"The overall synthesis **did not find consistent evidence** that psychological debriefing helps"* — but argues Rose et al.'s conclusion *"may have been premature"*. **Use only as a hedge on the strength of the harm claim, never as grounds to soften "do not offer."** `?` Author list not verified | 2023 |
| **"Safety behaviours must always be eliminated"** | **REVISED — and the revision is itself contested** | Rachman, Radomsky & Shafran 2008 proposed *"judicious use"*; Blakey & Abramowitz 2016 *CPR* 49:1–15 countered from inhibitory learning (*"Therapists are advised to **fade** safety behaviors as soon as patients are willing"*); **the RCT settled the acceptability claim negatively** — Blakey et al. 2019 *BRAT* 112:28–35, N = 60: large effects in both arms, **no significant group differences in outcome OR in acceptability/tolerability**. Latest synthesis: Goodson, Patel, Zech, Sala & Cougle, *CPR* 2026;124:102703, PMID 41558151 (2026-01-16) — reduction vs control d = 0.52 (behaviours) / 0.43 (symptoms); increase conditions d = 2.96 / 0.65; **evidence of publication bias found**; conclusion hedged. ⚠ **A sentence widely quoted as this paper's finding is in its *introduction*, summarising prior work** | 2016 → 2026 |
| **Current defensible statement on safety behaviours** | **Fade, don't ban at session one; eliminate before termination; do not treat their presence as automatically fatal to an exposure trial.** Note the tension: Craske 2022 lists "removal of safety signals" as a **fundamental** strategy | — | — |
| **Relaxation as a routine CBT package component** | **CONTESTED — and one corpus file overstated this as "superseded, evidence of harm".** See [§10](#10-where-this-dossiers-own-sources-disagree) | *Against:* Furukawa et al. 2021 *Lancet Psychiatry* (relaxation iMD **+1.20**, 0.17–2.27, i.e. worse depression, in **internet** CBT); Pompoli et al. 2018 (muscle relaxation significantly lower efficacy in panic); Furukawa Y. et al. 2024 *JAMA Psychiatry* (relaxation iOR **0.81, 0.64–1.02**, *"potentially counterproductive"* — **CI touches 1.00**, and 21% of participants were on hypnotics). *For:* **AASM 2021 still conditionally recommends relaxation therapy as a single-component therapy for insomnia**, unrevisited in the April 2026 combination CPG; **NICE CG113 rec 1.2.18 [2011] keeps applied relaxation as a co-equal high-intensity option for GAD**, at the identical 12–15-session dose, and the **NHS Talking Therapies manual v7.1 still lists it as a step-3 GAD option** | open |
| **Relaxation training for PTSD specifically** | **NOT RECOMMENDED — and this does not rest on ISTSS alone** | ISTSS 2018/2019 "Insufficient Evidence to Recommend"; **APA 2025** lists Relaxation Training in its insufficient-evidence set; **VA/DoD 2023 Recommendation 27** *"insufficient evidence to recommend for or against… relaxation training"*, confidence *"very low"*; **Phoenix Australia**: *"For adults with PTSD there was insufficient evidence to make a recommendation on Relaxation training"* | 2018 → 2025 |
| **Applied relaxation at parity with CBT for GAD (CG113 1.2.18/1.2.20)** | **CONTESTED by post-2011 evidence, unchanged in the guideline** | Papola et al. *JAMA Psychiatry* 2023-10-18: relaxation vs TAU −0.59 at **LOW** certainty, and **loses superiority when high-RoB studies are excluded (−0.47, −1.18 to 0.23)**; **only CBT** remains effective at 3–12 months (−0.60, −0.99 to −0.21). **NICE has not revisited 1.2.18 and no update is scoped** | 2023 → 2026 |
| **Virtual-reality exposure as broadly inferior** | **DOMAIN-LIMITED, and the question is now answered** | Pompoli 2018 found VR exposure *"associated with significantly lower efficacy"* **as a component of panic CBT specifically**. **Chang et al., *J Glob Health* 2026;16:04090 (2026-03-27), 26 RCTs / 1,649 participants restricted to clinically diagnosed samples**, finds phobia g = −0.98, anxiety −0.61, PTSD −0.51 — i.e. VRET works outside panic. **The panic-specific negative component finding should be treated as domain-limited** | 2018 → 2026 |
| **Stress Inoculation Training as Strong-for in PTSD** | **DOWNGRADED** | VA/DoD 2017 → 2023: **SIT and IPT Strong/Weak for → "Neither for nor against"**; also **BEP and NET Strong → Neither**; **Ehlers' CT and Written Exposure Therapy Strong → Weak for**; **fluoxetine Strong → Neither**. Cause stated by VA/DoD: individual rather than class-level GRADE assessment | 2023 |
| **Psychoeducation as a stand-alone treatment for PTSD** | **NOT RECOMMENDED** — but read the label correctly | ISTSS 2018/2019; **APA 2025**; **VA/DoD 2023 Rec 10**; **Phoenix Australia**: *"insufficient evidence to make a recommendation on psychoeducation as a stand-alone intervention. **However, psychoeducation should be routinely provided as part of usual care**"* — the cleanest published statement of the "component, not treatment" position. **APA's own gloss: *"'insufficient evidence' indicates that there was not enough high-quality data… Insufficient evidence for a given treatment does not mean that there is evidence that the treatment is ineffective."*** | 2018 → 2025 |
| **"Group CBT is downgraded"** as a general statement | **OVERSTATED — true only in specific disorders** | *Downgrade evidence:* Wang et al. 2026 *BJPsych* (individual, remote and family-involved significantly more effective than group for OCD, SMD −0.38 to −0.60); NICE NG116 rationale (*"Group trauma-focused CBT was not seen to be clinically or cost effective"*); VA/DoD 2023 Recs 12–13 (insufficient evidence for any manualised group therapy for PTSD); NICE CG159 rec 1.3.3 (*"Do not routinely offer group CBT in preference to individual CBT"* for social anxiety). **Counter-evidence in the same corpus:** NICE **NG222 lists group CBT and group behavioural activation as first-line options for less severe depression, ranked *above* individual CBT** on cost-effectiveness (guided self-help → group CBT → group BA → individual CBT), and group CBT is one of two named relapse-prevention psychological options | mixed |
| **"Unguided self-help is downgraded"** as a flat rule | **OVERSTATED** | The OCD NMA's own authors: unguided self-help *"has lower but still moderate effects… and it **holds important potential** for assisting a larger number of individuals with OCD who face barriers to accessing treatments"*. And the 2026 support-levels NMA (§6.1) abandons the binary altogether, finding **no-support formats comparable to therapist-guided ones when pre-intervention human contact exists** | 2026 |

### C. Effect sizes, methods and evidence practices

| Claim / practice | Status | Superseded or contested by | Date |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Waitlist as an acceptable control condition** | **ABANDONED in depression research** — explicit instruction: *"WL control conditions should be avoided"* | Furukawa et al. 2014 *Acta Psychiatr Scand* 130:181–192 (the nocebo hypothesis); Zhou et al. 2015 *World Psychiatry* (waitlist significantly **inferior to no-treatment**, SMD −0.46, in youth depression); Skapinakis et al. 2016 (waitlist the **only** "intervention" *increasing* YBOCS vs drug placebo, **+5.62**); Reid/Laws et al. 2022 (ERP effect ~6× larger vs waitlist); Cuijpers et al. 2024 *EPS* 33:e56 (WL g = 0.95 vs CAU g = 0.63, p < 0.001) | 2014 → 2024 |
| **…but anxiety-disorder research has not caught up** | **CONTESTED / LAGGING** | 70–96% of anxiety-disorder trials still used waitlist as of the January-2023 search cut-off. **Consequence: any pre-2015 headline effect size for CBT in an anxiety disorder, OCD, PTSD or an eating disorder is inflated and should be relabelled "vs no treatment", not "efficacy"** | 2024 |
| **Pre-post (within-group) effect sizes as a meta-analytic outcome** — the method that generated the decline claim | **ABANDONED.** Produces I² ≈ 90% and is formally advised against | Cristea et al. 2017; Cuijpers, Weitz, Cristea & Twisk, *Epidemiol Psychiatr Sci* 26:364–368 | 2017 |
| **"CBT's effects for depression are falling linearly since 1977"** (Johnsen & Friborg 2015) | **NOT SUSTAINED as a linear trend** — but the original authors have **not conceded** | Ljótsson et al. 2017 (flat after 1995); Cristea et al. 2017 (*"most likely a spurious finding"*); Cuijpers 2023 *World Psychiatry* and 2023 *Am Psychol* (no association with publication year). Friborg & Johnsen 2017 maintain the BDI trend is quadratic not flat | 2017 → 2023 |
| **"CBT is superior to other psychotherapies for depression"** | **EFFECTIVELY ABANDONED** | Cuijpers 2023: g = 0.06 (95% CI 0–0.12), non-significant in most sensitivity analyses; **0.02 (−0.05 to 0.09), I² = 0%** in low-RoB trials; no difference in NMA | 2021–2023 |
| **"CBT is superior to antidepressants for acute depression"** | **NOT SUPPORTED.** The surviving claim is narrower and about *durability* | Cuijpers 2023: short-term g = 0.08 (−0.07 to 0.24); low-RoB −0.06; publication-bias corrected −0.05. At 6–12 months g = 0.34 (0.09–0.58) | 2023 |
| **"Common factors explain 30–70% of outcome variance, specific techniques 5–15%"** (Lambert & Barley 2001; Wampold 2001; Imel & Wampold 2008) | **STATISTICALLY INVALIDATED AS STATED** | de Felice et al., *New Ideas in Psychology* 54:50–55: the partition assumes independence; common and specific factors are demonstrably correlated, so ANOVA-style apportionment is *"statistically unsound and conceptually distorted"* | 2019 |
| **Dodo bird verdict as universally settled** | **MOSTLY UPHELD BUT NEWLY CRACKED** | Boxell et al. 2025 *J Contemp Psychother* (equivalence on post-treatment symptoms across 5 modalities and 18 disorders, but **rate of change is moderated by modality**); Cuijpers et al. 2026 *World Psychiatry* (**behavioural activation had poorer long-term outcomes than CBT**) | 2025–2026 |
| **"Preregistration shrinks effect sizes and positive-result rates"** | **CONTESTED** | van den Akker et al. 2023, 193 matched pairs: **no** significant difference in positive results, effect sizes or statistical errors | 2023 |
| **Pooling depression outcome instruments as interchangeable** | **CONTESTED / being replaced by core outcome sets** | Sprenger et al. *J Affect Disord* 397:120873: 17 instruments across 492 trials; heterogeneity has *increased* over 50 years; CES-D and GDS systematically shift effect estimates | 2025/2026 |
| **"Childhood maltreatment makes depressed patients treatment-resistant"** (Nanni, Uher & Danese 2012; Nemeroff et al. 2003) | **SUPERSEDED** | Kuzminskaite et al. *Lancet Psychiatry* 9(11):860–873: trauma patients start sicker but gain equally; treat *"regardless of childhood trauma status"* | 2022/2023 |
| **"Comorbid personality disorder means personality pathology must be treated first"** | **SUPERSEDED** | *Cognitive Therapy and Research*, DOI 10.1007/s10608-021-10203-x: the apparent effect is intake severity, not treatment resistance; exclusion *"unjustified"* | 2021 |
| **"New-wave therapies improve on classical CBT"** | **ABANDONED** | Cuijpers, *Am Psychol* 79(9):1407–1417: *"none of the new therapies that have been introduced over the past 50 years are more effective than previous treatments"* | 2024 |
| **"ACT outperforms CBT"** (Ruiz 2012) | **SUPERSEDED. Do not cite Ruiz 2012 as current.** `?` Its effect size is reported inconsistently — Öst (2014) gives 0.37, this dossier's corpus previously gave 0.40; use "≈0.37–0.40, favouring ACT" and quote no precise figure | Öst 2014 (ACT vs cognitive/behavioural treatments ES 0.16, n.s.); Gloster et al. 2020 (*"excluding CBT"*); Fang & Ding 2023 (CBT *better* on anxiety); Heinitz/Schefft et al. 2023 | 2014 → 2023 |
| **"MCT is roughly twice as good as CBT"** (Normann et al. 2014, g = 0.97) | **SUPERSEDED.** The estimate has shrunk monotonically as trials improved | 0.97 (2014) → 0.37–0.69 (Normann & Morina 2018) → **0.43 (Andersson et al. 2024)**; and Heinitz/Schefft 2023 show the NMA advantage was partly driven by two trials with non-improving control groups | 2024 |
| **"Predominantly group schema therapy is effective"** | **CONTESTED** | Zhang et al. 2023 found group ST (g = 0.859) far better than individual ST (0.163), while the much larger Arntz 2022 RCT (n = 495, 15 sites) found predominantly group ST **not significantly better than TAU** (d = 0.30, p = .32) | 2022 vs 2023 |
| **Mindfulness "has no side effects"** | **ABANDONED** | Farias et al. 2020 (pooled AEs 8.3%); Goldberg et al. 2022 (32–50% in population survey terms); the 8-week-programme harms study (58% negatively-valenced, 37% functionally-impairing, 6–14% lasting); Matko & Van Dam 2026 (25–87% report AEs, 3–37% functional impairment). **Adverse-event reporting is absent in >80% of mindfulness RCTs — historical safety claims rest on non-measurement** | 2020 → 2026 |
| **"CBT is a quasi-neuroleptic" / substantial effect on core psychotic symptoms** | **CONTESTED AND DOWNGRADED** — yet CBTp remains guideline-recommended | Jauhar et al. 2014 (masked-assessor effect −0.15 overall, **−0.08 non-significant on positive symptoms**), with four published rebuttals and replies; Berendsen et al. 2024 (only 42.9% of comparisons significant, **no convincing or highly suggestive evidence**, weak-to-null at follow-up) | 2014 / 2024 |
| **"CBT (as a branded package) is the best psychotherapy for OCD"** | **CONTESTED** | Skapinakis 2016 found CBT *smaller* than behavioural therapy or cognitive therapy in the full network and not different from psychological placebo: *"our analysis does not support the view that the three types of psychotherapy have different effects."* ⚠ **Balance:** the same authors' Interpretation favours **combined** psychotherapy + pharmacotherapy in severe OCD | 2016 |
| **Clomipramine as superior to SSRIs in OCD** | **ABANDONED** | Skapinakis 2016: −1.23 (−3.41 to 0.94), not significant. *"This finding contrasts with previous direct analyses"* — the earlier signal was driven by old completer-only trials | 2016 |
| **Long-term durability of CBT for youth depression** | **NOT SUPPORTED** | Zhou et al. 2015: at long-term follow-up *"CBT was not superior to any control condition"* (IPT was). Weersing 2024 records the field-level downgrade | 2015 / 2024 |
| **Non-pharmacological treatment of adult ADHD as a symptom-reducing intervention** | **WEAKENED** | Ostinelli et al. *Lancet Psychiatry* 12(1):32–43: CBT beat placebo **only on clinician-reported** scales; *"the effects of non-pharmacological strategies were inconsistent across different raters"* | 2024/2025 |
| **Adjunctive CBT as a distinctive relapse-prevention ingredient in bipolar disorder** | **CONTESTED** | Chatterton 2017 (CBT's relapse RR not significant vs TAU; *"no intervention was associated with a significant reduction in depression symptom scale scores"*) vs Miklowitz 2021 (CBT OR 0.52) — an unresolved disagreement between two NMAs of largely the same trials; plus the 2024 SEKT-vs-FEST RCT (49% vs 46% relapse; **worse in BD-II**) | 2017 → 2024 |
| **Transdiagnostic CBT as superior to disorder-specific CBT** | **NOT SUPPORTED** | Schaeuffele et al. 2024 *Nat Hum Behav* (depression g = 0.09, anxiety g = 0.09; no advantage at 24 months), plus **significantly higher dropout in transdiagnostic arms** | 2024–2025 |
| **Single-session interventions as durable stand-alone treatments** | **NOT SUPPORTED** | *Nature Human Behaviour* megastudy, N = 7,505: immediate gains (d ≤ 0.37) mostly gone at 4 weeks (only 2 of 12 significant, d = 0.14–0.15), and **completing an SSI reduced confidence and interest in making changes (d = 0.05)** | 2026-03-02 |
| **d-cycloserine as a routine exposure augmenter** | **SUPERSEDED** | Smits, Kredlow, Monfils & Otto, *Annu Rev Clin Psychol* 2026;22:263–287 — *"Despite early promise, results across preclinical, human laboratory, and clinical trials have been mixed"*; baseline g = 0.15, n.s. (McGuire 2017) | 2026 |
| **Symptom-network centrality as the way to pick treatment targets** | **SUPERSEDED** | Schumacher et al. 2024 *World Psychiatry*; *Annu Rev Clin Psychol* 2026;22:23–48 (idiographic models *"prone to noise and spurious relations"*) | 2024 → 2026 |

### D. Guidelines and official documents

| Document | Status | Replaced by / current position | Date |
|---|---|---|---|
| **NICE CG90, *Depression in adults* (October 2009)** | **REPLACED** — and with it the whole **stepped-care** vocabulary and the four-band subthreshold/mild/moderate/severe taxonomy | **NG222** (29 June 2022), using **matched care** and **less severe / more severe** with a PHQ-9 cut of 16. Anything citing "NICE stepped care for depression" is on CG90 | 2022 |
| **NG222's own recommendation numbering from the 29 June 2022 PDF** | **SUPERSEDED.** The May 2024 "presentational" simplification removed the general-principles recommendations and shifted numbers | **Cite NG222 by text, not by number** | May 2024 |
| **NG222 rec 1.5.3 as published in June 2022** — *"…, unless that is the person's preference."* | **SUPERSEDED** | Live text since **October 2025**: *"Do not routinely offer antidepressant medication as first-line treatment for less severe depression. **Only offer it if that is the person's informed preference.**"* NICE calls it *"to clarify our advice"*. **The single substantive addition is the word *informed*, which raises the bar — do not describe it as a softening.** And **do not quote only the first sentence**: that overstates the prohibition. **The recommendation is still tagged [2022], so the year tag conceals the edit** | Oct 2025 |
| **NICE CG26, *Post-traumatic stress disorder* (March 2005)** | **REPLACED** by NG116 (5 December 2018) | Debriefing is now an explicit do-not-do | 2018 |
| **NICE CG22 (anxiety, 2004), ESUOM12, and CG123 (common mental health disorders, 2011)** | **REPLACED by CG113** — CG123's full guideline was formally **"stood down" in May 2024** | *But* the panic-disorder CBT recommendations inside CG113 are still tagged `[2004]` and have never been re-reviewed. **"CG113 last reviewed 7 May 2024" ≠ "CG113's CBT dosing is 2024 evidence"** | 2011 / 2024 |
| **NICE TA97 (February 2006), phobia section** | **REPLACED** by CG159 (May 2013); its functional successor for anxiety is now HTG676 | 2013 |
| **NICE CG82 (March 2009) and ESNM39** | **REPLACED** by CG178 (2014). And newly: **NICE reviewed and rejected metacognitive therapy for psychosis on 29 July 2025** | 2014 / 2025 |
| **NICE CG31 (OCD/BDD, November 2005)** | **LIVING ON BORROWED TIME.** NICE has confirmed "several areas for update" since **February 2019** | Replacement **GID-NG10435, expected 16 February 2027**; batch 1 consultation autumn 2026. Any 2026 statement of the form "NICE recommends X therapist-hours of ERP" should be labelled **2005 guidance under active revision** | 2027 |
| **NICE MTG70 (Sleepio) / HTE8 / HTE9 numbering** | **RENUMBERED** — content unchanged | **HTG624 / HTG675 / HTG676** under the NICE HealthTech programme. **HTE8 migrated to HTG675 in December 2025** (NICE Update-information page — the specific, verifiable date; two corpus files date the migration more vaguely as "2025–2026") | Dec 2025 |
| **NICE HTE8's original recommendation of three depression programmes** | **TWO REMOVED — for commercial withdrawal, not efficacy** | **Beating the Blues**: *"As of February 2024, this technology is no longer available to the NHS"* (removed from the **anxiety** guidance HTG676 — an earlier corpus draft attributed this to the depression guidance, which is wrong). **Deprexis**: *"no longer available in the UK"*, removed from HTG675 rec 1.1 in the 14 January 2025 update. **Only Space from Depression remains recommended** | 2024–2025 |
| **Deprexis and Minddistrict (GAD) as NHS Talking Therapies options** | **BOTH "NOT COMPLIANT"** with NHS England's DET assessment criteria | Deprexis for delivering self-help rather than guided self-help and containing substantial non-protocol content; Minddistrict for mixing models and lacking module-specific evidence | 2024 cohort |
| **WHO mhGAP 2010 and 2015 guidelines** | **SUPERSEDED by the 3rd edition, 20 November 2023** (ISBN 978-92-4-008427-8), **30 updated + 18 new recommendations alongside 90 pre-existing**, GRADE-based, including a **new anxiety (ANX) module** | Key content: **DEP3** *"Structured psychological interventions should be offered… namely behavioural activation therapy, brief psychodynamic therapy, cognitive behavioural therapy, interpersonal therapy, problem-solving therapy and third wave therapies. **Strong recommendation. Moderate certainty.**"* — **WHO deliberately does NOT privilege CBT.** **DEP4:** *"**Antidepressant medicine alone… should only be considered when psychological interventions are not available.** Conditional. Low certainty."* **ANX2 (new):** *"**Brief, structured psychological interventions based on principles of cognitive behavioural therapy should be offered for adults with GAD and/or panic disorder. Strong recommendation. Moderate certainty.**"* — the strongest CBT-specific WHO statement. **STR1 (PTSD, adults):** individual and group face-to-face trauma-focused CBT, digital/remote TF-CBT, EMDR and stress management — **CONDITIONAL, MODERATE certainty**. **STR2 (PTSD, children/adolescents): STRONG, moderate certainty.** Note the asymmetry is in *strength* only — certainty is moderate for both | 2023 |
| **…but the operational tool has NOT caught up** | **mhGAP Intervention Guide is still Version 2.0 (2016)** and WHO's own 2023 evidence pages still link to it. **Do not present mhGAP-IG 2.0 content as the current WHO recommendation set** | 2016 vs 2023 |
| **APA PTSD guideline 2017** | **SUPERSEDED by the 2025 edition** (approved by APA Council of Representatives 22 February 2025), built on **15 systematic reviews** rather than one | Concrete changes: 2017 **strongly** recommended four psychotherapies (CBT, CPT, **CT**, PE); 2025 strongly recommends three (**CPT, PE, trauma-focused CBT**), with **CT demoted to conditional**, joining EMDR and NET; **Brief Eclectic Psychotherapy moved from conditionally recommended to "insufficient evidence"**. **Citation trap: `apa.org/ptsd-guideline/ptsd.pdf` now serves the 2025 document**, so a reference reading "APA (2017), apa.org/ptsd-guideline/ptsd.pdf" is broken on both counts. Also: **do not cite the third-party mirrors** (gonetowar.com, naasca.org) that host the 2017 text | Feb 2025 |
| **APA 2025 as settled consensus on EMDR** | **CONTESTED ON PUBLICATION** | Its second-tier placement of EMDR is out of step with NICE (2018), ISTSS (2019/2020), Phoenix Australia (2022), VA/DoD (2023) and APS (2024). Peer-reviewed critiques argue it relied on reviews whose searches ended 2017–2019 — but note the leading one is in an **EMDR-specialist journal** and is not a neutral party | 2025–2026 |
| **APA depression guideline cited as "2019, update in progress"** | **CITATION TRAP** | The landing page is *"Last updated: June 2025; Date created: 2019"* and **prints its suggested citation with the year 2025** for what is substantively the February-2019 document. **"APA (2025), depression guideline" is wrong about the evidence vintage by six years** | verified 2026-07-28 |
| **VA/DoD PTSD CPG 2017** | **SUPERSEDED by Version 4.0 (2023)** | Explicit downgrades on the record (see §B above). Cause stated by VA/DoD: individual rather than class-level GRADE assessment. **Any citation of the 2017 grading is superseded** | 2023 |
| **ISTSS Prevention and Treatment Guidelines 2009 and 2012** | **SUPERSEDED** by the 2018 set (amended 19 March 2019) | **Never cite ISTSS 2009/2012.** Prefer the dated filename `ISTSS_PreventionTreatmentGuidelines_FNL-March-19-2019.pdf` | 2018/2019 |
| **The ISTSS 2018/2019 set as *current* evidence** | **LIVE BUT FORMALLY ON NOTICE — and eight years old at the evidence layer** | Its own words: *"The recommendations were generated with the information available to the ISTSS Guidelines Committee on 10 October 2018"*, and the underlying searches are narrower still — *"New systematic searches were undertaken by the Cochrane Collaboration for the period 1 January 2008 to **31 March 2018**"*. ISTSS now advertises *"**New ISTSS Guidelines Coming Soon**"* with **no date, quarter or year anywhere**. **Read "Insufficient Evidence to Recommend" correctly**: ISTSS's own definition is *"an absence of evidence of effectiveness **or ineffectiveness**"*, and **78 of 125 recommendations (62%)** fall in that bucket. **Recency arithmetic:** the documents date from November 2018 (amended March 2019) against a dossier date of 28 July 2026 — **that is seven years from the amendment and over eight from the RCT search cut-off.** Do not say "7 years old" without saying which anchor | verified 2026-07-28 |
| **INSERM *Psychothérapie : trois approches évaluées* (February 2004)** | **HISTORICAL — and both of the usual rhetorical uses are wrong** | Withdrawn from the ministry website in February 2005 by ministerial decision, **never scientifically retracted, never superseded by a newer INSERM expertise, evidence cut-off second half of 2003**. Contested in the peer-reviewed French literature as *"relativement daté, tant au plan méthodologique que dans ses conclusions"* | 2004/2005 |
| **HAS ALD n°23 guide (2012 version)** listing psychothérapie and psychologue | **SUPERSEDED by the January 2025 version**, which explicitly removed both (*"Suppression du psychologue"*, *"Suppression de la psychothérapie"*) because the APALD covers only acts reimbursed under droit commun | The document warns of itself: *"L'APALD n'est pas une recommandation de bonne pratique."* **Consequence: under ALD 23, psychotherapy delivered by a psychologue is not a listed reimbursable act** | Jan 2025 |
| **HAS 2007 ALD-23 guide médecin as a statement of current practice** | **HISTORICAL** — validated June 2007; predates the entire inhibitory-learning literature, the 2016–2019 safety-behaviour work, NG222/NG116/CG159, the UP equivalence trial and every meta-analysis in this dossier | **It remains the only French numeric dose anchor in existence** (12–25 sessions for GAD/panic, ≥25 for OCD), materially higher than English dose–response data and than the Mon soutien psy ceiling of 12 | 2007 |
| **HAS insomnia RBP (validated 20 December 2006)** | **THE OLDEST LIVE ITEM IN THIS DOSSIER.** Its behavioural content is limited to stimulus control and sleep restriction, described even then as *"peu répandues en France"*; it predates the term TCC-I and all digital-CBT-I evidence | `?` Whether HAS has formally flagged it obsolete or archived is **not confirmed either way** | 2006 |
| **Anaes 2002, *Prise en charge d'un épisode dépressif isolé de l'adulte en ambulatoire*** | **SUPERSEDED** by the HAS RBP of October 2017 — which is itself now nine years old | 2017 |
| **"HAS recommends TCC first-line for anxiety"** | **UNSOURCED for 2026 — do not assert it.** The only HAS-branded anxiety document is the **June 2007** ALD guide | — |
| **"Les recommandations HAS sur le TSPT"** | **DOES NOT EXIST.** The note de cadrage was validated 14 October 2020 with a November 2021 target, missed by ~5 years | The reference standards in France are NICE NG116, APA 2025 and VA/DoD 2023 | — |
| **HAS/ANESM 2012 autism position (psychoanalysis and packing "non consensuelles")** | **SUPERSEDED** | HAS RBP validated **8 January 2026**, online 12 February 2026: psychoanalysis and psychoanalytically-based interventions **"non recommandées"** (insufficient evidence tier); **packing** in the *proven absence of effect* tier | 2026 |
| **German S3-Leitlinie *Behandlung von Angststörungen* (2021) as "in force"** | **EXPIRED 5 April 2026**, revision **not yet registered** | AWMF: *"Stand: 06.04.2021 (in Überarbeitung), gültig bis 05.04.2026… Seit > 5 Jahren nicht aktualisiert… Anmeldeverfahren noch nicht abgeschlossen"*. **This is the only guideline-level VR positioning in the corpus, and it is past its Gültigkeitsdatum** | 2026-04-05 |
| **NVL *Unipolare Depression* — "there is a version newer than 3.2"** | **NO.** v3.1 = January 2023, v3.2 = July 2023, both **editorial/regulatory patches, not evidence updates**; no later version exists at 2026-07-28 | Adopted 29 September 2022, valid to 29 September 2027; explicitly a **Teilpublikation** with older patients, culture/migration, gender, peripartum and comorbidity chapters outstanding | 2023 |
| **"IAPT"** as the programme name | **RENAMED** to **NHS Talking Therapies for anxiety and depression** in 2023 (manual v7, updated March 2024). The underlying dataset is still versioned "IAPT 2.1" | 2023 |
| **The NHS "Internet Enabled Therapy sessions" monthly series as continuous** | **BROKEN BY A DEFINITION CHANGE** in January 2026 | Monthly counts drop from ~35–39k (Sep 2025–Feb 2026) to 16,681 (Mar), 14,974 (Apr), **15,610 (May 2026)**. Describe it as a **level shift followed by a plateau**, not a continuing decline | Jan 2026 |
| **NHS Talking Therapies manual v7.1's citation of "CG113, CG123"** | **STALE** — CG123 was stood down in May 2024, two months after the manual shipped. **No version 8 of the manual exists at 2026-07-28** | May 2024 |
| **"Structured problem solving" as a specialist-care option for panic** | **WITHDRAWN JULY 2019 AS AN ERROR** from CG113 rec 1.3.38 — **but still printed on NICE's live patient-facing page** (footer "last updated: 01 January 2011") | Anyone reading the CG113 information-for-the-public page is given an option NICE formally removed seven years ago | 2019 |
| **Contested and unresolved by any guideline: whether CBT is superior to other bona-fide psychotherapies for depression** | WHO 2023 DEP3 bundles CBT with BAT, DYN, IPT, PST and third-wave under one strong recommendation; NICE NG222 ranks CBT-family first but states every option "can be used as first-line"; CANMAT 2023 puts CBT, IPT and BA together first-line; HAS 2017 lists TCC alongside psychodynamic and systemic without ranking. **No current guideline recommends CBT to the exclusion of other structured psychotherapies for depression** | — |

### E. France — reimbursement rules that have changed

| What used to be true | What is true now | Superseding text & date |
|---|---|---|
| **Mon soutien psy: 8 sessions/year** | **12 sessions/year** (1 entretien + 11 séances) | Operational June 2024; codified by **décret n° 2025-424 du 13 mai 2025** amending R. 162-65 CSS (JORF 15 May 2025), in force 16 May 2025 |
| **€40 entretien / €30 séance de suivi** | **€50 for both** (codes EEP / APS / PSS) | **Arrêté du 24 juin 2024**, JO 28 June 2024 |
| **Mandatory *courrier d'adressage*** | **Direct access**; referral optional | **Arrêté du 24 juin 2024** (deleting the wording from the convention type); R. 162-64 2° deleted by **décret n° 2025-424**. ameli: *"Depuis le 15/06/2024"* |
| Referral valid 3 months; antidepressant window <3 months | Referral requirement removed; antidepressant window widened to **<6 months**, plus a dérogation (no psychiatrist opinion for hypnotics/benzos <1 month) | Arrêté du 24 juin 2024; **arrêté du 13 mai 2025** |
| Psychologists selected by **la ministre chargée de la santé** | Selected by the **médecin-conseil national auprès du directeur général de la CNAM**; supporting experts become *collaborateurs occasionnels du service public* | **Décret n° 2026-163 du 4 mars 2026**, JO n° 56 du 6 mars 2026, in force **7 March 2026** |
| **12-session cap applied to everyone** | **No cap** for minors with an ALD (3°/4° of L. 160-14) when sessions are prescribed within a protocole de soins | **LOI n° 2026-492 du 12 juin 2026**, art. 13, adding a III to L. 162-58 (JO n° 137, 13 June 2026) |
| **No tiers payant** except CSS/AME/ALD/AT-MP/pregnancy | **Tiers payant generalised on the AMO share from 1 October 2026** (patient advances at most €20) | **LOI n° 2025-1403 du 30 décembre 2025** (LFSS 2026); **application décret still pending at 2026-07-28** |
| **Unbounded remote delivery of follow-up sessions** | **CAPPED**: no exclusively-remote conventioned practice; **the *séance de bilan* must be in person**; **max 20% of a psychologist's annual conventioned activity may be remote** (a practitioner-level, not per-patient, ceiling) | **Arrêté du 17 juin 2026**, JORF n° 0142 du 19 juin 2026 — **but binding on each psychologist only from their own signature date, for one year** |
| The **arrêté du 2 mars 2022 convention type** | Annexe wholly replaced; adds RGPD clauses, 2-month resignation notice; **unsigned = unconventioned** | **Arrêté du 17 juin 2026** |
| Psychologists identified by an **ADELI number** | **RPPS number** (11 digits, national, permanent). **ADELI decommissioned** | Psychologues lot 3, **3 June 2024**; psychothérapeutes lot 4, **11 October 2024** |
| **Benzodiazepines / Z-drugs had a place in chronic insomnia** | **No hypnotic is recommended in chronic insomnia**; benzodiazepines limited to short-term occasional/transient insomnia, ≤28 days including weaning | HAS fiche *Quelle place pour les benzodiazépines dans l'insomnie ?*, **11 juillet 2024**; restated in the HAS CT avis on QUVIVIQ |
| **Digital TCC-I might be reimbursed in France** | **SOMNIO's *service attendu* judged insufficient** (LPPR route); **HelloBetter Insomnie refused** (PECAN route) | HAS CNEDiMTS, 15 July 2025 / published 25 September 2025; PECAN refusal online 2 August 2024 |
| `?` Scheme formerly branded **"MonPsy" / "Mon parcours psy"** | **"Mon soutien psy"** | `?` **No primary source for the rename was found** — the change is visible across ameli/info.gouv pages but could not be dated from an official text |
| **Psychiatrist consultation tariff figures published in 2024 and earlier** | Revalued end-2024 and again **1 January 2026**: CNP €52.00, CP €42.50, APY €67.50, MP (≤25 ans) +€18.00 | CNAM, *ce qui change en 2026*, 22 December 2025; ameli tariff table re-fetched 2026-07-28. **The €52/€55/€57 spread is bare-act vs bundle, not a supersession** |

### F. Digital / AI

| What is no longer current | What replaced it | Date |
|---|---|---|
| **Karyotaki et al., *JAMA Psychiatry* 2021 as "the single strongest source" on guided vs unguided iCBT** (searches stopped 1 January 2019) | Superseded as the current reference by Tong et al., *Br J Psychiatry*, **141 RCTs / 169 comparisons / n = 32,197**, searches to **1 January 2025**, from an author team including Karyotaki and Cuijpers themselves. Karyotaki 2021 is retained as the **superseded baseline** and as the best remaining **patient-level (IPD)** evidence on severity moderation | 2026-05-22 |
| **The binary "guided vs self-guided / unguided" classification itself**, field-standard for ~20 years | **RETIRED BY ITS OWN USERS**: it *"did not have a unified definition and no longer reflects the complexity of modern digital mental health treatments"*. Replaced by **seven support levels** | 2026-05-22 |
| **"Guidance buys a modest post-treatment advantage (MD −0.8 PHQ-9)"** as the operative practice statement | **REVERSED IN SCOPE.** Therapeutic support is statistically **similar to full coaching, automated support, on-demand support and no support**; it significantly outperforms only **minimal coaching (g = 0.19)** and **technical support (g = 0.27)**. **In the low-RoB subset all levels are comparable and automated support ranks first (g = 0.66)** | 2026-05-22 |
| **"Unguided iCBT" as a single coherent category** | Splits into formats with materially different performance: **technical support only g = 0.15, not superior to care-as-usual, worst retention (RR 1.61)** vs **automated support g = 0.30, significant**. Pooling them produced the old average | 2026 vs 2021 |
| **Baseline severity as *the* headline moderator** | Displaced in the aggregate literature by **pre-intervention human contact**. **Severity is not contradicted — it is simply not analysed** in the 2026 NMA (aggregate data cannot do patient-level moderation) | 2026 |
| **App effect sizes of g ≈ 0.45 (depression) / 0.35 (anxiety)** vs inactive control | Superseded **within the same paper** by publication-bias-adjusted estimates: **0.18 and 0.18** | Nov 2025 |
| **"Apps for alcohol misuse and suicidal behaviours should not be recommended"** as the scope of the negative recommendation | The authors' list is **five** conditions — smoking, alcohol misuse, self-injury, suicidal ideation and **schizophrenia**. The alcohol/suicide sentence is a separate, weaker statement about *unknown potential for harm* | Nov 2025 |
| **"Chatbot effect sizes are small but positive across depression, anxiety and stress"** | Narrowed and partly reversed: in 15 RCTs of **CBT-based NLP-enabled AI agents**, effects on **generalised anxiety, stress and positive affect were not significant after adjusting for publication bias** | 2026-06-12 |
| **Therabot's waitlist-controlled effect sizes as evidence of parity with therapy** | **CONTESTED IN THE SAME JOURNAL**: the waitlist is *"a likely nocebo"*; an attention-equivalent control, or a human-therapist comparator, was needed | 2025 |
| **Pear Therapeutics' reSET, reSET-O, Somryst** — the products that created the PDT category | Chapter 11 on **7 April 2023**; assets auctioned; **not accepting new prescriptions or refills** | 2023 onward |
| **FDA COVID-19 enforcement discretion for computerised behavioural therapy devices** | **EXPIRED.** The List-1 guidances *"will no longer be in effect… after November 7, 2023"* | 2023-11-07 |
| **Woebot** direct-to-consumer app | **SHUT DOWN 30 June 2025**; the founder cited FDA authorisation cost and the absence of an LLM pathway | 2025 |
| **Ash (Slingshot AI) availability in the UK** | **WITHDRAWN 23 January 2026** — *"there isn't a clear regulatory pathway for wellbeing products like ours"* | 2026 |
| **Unregulated consumer "AI therapy"** | **2025 wave:** Illinois PA 104-0054, Nevada AB406, Utah HB452. **2026 wave:** Maine LD 2082, Colorado HB 1195, Tennessee SB 1580, Rhode Island S 2195/H 7350, California SB 243. `?` ~14 chatbot-safety measures across 13 states in 2026 (advocacy tracker) | 2025 → 2026 |
| **The 2025 "three states ban AI therapy" snapshot** | **STALE.** Superseded by the 2026 enactments above; California SB 903, Pennsylvania HB 2100 and New Jersey A 5603 are **still not law** | 2026 |
| **Bouchard et al. 2022 GAD videoconference RCT as originally published** | **CORRECTED 3 January 2025** (Tables 1, 2, 5, 6 and the Section 3.4 regression: F(7,112)=10.05, R²=0.62 → F(7,113)=7.33, R²=0.32). **The non-inferiority result is not among the corrections** | 2025 |
| **Assumption that a "CBT app" delivers a NICE-faithful CBT protocol** | **CONTRADICTED IN PRACTICE**: NHS England required SilverCloud to strip non-GAD content and add missing GAD-protocol elements before Space from Anxiety could be judged compliant | 2025 |
| **CY2026 Medicare PFS as the frontier of DMHT payment policy** | Still operative for all of 2026, but the **CY2027 proposed rule (CMS-1848-P)** was issued 2026-07-14, comments to 2026-09-14, introducing a **"Software as a Medical Service"** category expressly distinct from prescription digital therapeutics | 2026-07-14 |
| **DiGA portfolio figures (44 permanent + 14 provisional, 11 delisted, €234m, €541/prescription)** | **A 16 JUNE 2025 SNAPSHOT, not current state.** The DiGA directory is a live register — re-read the BfArM DiGA-Verzeichnis before quoting any 2026 figure | Jun 2025 |
